Java — Interview Questions

Read notes

Answered the way you would say it in the room — first person, with an example every time. Tap a question to reveal the answer. Levels: Basic, Intermediate, Advanced.

Part 1 — Java Basics

1. What Java Is: JDK, JRE & JVM

BasicWhat's the difference between the JDK, the JRE and the JVM?

So they're three nested layers. The JVM is the engine that actually runs bytecode. The JRE is the JVM plus the standard class library, which is everything you need to run a Java program. The JDK is the JRE plus developer tools like javac, javap, jshell and jlink, which is what you need to build one.

ContainsWho needs it
JVMClass loader, bytecode interpreter, JIT compiler, garbage collectorNobody installs it alone; it lives inside the JRE/JDK
JREJVM + core libraries (java.lang, java.util, …) + the java launcherAnyone running Java apps
JDKJRE + javac, jar, javap, jdb, jshell, jlink, jcmdDevelopers and build servers

One thing I'd add for modern Java: since Java 11, Oracle and most vendors stopped shipping a separate JRE download. You install a JDK, and if you want a small runtime for production you use jlink to build a custom image with only the modules your app needs. That's what we did for our Docker images, and it cut the base image size a lot.

So in practice I just say "install JDK 21" and let jlink or a slim base image handle the runtime side.

Interviewers look for: the nesting (JDK contains JRE contains JVM) and bonus points for knowing the standalone JRE went away after Java 8.

BasicIs Java compiled or interpreted?

So honestly, both. Java source is compiled ahead of time by javac into bytecode, and then at runtime the JVM first interprets that bytecode and JIT-compiles the hot parts into native machine code.

Let me show you with a quick example. Here's a tiny program and the bytecode javac produced for it, which I got from javap -c:

public class Hello {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        int a = 2, b = 3;
        System.out.println("Sum: " + (a + b));
    }
}

// javap -c Hello  (main method)
0: iconst_2
1: istore_1
2: iconst_3
3: istore_2
4: getstatic     java/lang/System.out
7: iload_1
8: iload_2
9: iadd
10: invokedynamic makeConcatWithConstants
15: invokevirtual java/io/PrintStream.println
18: return

That bytecode is not x86 or ARM. It's instructions for a stack machine. When I run java Hello, the JVM starts by interpreting those instructions one by one. It also counts how often each method runs, and once a method gets hot, the JIT compiler (C1 first, then C2 for really hot code in HotSpot) turns it into optimized native code.

So the accurate answer is "compiled to bytecode, then interpreted and JIT-compiled at runtime." That's also why Java services get faster after warm-up.

BasicWhy is Java platform independent, but the JVM platform dependent?

So the .class file is the portable part. Bytecode is the same on every OS, so I compile once and the same JAR runs on Windows, Linux or macOS. The JVM is the part that has to talk to the real hardware and OS, so there's a different JVM build for each platform.

Let me put it as a picture:

Hello.java --javac--> Hello.class (bytecode, same everywhere)
                          |
        +-----------------+-----------------+
        |                 |                 |
  JVM for Windows   JVM for Linux     JVM for macOS/ARM
   (x86-64 code)    (x86-64 code)     (AArch64 code)

The JVM has to allocate memory, create native threads, open files and emit real machine instructions through the JIT. All of that is OS- and CPU-specific, so the JVM itself is a native program. It absorbs the platform differences so my bytecode doesn't have to.

That's what "write once, run anywhere" really means: the bytecode is portable because someone already ported the JVM. The caveat I'd mention is that your code can still break portability, for example hard-coding "\\" as a path separator or calling native libraries through JNI.

Interviewers look for: that you place portability in the bytecode, not in the JVM, and can name what makes the JVM native (memory, threads, I/O, JIT output).

IntermediateIs Java a 100% object-oriented language?

So no, not strictly. Java is strongly object-oriented, but it has eight primitive types that are not objects, and it has static members that belong to a class rather than to any object.

Let me show you with a quick example.

int count = 5;             // primitive: no methods, no null, lives directly in the variable
Integer boxed = count;     // object wrapper, created by autoboxing
// count.toString();       // does not compile: primitives have no methods
boxed.toString();          // fine

Math.max(3, 7);            // static method: no object involved at all

The primitives were a deliberate performance choice. An int[] of a million elements is one block of 4 MB. An Integer[] is a million references pointing at a million separate objects with headers, which is far more memory and much worse for the CPU cache.

Purely OO languages like Smalltalk make everything an object. Java chose speed for numbers. Project Valhalla is working on value classes that would narrow the gap, but that's not in Java 21.

So in an interview I'd say "object-oriented, but not pure, because of primitives and statics," and explain that it's a performance trade-off.

IntermediateWhat are LTS releases, and what are the big things in Java 8, 11, 17 and 21?

So Java ships a new version every six months, and every two years one of them is a Long-Term Support release that vendors patch for many years. Companies mostly run LTS versions: 8, 11, 17 and 21.

VersionHeadline features
8 (2014)Lambdas, Streams, Optional, default methods, java.time
11 (2018)var in lambdas (var itself came in 10), HttpClient, String.isBlank/strip/repeat/lines, run a single file with java File.java, no separate JRE
17 (2021)Records (16), sealed classes, text blocks (15), switch expressions (14), pattern matching for instanceof (16)
21 (2023)Virtual threads, pattern matching for switch, record patterns, sequenced collections

In my last project we moved a Spring service from 11 to 17 mainly for records and switch expressions, which removed a lot of boilerplate DTO code. Then 21 was attractive for virtual threads, because our service was mostly blocking I/O against other services.

So in practice I target the latest LTS my framework supports, which today is 21, and I read the release notes for removed APIs before upgrading.

Interviewers look for: knowing that var is Java 10, records 16, and that virtual threads are final in 21, not preview.

AdvancedIs there only one JVM? What's the difference between the JVM specification and HotSpot?

So "the JVM" is actually a specification: a document that defines the class file format, the bytecode instructions and how they must behave. HotSpot is Oracle's implementation of that spec, and it's the one inside OpenJDK builds from Oracle, Temurin, Corretto and most others.

There are other implementations that all run the same bytecode:

  • HotSpot: the default. Interpreter plus C1 and C2 JIT compilers, and GCs like G1 (default since 9), ZGC and Shenandoah.
  • Eclipse OpenJ9: from IBM, tuned for lower memory footprint and fast startup.
  • GraalVM: a JIT written in Java, plus native-image, which compiles ahead of time into a standalone binary that starts in milliseconds.

The spec leaves a lot open on purpose. It doesn't say how garbage collection works, how objects are laid out in memory, or when to JIT-compile. That's why two JVMs can run the same JAR with very different memory and startup profiles.

In my last team we tried GraalVM native image for a small CLI tool because JVM startup was about a second and native image got it to tens of milliseconds. The trade-off was that reflection-heavy libraries needed extra configuration.

So in practice I use HotSpot for long-running services, where the JIT wins, and consider native image only where startup time matters.

2. How a Java Program Runs

BasicWhy is main declared public static void? What happens if I remove static?

So each keyword exists because of how the JVM calls it. public because the launcher is outside the class and must be able to see it. static because no object exists yet when the program starts, so the JVM needs to call it on the class itself. void because the program's exit code isn't a return value; you use System.exit(code) for that. And String[] args carries the command-line arguments.

Let me show you what happens if I drop static:

public class NoStatic {
    public void main(String[] args) { System.out.println("hi"); }
}
$ javac NoStatic.java     // compiles fine
$ java NoStatic
Error: Main method is not static in class NoStatic, please define the main method as:
   public static void main(String[] args)

It compiles, because it's just an ordinary method called main. The error comes at launch, when the JVM looks for the entry point and can't find a static one.

One update for Java 21: JEP 445 added instance main methods and unnamed classes as a preview feature. With --enable-preview, the same file actually prints "hi". That's aimed at beginners, and it's still preview in 21, so in real code I always write the full public static void main(String[] args).

Interviewers look for: the reason behind each keyword, and that the failure is at runtime, not compile time.

BasicWalk me through what happens when you type java Hello.

So at a high level: the launcher starts a JVM, the class loader finds and loads Hello.class, the JVM verifies and links it, runs its static initializers, then calls main on the main thread, interpreting bytecode and JIT-compiling hot methods as it goes.

Let me break it into steps:

  1. Start the JVM: the java launcher creates the heap, starts GC and JIT threads.
  2. Load: the application class loader searches the classpath for Hello.class and reads the bytes. Classes like String come from the bootstrap loader.
  3. Link: verify the bytecode is safe (no stack overflows in the operand stack, no bad casts), prepare static fields with default values, and resolve symbolic references, often lazily.
  4. Initialize: run static field initializers and static {} blocks, once per class.
  5. Execute: call main(String[]). Each method call gets a stack frame; objects go on the heap.
  6. Exit: when all non-daemon threads finish, or someone calls System.exit, shutdown hooks run and the JVM exits.

A detail I like to mention: classes are loaded lazily. If main never touches a class, it's never loaded or initialized. That's why a missing dependency can blow up only when a particular code path runs, sometimes days after deployment.

So in practice when a service fails with NoClassDefFoundError in production, I think "that code path just ran for the first time."

IntermediateCan you overload main? Can a program run without a main method?

So yes, you can overload main like any other method, but the JVM only ever calls the public static void main(String[]) version. The others are just normal methods you call yourself.

Let me show you with a quick example.

public class MainOverload {
    public static void main(String[] args) {
        System.out.println("JVM called main(String[])");
        main(42);
    }
    public static void main(int x) {
        System.out.println("overloaded main(int): " + x);
    }
}
// JVM called main(String[])
// overloaded main(int): 42

Also valid: String... args instead of String[] args, final on the parameter, and any parameter name. Changing the return type or making it private is not.

Can it run without main? Back in Java 6 and earlier there was a trick of putting code in a static block and calling System.exit before the JVM looked for main. Since Java 7 the launcher checks for main first, so that no longer works. The real exceptions today are frameworks and containers: a servlet or a JUnit test has no main of its own, because the container's main loads and calls your class.

Interviewers look for: "overloading is allowed but only the String[] signature is the entry point," and knowing the static-block trick is dead since Java 7.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between ClassNotFoundException and NoClassDefFoundError?

So ClassNotFoundException is a checked exception you get when your code asks to load a class by name at runtime and it isn't there. NoClassDefFoundError is an error you get when a class was present at compile time, the JVM tries to load it implicitly, and it's missing or failed to initialize.

// ClassNotFoundException: dynamic loading by name
Class.forName("com.mysql.cj.jdbc.Driver");   // driver JAR not on classpath

// NoClassDefFoundError: a normal reference that compiled fine
Report r = new Report();   // Report.class was deleted from the deployed JAR,
                           // or Report's static initializer threw earlier
ClassNotFoundExceptionNoClassDefFoundError
TypeChecked exceptionError (a LinkageError)
TriggerClass.forName, loadClassImplicit load by new, method call, field access
Typical causeMissing JAR, typo in class nameClasspath differs between build and runtime, or static init failed

The sneaky case is the second cause. In one project a class had a static block that read a config file. The first access threw ExceptionInInitializerError, and every access after that threw NoClassDefFoundError, which sent us hunting for a missing JAR that wasn't missing. The real error was in the logs from the first failure.

So in practice when I see NoClassDefFoundError, I search the logs for an earlier ExceptionInInitializerError before I touch the classpath.

IntermediateWhat are class loaders, and what is parent delegation?

So class loaders are the objects that find class bytes and turn them into Class objects. Since Java 9 there are three built-in ones: bootstrap (core java.base classes, written in native code), platform (other JDK modules like java.sql), and application (your classpath). Parent delegation means a loader first asks its parent to load a class, and only tries itself if the parent can't.

System.out.println(String.class.getClassLoader());                        // null (bootstrap)
System.out.println(java.sql.Connection.class.getClassLoader().getName()); // platform
System.out.println(Loaders.class.getClassLoader().getName());             // app
System.out.println(Loaders.class.getClassLoader().getParent().getName()); // platform

The bootstrap loader shows up as null because it isn't a Java object.

Delegation gives two guarantees. Security: if I put my own java/lang/String.class on the classpath, the app loader asks bootstrap first, which loads the real one, so I can't replace core classes. Consistency: a class is identified by its name plus its loader, so delegation stops the same class being loaded twice and causing "cannot cast Foo to Foo" errors.

So in practice this matters when I work with app servers, plugin systems or hot reload in Spring DevTools, which deliberately use child-first loaders and can produce exactly that cast error.

Interviewers look for: "a class is unique by name plus loader," which explains the weird ClassCastException between identical names.

AdvancedPredict the output: static blocks, instance blocks and constructors with inheritance. 
static class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1. Parent static block"); }
    { System.out.println("3. Parent instance block"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("4. Parent constructor"); }
}
static class Child extends Parent {
    static { System.out.println("2. Child static block"); }
    { System.out.println("5. Child instance block"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("6. Child constructor"); }
}

new Child();
System.out.println("--- second object ---");
new Child();

So it prints the numbers in order, 1 to 6, and then for the second object only 3 to 6.

1. Parent static block
2. Child static block
3. Parent instance block
4. Parent constructor
5. Child instance block
6. Child constructor
--- second object ---
3. Parent instance block
4. Parent constructor
5. Child instance block
6. Child constructor

Here's what's going on. Before Child can be initialized, its superclass must be, so the static blocks run parent first, and only once per class, ever. Then for each new Child(), the Child constructor's first action is an implicit super(). The parent's instance initializers run, then the parent constructor body, and only then does control come back to run the child's instance initializers and constructor body. Instance blocks are effectively copied into the start of each constructor, right after the super() call.

So in practice this is why I never call an overridable method from a constructor: the parent constructor would run the child's override before the child's fields are initialized.

Interviewers look for: statics once and parent-first, and the implicit super() explaining the instance order.

AdvancedWhat is the JIT compiler, and why is a Java service slow right after startup?

So the JIT, just-in-time compiler, watches which methods run a lot and compiles them from bytecode into optimized native code while the program runs. Right after startup almost everything is still being interpreted, and classes are still being loaded, so the first requests are slower. That's the warm-up period.

HotSpot uses tiered compilation:

  • Tier 0: interpreter, collecting profile data like which branches are taken and which types show up at a call site.
  • C1: quick compile with light optimizations once a method is warm.
  • C2: heavy optimization for hot methods, using the profile: inlining, escape analysis to put objects on the stack, loop unrolling, and removing virtual call overhead when only one implementation has been seen.

Because C2 optimizes based on what it observed, it can also deoptimize. If it assumed only one subclass existed and a new one gets loaded, it throws that compiled code away and goes back to the interpreter.

In my last project, after each deploy the p99 latency spiked for about a minute. We fixed it by sending synthetic warm-up traffic before putting the pod behind the load balancer. Other options are CDS archives for faster class loading, or GraalVM native image when startup really matters.

So in practice I never benchmark Java with a single timed loop in main. I use JMH, which handles warm-up properly.

3. Types, Variables & Wrappers

BasicWhat are the primitive types, and how are they different from wrapper classes?

So Java has eight primitives that hold raw values, and each has a wrapper class that is a real object. Primitives are fast and can't be null. Wrappers can be null, can go into collections and generics, and have utility methods.

PrimitiveSizeDefaultWrapper
byte8 bits0Byte
short16 bits0Short
int32 bits0Integer
long64 bits0LLong
float32 bits0.0fFloat
double64 bits0.0Double
char16 bits (UTF-16 unit)'\u0000'Character
booleanJVM-dependentfalseBoolean

The key practical differences:

  • Generics only take objects, so it's List<Integer>, never List<int>.
  • Wrappers can be null, which is useful for "no value yet" in a database entity, but dangerous when unboxed.
  • == on wrappers compares references, not values.
  • Wrappers cost memory: an Integer is about 16 bytes on a 64-bit JVM versus 4 for an int.

So in practice I use primitives for local math and fields that always have a value, and wrappers only where I need null or generics.

Interviewers look for: the sizes, that defaults apply only to fields and array elements (local variables must be assigned before use), and a hint at the == trap.

BasicWhy does byte b = 10; b = b + 1; fail to compile, but b += 1 works?

So in Java, arithmetic on byte, short and char is always promoted to int first. b + 1 is an int, and assigning an int to a byte is a narrowing conversion that needs an explicit cast. Compound assignment like += has an implicit cast built in.

byte b = 10;
// b = b + 1;       // error: possible lossy conversion from int to byte
b = (byte) (b + 1); // fine with a cast
b += 1;             // fine: means b = (byte)(b + 1)
// b is 12

byte big = (byte) 200;
// big is -56: only the low 8 bits are kept

int max = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
max + 1;            // -2147483648: silent overflow, no exception

long wrong = 1_000_000 * 1_000_000;   // -727379968 (int overflow happens first)
long right = 1_000_000L * 1_000_000;  // 1000000000000

That last pair is the one I've actually seen in production. Someone computed a timeout in microseconds with two int literals, stored it in a long, and got a negative number. The multiplication happens in int before it's widened to long. Making one operand 1_000_000L fixes it.

So in practice when I multiply values that could be large I use long from the start, or Math.multiplyExact, which throws on overflow instead of wrapping silently.

IntermediatePredict the output: Integer a = 127, b = 127 and Integer c = 128, d = 128. 
Integer a = 127, b = 127;
Integer c = 128, d = 128;
System.out.println(a == b);          // true
System.out.println(c == d);          // false
System.out.println(c.equals(d));     // true
System.out.println(c.intValue() == d); // true

Long l = 127L;
System.out.println(l.equals(127));   // false
System.out.println(l.equals(127L));  // true

So a == b is true but c == d is false. Autoboxing calls Integer.valueOf, and valueOf returns cached objects for values from -128 to 127. So both 127s are literally the same object. 128 is outside the cache, so each boxing creates a new object, and == compares references.

When one side is a primitive, like c.intValue() == d, Java unboxes the other side and compares values, so that's true.

The Long one catches people too. 127 is an int, so it boxes to Integer, and Long.equals returns false for anything that isn't a Long. Same values, different types.

The cache exists for Byte, Short, Integer, Long and Character (0 to 127). The upper bound for Integer can be raised with -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax, which is exactly why you must never depend on it.

I've seen this bug in real code: comparing two Long IDs with == worked in every test, because test IDs were small, and failed in production once IDs passed 127.

So in practice I always compare wrappers with equals or Objects.equals, never ==.

Interviewers look for: the exact range -128 to 127, that it comes from valueOf, and the "works in tests, fails in prod" story.

IntermediateHow can autoboxing cause a NullPointerException?

So unboxing a wrapper is really a method call like intValue(). If the wrapper is null, that call throws an NPE, and the line often doesn't look like it dereferences anything.

Let me show you the two ways I've seen it happen.

Map<String, Integer> stock = new HashMap<>();
int qty = stock.get("apple");
// NullPointerException: Cannot invoke "java.lang.Integer.intValue()"
// because the return value of "java.util.Map.get(Object)" is null

Integer none = null;
Integer r = true ? none : 0;   // NPE, even though r is an Integer!

The first one is the classic: get returns null for a missing key, and assigning to an int unboxes it. The helpful message is from Java 14's helpful NullPointerExceptions, which names exactly what was null.

The ternary is sneakier. When one branch is Integer and the other is the primitive 0, the whole expression's type becomes int. So Java unboxes none to produce an int, crashes, and would have re-boxed it afterwards.

The fixes:

int qty = stock.getOrDefault("apple", 0);   // 0
Integer r = true ? none : Integer.valueOf(0); // both branches Integer, no unboxing

So in practice I use getOrDefault or merge for counters, keep entity fields as wrappers only when null has a meaning, and watch mixed wrapper/primitive ternaries in code review.

IntermediateWhat is var, and where can't you use it?

So var, added in Java 10, is local variable type inference. The compiler figures out the static type from the initializer. It's still fully statically typed; it just saves me writing the type twice.

var list = new ArrayList<String>();   // ArrayList<String>
list.add("x");
// list.add(42);                       // compile error: still typed

var map = new HashMap<String, List<Integer>>();   // saves a lot of noise

for (var entry : map.entrySet()) { }  // fine in loops
try (var in = new FileInputStream("a.txt")) { }  // fine in try-with-resources

// var x;                 // error: needs an initializer
// var n = null;          // error: can't infer from null
// var f = () -> 1;       // error: lambda needs a target type
// var arr = {1, 2};      // error: array initializer needs a type
// private var field;     // error: only for local variables

It's not allowed for fields, method parameters or return types. Java 11 did allow var for lambda parameters, mainly so you can put annotations on them.

One trap: var list = new ArrayList<>(); with the diamond infers ArrayList<Object>, which is probably not what you wanted.

So in practice I use var when the right-hand side makes the type obvious, like a constructor call, and I write the type out when it comes from a method whose return type a reader can't see.

Interviewers look for: that var is not dynamic typing, and that it's local-only.

AdvancedWhy is 0.1 + 0.2 not equal to 0.3, and how do you handle money in Java?

So double is binary floating point, and 0.1 has no exact binary representation, the same way 1/3 has none in decimal. The small errors add up, so the sum is slightly off.

System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2);          // 0.30000000000000004
System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);   // false

new BigDecimal(0.1);
// 0.1000000000000000055511151231257827021181583404541015625
BigDecimal.valueOf(0.1);                // 0.1
new BigDecimal("0.1").add(new BigDecimal("0.2"));   // 0.3

new BigDecimal("2.0").equals(new BigDecimal("2.00"));    // false (scale differs)
new BigDecimal("2.0").compareTo(new BigDecimal("2.00")); // 0

For money I use BigDecimal, or I store amounts as a long of the smallest unit, like cents. Three traps I watch for with BigDecimal:

  • new BigDecimal(0.1) captures the binary error. Use the String constructor or BigDecimal.valueOf.
  • equals checks scale too, so 2.0 and 2.00 aren't equal. Use compareTo for value comparison. That also means a HashSet<BigDecimal> can hold both.
  • divide throws ArithmeticException for non-terminating results like 1/3 unless you pass a scale and RoundingMode.

For comparing doubles in non-money code, like geometry, I compare with a tolerance: Math.abs(a - b) < 1e-9.

So in practice: double for measurements and science, BigDecimal or long cents for anything a customer will see on a bill.

4. Operators & Control Flow

BasicPredict the output of these char and String expressions. 
char c = 'a';
System.out.println(c + 1);          // 98
System.out.println((char) (c + 1)); // b
c++;
System.out.println(c);              // b
System.out.println("" + 'a' + 1);   // a1
System.out.println('a' + 1 + "");   // 98
System.out.println('a' + 'b');      // 195
System.out.println("x" + 1 + 2);    // x12
System.out.println(1 + 2 + "x");    // 3x
System.out.println('7' - '0');      // 7

So the rule is that a char is really a 16-bit unsigned number, and + is evaluated left to right. If neither operand is a String, it's numeric addition, and char gets promoted to int. As soon as one side is a String, it becomes concatenation from that point on.

So c + 1 is 97 + 1 = 98, printed as a number. Casting back to char gives b. c++ stays a char because increment has an implicit cast, like +=. 'a' + 'b' is 97 + 98 = 195, not "ab".

For the string ones, "" + 'a' is already a String, so the 1 is appended. But 'a' + 1 runs first as math, giving 98, and only then becomes a String.

'7' - '0' is the trick I use all the time in parsing problems to turn a digit character into its value. Same idea with ch - 'a' for a letter's index in a 26-slot count array.

Interviewers look for: left-to-right evaluation and "numeric until a String appears."

BasicWhat's the difference between & and &&?

So && is the logical AND that short-circuits: if the left side is false, the right side is never evaluated. & on booleans always evaluates both sides, and on integers it's bitwise AND. Same story for || and |.

String s = null;

if (s != null && s.length() > 3) { }   // safe: length() never called

if (s != null & s.length() > 3) { }    // NullPointerException:
                                        // both sides run

int flags = 0b1010;
int masked = flags & 0b0010;            // 2: bitwise on ints

Short-circuiting is what makes the null-guard idiom work. I rely on it constantly: list != null && !list.isEmpty(), or i < arr.length && arr[i] == target, where the order of the two conditions is what prevents the exception.

The only time I'd use the non-short-circuit version on booleans is when the right side has a side effect I always want to happen, and honestly I'd rewrite that as two statements because it's easy to misread.

So in practice I use && and || for conditions and keep &, |, ^ for bit manipulation.

IntermediateWhat does i = i++ leave in i? And what is j++ + ++j? 
int i = 5;
i = i++;
System.out.println(i);         // 5

int j = 1;
int k = j++ + ++j;
System.out.println(j + " " + k); // 3 4

So i stays 5. Here's exactly what happens: i++ saves the old value, 5, as the result of the expression, then increments i to 6. Then the assignment writes the saved 5 back into i, overwriting the 6. The increment happened, it just got thrown away.

For the second one, Java guarantees left-to-right evaluation of operands, unlike C where this is undefined behavior. j++ yields 1 and makes j 2. Then ++j makes j 3 and yields 3. So k is 1 + 3 = 4, and j ends at 3.

The one-line rule I use: postfix returns the old value, prefix returns the new value, and both change the variable.

So in practice I'd never write either of these in real code. If I see i = i++ in a review, it's a bug: the author meant i++. I keep increments on their own line or in the for-loop header.

Interviewers look for: that Java defines left-to-right evaluation, so the answer is deterministic, and that you'd flag it in code review.

IntermediateHow does switch on a String work? Any gotchas?

So switching on String has worked since Java 7. Under the hood the compiler calls hashCode() on the string, jumps on that number, and then calls equals() to confirm, because two different strings can share a hash.

Let me show you with a quick example, including the classic fall-through bug.

static String oldStyle(String day) {
    String type;
    switch (day) {
        case "SAT":
        case "SUN":
            type = "weekend";
            break;
        case "MON":
            type = "start";      // forgot break!
        default:
            type = "weekday";
    }
    return type;
}
oldStyle("MON");   // "weekday": fell through into default
oldStyle(null);    // NullPointerException

// javap -c shows: String.hashCode, lookupswitch, then String.equals per case
"FB".hashCode();   // 2236
"Ea".hashCode();   // 2236 (collision, which is why equals is still needed)

The gotchas I'd list:

  • Null: the hashCode() call throws NPE. I guard it or, in Java 21, add case null.
  • Fall-through: with colon-style cases, a missing break runs the next case.
  • Case-sensitive: "mon" won't match "MON", so I normalize first.

So in practice I use the arrow form from Java 14, which has no fall-through, and for a fixed set of values I prefer an enum so the compiler catches typos.

IntermediateWhat changed with switch in Java 14 and 21?

So Java 14 made switch an expression with arrow labels: it returns a value, has no fall-through, and multiple labels go on one line. Java 21 added pattern matching for switch: you can switch on type, deconstruct records, add when guards, and handle null directly.

// Java 14: switch expression
String type = switch (day) {
    case "SAT", "SUN" -> "weekend";
    case "MON" -> "start";
    default -> "weekday";
};

// Java 21: patterns, guards, null
static String describe(Object o) {
    return switch (o) {
        case null -> "null!";
        case Integer n when n > 100 -> "big int " + n;
        case Integer n -> "int " + n;
        case String s -> "string of length " + s.length();
        default -> "something else";
    };
}
// describe(500) -> "big int 500", describe(5) -> "int 5"
// describe("hey") -> "string of length 3", describe(null) -> "null!"

// Java 21 with a sealed hierarchy: no default needed
sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square {}
record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}
record Square(double side) implements Shape {}

static double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {
        case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();
        case Square sq -> sq.side() * sq.side();
    };
}   // area(new Square(3)) -> 9.0

The best part is exhaustiveness. With a sealed interface, if someone adds a Triangle, every switch without a case for it stops compiling. With the old if-else and instanceof chain, that was a silent runtime bug.

Order matters: the guarded Integer n when n > 100 must come before plain Integer n, or the compiler reports it as dominated.

So in practice on Java 21 I use switch expressions by default and reach for patterns plus sealed types instead of the visitor pattern.

Interviewers look for: the version numbers (14 for expressions, 21 for patterns) and exhaustiveness with sealed types.

AdvancedWhat do -7 / 2 and -7 % 2 give, and why is Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE) negative? 
System.out.println(-7 / 2);                 // -3
System.out.println(-7 % 2);                 // -1
System.out.println(Math.floorMod(-7, 2));   // 1
System.out.println(Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE)); // -2147483648
System.out.println(10 / 3.0);               // 3.3333333333333335
System.out.println(1 / 0.0);                // Infinity
System.out.println(1 / 0);                  // ArithmeticException: / by zero

So integer division in Java truncates toward zero, so -3.5 becomes -3, not -4. And % takes the sign of the dividend, so -7 % 2 is -1. That breaks the common check n % 2 == 1 for odd numbers when n is negative. I write n % 2 != 0 instead, or use Math.floorMod, which always returns a non-negative result for a positive divisor. That matters for things like circular buffer indices and hash bucket indices.

The Math.abs one is two's complement. int goes from -2,147,483,648 to 2,147,483,647, so the positive version of MIN_VALUE doesn't fit, and it overflows back to itself. I've seen this bug in sharding code: Math.abs(key.hashCode()) % n returned a negative shard index for the one key whose hash was MIN_VALUE. The fix is Math.floorMod(key.hashCode(), n).

And division by zero behaves differently by type: integers throw, floating point gives Infinity or NaN.

So in practice for any index computed from a hash I use floorMod, and I use Math.absExact (Java 15) when I want an exception instead of a silent wrong answer.

AdvancedHow do labeled break and continue work? Predict the output. 
outer:
for (int r = 0; r < 3; r++) {
    for (int q = 0; q < 3; q++) {
        if (q == 2) continue outer;
        if (r == 2) break outer;
        System.out.print(r + "" + q + " ");
    }
}
// 00 01 10 11

So a label names a loop, and break label or continue label acts on that outer loop instead of the innermost one. Plain break only exits the inner loop.

Walking through it: for r = 0, it prints 00 and 01, and when q hits 2, continue outer skips the rest of the inner loop and moves to the next r. Same for r = 1, printing 10 and 11. When r = 2, the very first inner iteration hits break outer and leaves both loops. Notice the r + "" + q: without the empty string it would add the ints.

Where I actually use this: searching a 2D grid and stopping as soon as I find a target, without a found flag checked in two places.

search:
for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++)
    for (int c = 0; c < grid[r].length; c++)
        if (grid[r][c] == target) { row = r; col = c; break search; }

So in practice labels are fine for that one case, but if I need more than one, I extract the loops into a method and use return, which reads better. Java has no goto; it's a reserved word that does nothing.

5. Strings

BasicWhy is String immutable in Java?

So once a String is created, its characters can never change. Every method that looks like it modifies a string, like toUpperCase or replace, returns a new one. Java did this for four reasons: pooling, hashing, security and thread safety.

String s = "hello";
s.toUpperCase();        // result discarded
System.out.println(s);  // hello
s = s.toUpperCase();    // now s points to a new object
System.out.println(s);  // HELLO

The reasons I'd give:

  • String pool: the JVM can let every "hello" literal in the program share one object only because nobody can change it.
  • Hash keys: String caches its hashCode on first use. If a key could change after going into a HashMap, it would sit in the wrong bucket and never be found again.
  • Security: class names, file paths, URLs and database URLs are strings. If a string could change after a security check, you'd have a time-of-check to time-of-use hole.
  • Thread safety: immutable objects can be shared across threads with no locking.

The class is also final, so no one can subclass it and add mutable behavior.

The cost is that every change is a copy, which is why building strings in a loop needs StringBuilder.

So in practice immutability is why I can use strings freely as map keys and share them across threads without thinking about it.

Interviewers look for: at least three reasons, with hashing and the pool first, plus the cost side.

BasicWhat's the difference between == and equals() for Strings?

So == compares references: are these the same object in memory? equals() compares content: do they have the same characters? For strings I almost always want equals.

String a = "java";
String b = "java";
String c = new String("java");

System.out.println(a == b);        // true  (same pooled literal)
System.out.println(a == c);        // false (different objects)
System.out.println(a.equals(c));   // true  (same characters)

String x = null;
System.out.println("java".equals(x));        // false, no NPE
// x.equals("java");                         // NPE
System.out.println(Objects.equals(x, null)); // true, null-safe both sides
System.out.println("Java".equalsIgnoreCase("JAVA")); // true

The dangerous part is that == seems to work, because literals are pooled, so a == b is true. It breaks the moment a string comes from user input, a file, a database or substring, because those create new objects. So code that passed every unit test with literals fails in production.

For null safety I either put the constant first, "ADMIN".equals(role), or use Objects.equals(a, b) when both could be null.

So in practice == on strings is a code review flag for me. The only legitimate use is comparing against something I know is interned, and I can't remember the last time that was worth it.

BasicWhat is the String pool, and how many objects does new String("x") create?

So the String pool is a table inside the JVM, on the heap since Java 7, that holds one shared copy of each string literal. When I write "x", the JVM reuses the pooled object if it exists. new String("x") creates up to two objects: the literal "x" in the pool, if it isn't there already, and a brand new String on the heap that is never pooled.

String a = "java";              // pooled
String c = new String("java");  // new heap object with its own identity, never pooled
a == c;            // false
a == c.intern();   // true: intern() returns the pooled copy

So the standard answer to "how many objects" is: two if "x" hasn't appeared before, one if the literal is already in the pool. Strictly speaking the literal gets pooled when the class is loaded and the constant is resolved, not when the line runs.

Let me picture it:

Stack         Heap
a  ---------> [String pool] "java"
c  ---------> String "java"   (a separate object outside the pool)

There's almost never a reason to write new String("literal"). It just wastes an object. intern() can save memory if you have millions of duplicate strings, like country codes loaded from a file, but these days G1's string deduplication (-XX:+UseStringDeduplication) does something similar automatically.

So in practice I use literals, never new String, and compare with equals.

Interviewers look for: "one or two objects depending on whether the literal is already pooled," and that the pool moved from PermGen to the heap in Java 7.

BasicStringBuilder vs StringBuffer: what's the difference?

So both are mutable character buffers with the same API. StringBuffer is from Java 1.0 and every method is synchronized. StringBuilder came in Java 5 as the unsynchronized version, which is faster, and it's what you should use almost all the time.

StringStringBuilderStringBuffer
MutableNoYesYes
Thread-safeYes (immutable)NoYes (synchronized)
Since1.051.0
Use forValues, keys, constantsBuilding strings in one threadLegacy APIs
StringBuilder x = new StringBuilder("abc");
StringBuilder y = new StringBuilder("abc");
x.equals(y);                          // false! not overridden, compares references
x.toString().equals(y.toString());    // true
x.compareTo(y) == 0;                  // true (Comparable since Java 11)

That equals result surprises people. Neither builder class overrides equals or hashCode, so never use them as map keys.

Why is StringBuffer rarely the answer even with threads? Because a builder is almost always a local variable inside one method, so it's never shared. And if two threads really append to the same buffer, per-method locking still doesn't make a sequence of appends atomic, so you'd need your own lock anyway.

So in practice I use StringBuilder everywhere and only see StringBuffer in old APIs like Matcher.appendReplacement overloads.

IntermediateWhat's wrong with concatenating strings with += in a loop? 
String s = "";
for (int i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) s += 'x';

So each += creates a brand new String and copies every character built so far. The first iteration copies 1 char, the next 2, and so on, so the total work is about n²/2 character copies. That's O(n²) for what should be O(n).

Let me show you with a quick measurement I ran on Java 21:

// same 100,000-char result both ways
String s = "";
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) s += 'x';       // about 1800 ms

StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sb.append('x'); // about 9 ms
String r = sb.toString();

A StringBuilder keeps one growable array and doubles its capacity when full, so each append is amortized O(1).

A follow-up I often get is "doesn't the compiler optimize +?" It does, but only within a single expression. "Hello " + name + "!" on one line is fine; since Java 9 it compiles to one invokedynamic call to StringConcatFactory, which sizes the result exactly. The compiler does not hoist a builder out of a loop, so the loop still creates a new string every iteration.

Other good options: String.join(",", list), Collectors.joining(",") for streams, and new StringBuilder(expectedSize) when I know the size, to avoid resizing.

So in practice: + for one-liners, StringBuilder or joining for loops.

Interviewers look for: the O(n²) reasoning, and knowing that single-expression concatenation is already optimized.

IntermediatePredict the output: compile-time constants and ==. 
String a = "java";

String ab = "ja" + "va";
System.out.println(a == ab);       // true

String part = "ja";
String built = part + "va";
System.out.println(a == built);    // false

final String fpart = "ja";
String built2 = fpart + "va";
System.out.println(a == built2);   // true

So the difference is whether the compiler can compute the value. "ja" + "va" is two literals, a compile-time constant, so javac folds it into the single literal "java" in the class file, which gets the pooled object. a == ab is true.

With part + "va", part is an ordinary variable, so the concatenation happens at runtime and produces a new object that isn't pooled. False.

The third case is the tricky one. final String fpart = "ja" is a constant variable: final, and initialized with a constant expression. The compiler substitutes its value, so fpart + "va" is folded again and is true. If fpart were final but assigned from a method call, like getPrefix(), it would be false again.

So in practice this question is really testing whether I know == checks identity and that identity depends on compiler details. That's exactly why I never use == for string content.

AdvancedWhy is char[] preferred over String for passwords? And what are compact strings?

So for passwords: a String is immutable and may be pooled, so I can't wipe it. It stays in memory until the GC reclaims it, which could be a long time, and it can show up in a heap dump or get logged by accident. A char[] I can overwrite with zeros the moment I'm done.

char[] password = console.readPassword("Password: ");
try {
    authenticate(password);
} finally {
    Arrays.fill(password, '\0');   // wipe it
}

That's why Console.readPassword, JPasswordField.getPassword and KeyStore APIs use char[]. A bonus: printing a char[] by accident via toString shows [C@1b6d3586, not the secret. I'd be honest that this is defense in depth, not a guarantee, because the JVM may have copied the data during GC.

Compact strings came in Java 9. Before that, a String was backed by a char[], 2 bytes per character. Now it's a byte[] plus a coder flag: if every character fits in Latin-1, it uses 1 byte per character, otherwise UTF-16 with 2 bytes. Since most strings in typical apps (JSON keys, IDs, log messages) are ASCII, this cut string memory roughly in half with zero code changes.

So in practice I use char[] for secrets I handle directly, and compact strings are a good example of why upgrading the JDK can reduce heap usage for free.

Interviewers look for: "I can clear an array but not a String," and the Java 9 byte[] plus coder layout.

6. Arrays

BasicWhat are the key properties of arrays in Java? length vs length() vs size()?

So a Java array is a fixed-size object holding elements of one type in contiguous memory. Once created, its size never changes. It knows its length, it's bounds-checked on every access, and its elements get default values.

boolean[] flags = new boolean[2];   // [false, false]
String[] names = new String[2];     // [null, null]
double[] ds = new double[1];        // [0.0]

int[] arr = new int[3];
// arr[3] = 1;   // ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException: Index 3 out of bounds for length 3

int[][] jag = new int[3][];          // jagged: rows created separately
jag[1] = new int[4];
jag[1].length;   // 4
jag[2];          // null (row never created)

And the three "length" spellings people mix up:

ThingSyntaxWhy
Arrayarr.lengthA final field, no parentheses
Strings.length()A method
Collectionlist.size()A method on the interface

Arrays are objects, so they live on the heap even when they hold primitives, and a variable of type int[] is a reference. A 2D array is really an array of references to row arrays, which is why rows can have different lengths.

So in practice I use arrays for fixed-size, performance-sensitive data like DP tables or counts, and ArrayList when the size changes.

BasicHow do you compare and print two arrays correctly?

So arrays don't override equals, hashCode or toString. They inherit them from Object, which means identity comparison and an unreadable default string. I use the static helpers in java.util.Arrays instead.

int[] a = {1, 2, 3};
int[] b = {1, 2, 3};
System.out.println(a == b);                 // false
System.out.println(a.equals(b));            // false (same as ==)
System.out.println(Arrays.equals(a, b));    // true
System.out.println(a);                      // [I@2a139a55 (type code + hash)
System.out.println(Arrays.toString(a));     // [1, 2, 3]

int[][] m1 = {{1, 2}, {3}};
int[][] m2 = {{1, 2}, {3}};
System.out.println(Arrays.equals(m1, m2));      // false
System.out.println(Arrays.deepEquals(m1, m2));  // true
System.out.println(Arrays.deepToString(m1));    // [[1, 2], [3]]

The 2D case is the trap. Arrays.equals compares element by element with equals, and the elements are row arrays, which again compare by identity. deepEquals and deepToString recurse into the nested arrays.

The same problem hits hashing: an int[] as a HashMap key or in a HashSet uses identity, so two arrays with the same content are different keys. For a "seen" set of states I convert to a List, a String key, or use Arrays.hashCode inside a wrapper record.

So in practice I never call equals or toString directly on an array.

Interviewers look for: knowing deepEquals exists for nested arrays, and the HashSet-of-arrays bug.

IntermediateWhat are the pitfalls of Arrays.asList?

So Arrays.asList returns a fixed-size list that is a view over the original array. You can set elements, but add and remove throw, and changes flow both ways between the array and the list. And with a primitive array, it doesn't do what people expect at all.

List<String> fixed = Arrays.asList("a", "b", "c");
fixed.set(0, "z");            // fine: [z, b, c]
fixed.add("d");               // UnsupportedOperationException

String[] backing = {"x", "y"};
List<String> view = Arrays.asList(backing);
backing[0] = "CHANGED";
System.out.println(view);     // [CHANGED, y]  (it's a view)

int[] nums = {1, 2, 3};
List<int[]> oops = Arrays.asList(nums);
oops.size();                  // 1 !  a list containing one int[]

List<Integer> ok = Arrays.stream(nums).boxed().toList();  // [1, 2, 3]

The primitive case happens because generics can't hold int, so the varargs T... can't be int.... The whole int[] becomes the single T.

Compare with List.of (Java 9): fully immutable, so even set throws, and it rejects null elements.

In one project someone returned Arrays.asList(...) from a repository method and a caller later did result.add(...). It compiled, since the return type was List, and crashed at runtime.

So in practice when I need a list I can grow, I write new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(...)), and for constants I use List.of.

IntermediateWhat is array covariance, and why does it cause ArrayStoreException?

So arrays in Java are covariant: because String is a subtype of Object, String[] is treated as a subtype of Object[]. That lets bad code compile, so the JVM has to check every store into a reference array at runtime.

Object[] objs = new String[2];   // compiles: covariance
objs[0] = 42;                    // compiles: 42 is an Object
// runtime: ArrayStoreException: java.lang.Integer

List<Object> list = new ArrayList<String>();   // does NOT compile: generics are invariant

The array knows its real element type at runtime, String, so when I try to put an Integer in, the JVM throws. That's a type error that slipped past the compiler.

Generics fixed this by being invariant: List<String> is not a List<Object>, so the mistake is caught at compile time. When I do need flexibility, I use wildcards: List<? extends Object> lets me read but not add, which is safe.

Covariance exists because arrays predate generics. Before Java 5, something like Arrays.sort(Object[]) needed to accept a String[], and covariance was how they did it.

So in practice I prefer List over arrays in APIs, partly for this reason, and I think of arrays as "covariant and checked at runtime" versus generics as "invariant and checked at compile time."

Interviewers look for: the contrast with generics invariance, which is the setup for PECS and wildcards.

IntermediateHow do you copy an array? Is clone() deep or shallow?

So there are four ways: clone(), Arrays.copyOf, Arrays.copyOfRange and System.arraycopy. All of them are shallow: they copy the elements, and if the elements are references, like row arrays or objects, both arrays end up pointing at the same things.

int[][] grid = {{1, 2}, {3, 4}};
int[][] shallow = grid.clone();
shallow[0][0] = 99;
System.out.println(grid[0][0]);   // 99: rows are shared!

int[][] deep = new int[grid.length][];
for (int i = 0; i < grid.length; i++) deep[i] = grid[i].clone();
deep[1][1] = -1;
System.out.println(grid[1][1]);   // 4: original untouched

int[] a = {1, 2, 3};
int[] grown = Arrays.copyOf(a, 5);   // [1, 2, 3, 0, 0], pads with defaults

System.arraycopy(src, srcPos, dest, destPos, length); // fastest, into an existing array

For a 1D array of primitives, shallow and deep are the same thing, so clone() is perfect. The problem is 2D arrays and arrays of mutable objects. I've hit this in backtracking problems: I "saved" a board with board.clone(), kept mutating the board, and all my saved snapshots changed with it.

System.arraycopy also handles overlapping ranges correctly, which is what ArrayList uses internally to shift elements on insert and remove.

So in practice: clone() or copyOf for 1D primitives, a per-row clone for 2D, and a real copy constructor for arrays of mutable objects.

AdvancedWhy can't you write new T[n] in a generic class?

So because of type erasure. Generics are a compile-time feature, and at runtime T is erased to Object (or its bound). But arrays need to know their real element type at runtime to do the store check we just talked about. The JVM has no idea what T is, so it can't create a T[].

class Box<T> {
    // T[] items = new T[10];            // compile error: generic array creation

    // Option 1: what ArrayList does internally
    private Object[] items = new Object[10];
    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    T get(int i) { return (T) items[i]; }

    // Option 2: pass the type in when you need a real T[]
    T[] toArray(IntFunction<T[]> factory, int n) { return factory.apply(n); }
}

// caller: box.toArray(String[]::new, 3) gives a real String[]

The unchecked cast in option 1 is safe as long as the Object[] never escapes the class, because only Ts go in. The trap is returning it as (T[]) items: the caller gets an Object[] pretending to be a String[], and assigning it to a String[] variable throws ClassCastException.

That's why Collection.toArray has an overload taking IntFunction<T[]> (Java 11), like list.toArray(String[]::new). The caller supplies the real array type.

So in practice inside a generic class I store an Object[] or just use a List<T>, and when I must hand out a typed array I take a generator or a Class<T> from the caller.

Interviewers look for: connecting erasure (generics are compile-time) with arrays being reified (their type is checked at runtime).

7. Methods & Pass-by-Value

BasicIs Java pass-by-value or pass-by-reference?

So Java is always pass-by-value. For primitives, the value is the number itself. For objects, the value that gets copied is the reference. The method gets its own copy of that pointer, so it can change the object it points to, but it can never make the caller's variable point somewhere else.

Let me show you with the classic swap example.

static void swap(Dog x, Dog y) { Dog tmp = x; x = y; y = tmp; }

Dog a = new Dog("Rex"), b = new Dog("Fido");
swap(a, b);
System.out.println(a + " " + b);   // Rex Fido  (nothing swapped)

Inside swap, x and y are copies of the references in a and b. Swapping them just swaps two local variables that disappear when the method returns. a and b in the caller still point where they did. In a true pass-by-reference language, like C++ with & parameters, this swap would work.

caller:  a --> [Dog Rex]  <-- x  (copy of a's reference)
         b --> [Dog Fido] <-- y  (copy of b's reference)
swap:    x and y exchange arrows; a and b never change

If I actually need a swap, I swap slots inside something both sides share, like an array:

static void swap(int[] arr, int i, int j) { int t = arr[i]; arr[i] = arr[j]; arr[j] = t; }
// arr = {1, 2} becomes {2, 1}

So in practice I say "pass-by-value, where the value of an object variable is a reference," which is precise and settles the argument.

Interviewers look for: the swap example as proof, and not saying "objects are passed by reference."

BasicPredict the output: modifying vs reassigning a parameter. 
static void rename(Dog d)   { d.name = "Max"; }
static void replace(Dog d)  { d = new Dog("Buddy"); d.name = "Charlie"; }
static void increment(int n) { n++; }
static void append(StringBuilder sb)   { sb.append(" world"); }
static void reassign(StringBuilder sb) { sb = new StringBuilder("gone"); }
static void change(String s) { s = s + "!"; }

Dog a = new Dog("Rex");
rename(a);   System.out.println(a);   // Max
replace(a);  System.out.println(a);   // Max
int n = 5; increment(n); System.out.println(n);   // 5
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("hello");
append(sb); reassign(sb); System.out.println(sb); // hello world
String s = "hi"; change(s); System.out.println(s); // hi

So the rule I apply: going through the reference to mutate the object is visible to the caller; reassigning the parameter is not.

rename follows the copied reference to the same Dog and changes its field, so the caller sees "Max". replace points its local d at a brand new Dog, and everything after that line touches the new object, so the caller's Dog stays "Max". increment gets a copy of 5. append mutates the shared builder, reassign only changes its local copy.

change with a String is the one people get wrong. It looks like modification, but strings are immutable, so s + "!" creates a new object and assigns it to the local s. The caller's string can't change, ever.

So in practice when a method needs to "return" a changed value, I return it, like s = addBang(s), rather than hoping a parameter change leaks out.

IntermediateHow does Java pick an overloaded method? Predict the output. 
static void f(long x)    { System.out.println("long"); }
static void f(Integer x) { System.out.println("Integer"); }
static void f(int... x)  { System.out.println("varargs"); }

f(5);                      // long

static void g(Object o) { System.out.println("Object"); }
static void g(String s) { System.out.println("String"); }

g(null);                   // String
Object o = "text";
g(o);                      // Object

So the compiler resolves overloads in three phases, and stops at the first phase that finds a match:

  1. Exact match or widening of primitives, and subtype for references. No boxing, no varargs.
  2. Boxing and unboxing allowed.
  3. Varargs allowed.

For f(5), phase 1 already finds f(long) through widening int to long, so Integer and varargs are never considered. This order exists for backward compatibility: code written before Java 5 added boxing and varargs had to keep calling the same method.

Within a phase, if several methods match, the compiler picks the most specific one. null fits both Object and String, and String is more specific, so "String". If I added a g(Integer), then g(null) would be ambiguous and fail to compile, because neither String nor Integer is more specific than the other.

And g(o) prints "Object" because overloading is chosen at compile time from the declared type. The runtime type being String doesn't matter. That's the key contrast with overriding, which is chosen at runtime.

Interviewers look for: the order "widening, then boxing, then varargs," and "overloading is compile time, based on the static type."

IntermediateMore overloading traps: char, long, byte and Integer arguments. 
static void h(char c)   { System.out.println("char"); }
static void h(int i)    { System.out.println("int"); }
static void h(double d) { System.out.println("double"); }

h('a');          // char
h(5L);           // double
byte b = 1;
h(b);            // int

static void k(Object o) { System.out.println("k Object"); }
static void k(long l)   { System.out.println("k long"); }

Integer boxed = 7;
k(boxed);        // k Object
k(7);            // k long

So each one follows the same phase rules. h('a') is an exact match. h(5L) has no long version, and long can't narrow to int, but it can widen to double, so "double". byte can widen to int or double, but it can't widen to char, because char is unsigned. Of the two, int is more specific, so "int".

k(boxed) is the interesting one. You might expect unboxing to long. But in phase 1, an Integer is already a subtype of Object with no conversion, so phase 1 succeeds and unboxing is never tried. k(7) is the reverse: phase 1 finds long by widening, which beats boxing 7 to reach Object.

A real-world version of this is List<Integer>.remove. list.remove(1) calls remove(int index), not remove(Object), so it removes the element at index 1, not the value 1. To remove the value I write list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1)).

So in practice I avoid overloads that differ only by numeric type or primitive versus wrapper. Distinct method names are clearer.

IntermediateCan you overload by return type alone? What are the rules for varargs?

So no. The compiler identifies a method by its name and parameter types, the signature. The return type isn't part of it, so two methods that differ only by return type won't compile, because at a call like calc(5); where the result is ignored, the compiler couldn't pick one.

int  calc(int x) { return x; }
// long calc(int x) { return x; }   // error: method calc(int) is already defined

static void v(int... xs) { System.out.println("v got " + xs.length); }
v();                   // v got 0
v(1, 2, 3);            // v got 3
v(new int[]{4, 5});    // v got 2  (an array can be passed directly)

Varargs rules I keep in mind:

  • Only one varargs parameter, and it must be last: format(String fmt, Object... args).
  • Inside the method it's just an array, and with no arguments it's an empty array, never null.
  • Each call allocates a new array, which is why APIs like List.of have fixed overloads for up to ten arguments before falling back to varargs.
  • Varargs is the last phase of overload resolution, so a fixed-arity method always wins if it matches.
  • Generic varargs like T... create a generic array, which gives a heap pollution warning; @SafeVarargs is how you promise the method is safe.

So in practice I use varargs for convenience APIs like logging and builders, and avoid overloading a varargs method with fixed-arity ones of similar types, because the resolution gets hard to predict.

Interviewers look for: "signature is name plus parameter types," and that throws clauses and return types don't count either.

AdvancedWhat happens on a StackOverflowError, and does Java optimize tail recursion?

So each method call pushes a frame onto the thread's stack with its local variables, parameters and return address. Deep or infinite recursion fills that fixed-size stack, and the JVM throws StackOverflowError. And no, HotSpot does not do tail call optimization, so even a tail-recursive method uses one frame per call.

static int depth = 0;
static void down() { depth++; down(); }

try { down(); }
catch (StackOverflowError e) { /* depth reaches thousands of frames, then this */ }

// tail-recursive, but still O(n) stack in Java
static long sum(int n, long acc) { return n == 0 ? acc : sum(n - 1, acc + n); }

// the fix: turn it into a loop
static long sumLoop(int n) { long acc = 0; while (n > 0) acc += n--; return acc; }

The exact depth depends on frame size and the stack size, which is typically 512 KB to 1 MB per thread by default and set with -Xss. It's an Error, not an Exception, so I shouldn't catch it in normal code; it signals a design problem.

In my last project a recursive tree walker over a category hierarchy worked fine until a customer imported data with a cycle, and it overflowed. The fix was an explicit Deque as a stack plus a visited set.

So in practice for recursion that could go deep, like DFS on a large graph or a long linked list, I use an explicit stack or a loop. Raising -Xss or running the recursion in a new Thread(null, task, "worker", 256L << 20) with a bigger stack works, but I treat it as a last resort.

Part 2 — Object-Oriented Programming

8. Classes & Objects

BasicWhat are the four pillars of OOP? Give me a real example of each, not the textbook definitions.

So the four pillars are encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance and polymorphism. Encapsulation hides an object's state behind methods. Abstraction exposes only what a caller needs. Inheritance reuses and specialises behaviour. Polymorphism lets one call do different things depending on the real object.

Let me show you with a quick example from a checkout service, where all four show up in about twenty lines.

interface PaymentMethod { String pay(long cents); }             // abstraction

static abstract class Card implements PaymentMethod {          // inheritance
    private final String last4;                                 // encapsulation
    Card(String number) { this.last4 = number.substring(number.length() - 4); }
    protected String masked() { return "**** " + last4; }
}
static class CreditCard extends Card {
    CreditCard(String n) { super(n); }
    public String pay(long cents) { return "Charged " + cents + " to credit " + masked(); }
}
static class Upi implements PaymentMethod {
    public String pay(long cents) { return "UPI request for " + cents; }
}

List<PaymentMethod> methods = List.of(new CreditCard("4111111111111111"), new Upi());
for (PaymentMethod m : methods) System.out.println(m.pay(2500));  // polymorphism
// Charged 2500 to credit **** 1111
// UPI request for 2500

Walking through it: the card number never leaves Card. Only the last four digits are stored, and they're private, so that's encapsulation. The checkout code only knows PaymentMethod.pay. It has no idea about card networks or UPI handles, so that's abstraction. CreditCard reuses the masking logic from Card through inheritance. And the loop calls pay on each element and the JVM picks the right implementation at runtime. That's polymorphism.

So in practice the pillar that matters most day to day is abstraction through interfaces. It's what lets me add a new payment method without touching the checkout loop.

Interviewers look for: one connected example instead of four memorised definitions, and a sense of which pillar actually earns its keep in real code.

BasicWhat's the difference between a class and an object? What actually happens when I write new?

So a class is the blueprint: it declares fields and methods and lives once in the JVM as loaded metadata. An object is one instance of that blueprint on the heap, with its own copy of the instance fields. One class, many objects.

When I write new Order("A1"), a few things happen in order:

  1. The JVM makes sure Order is loaded, linked and initialised. Static blocks run here, but only the first time.
  2. It allocates memory on the heap for all instance fields, including inherited ones, and sets them to defaults: 0, false, null.
  3. It runs the constructor chain. The Object constructor runs first, then each parent down the hierarchy, and for each class the field initialisers and instance blocks run before its constructor body.
  4. It returns the reference, which I store in a variable on the stack.
Order a = new Order("A1");
Order b = new Order("A1");
Order c = a;
a == b;   // false  two separate objects on the heap
a == c;   // true   two references to the same object

A point people miss: the variable a isn't the object. It's a reference to it. That's why c = a doesn't copy anything. It just adds a second arrow to the same object.

So in practice, when a bug looks like "I changed one object and another one changed too", I check whether they're really two references to one object.

BasicWhen does Java give you a default constructor? What happens if you add a parameterised one?

So the compiler adds a no-arg constructor only when the class declares no constructors at all. As soon as I write any constructor, the default one is gone, and new User() stops compiling.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class User {
    String name;
    User(String name) { this.name = name; }
}

User u = new User();
// error: constructor User in class User cannot be applied to given types;
//   required: String

The default constructor has the same access as the class and its only job is to call super(). That second part causes a related error with inheritance. If a parent class has only a parameterised constructor, a subclass that doesn't call super(...) explicitly won't compile, because the implicit super() has nothing to call.

static class Animal { Animal(String name) {} }
static class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog() { System.out.println("dog"); }   // implicit super() -> compile error
}

This matters in real projects with frameworks. JPA entities and Jackson (without extra config) need a no-arg constructor. I've seen deserialisation break the moment someone added a convenience constructor to a DTO, because the default one silently disappeared.

So in practice, if a framework needs a no-arg constructor, I write it explicitly, often protected, so it can't vanish by accident.

IntermediateWhat is constructor chaining? What are the rules for this() and super()?

So constructor chaining is one constructor calling another. this(...) calls a constructor in the same class, and super(...) calls one in the parent. The rule in Java 21 is that either call must be the very first statement, and you can have only one of them, never both.

Let me show you with a quick example, and predict the output.

static class Pizza {
    final String size; final String crust; final boolean cheese;
    Pizza()                   { this("medium");              System.out.println("Pizza()"); }
    Pizza(String size)        { this(size, "thin");          System.out.println("Pizza(size)"); }
    Pizza(String size, String crust) { this(size, crust, true); System.out.println("Pizza(size, crust)"); }
    Pizza(String size, String crust, boolean cheese) {
        this.size = size; this.crust = crust; this.cheese = cheese;
        System.out.println("Pizza(all) -> " + size + ", " + crust + ", " + cheese);
    }
}
new Pizza();
// Pizza(all) -> medium, thin, true
// Pizza(size, crust)
// Pizza(size)
// Pizza()

The calls go down the chain, then the bodies unwind back up, so the most complete constructor prints first. The pattern is called telescoping: every short constructor fills in a default and forwards, and all the real assignment happens in one place.

Why only one call, and why first? Because the object must be fully built from the parent down before my code touches it. If this() and super() were both allowed, the parent would be initialised twice. If there's neither, the compiler inserts super() for me. Java 22 started previewing "statements before super()" for argument validation, but in Java 21 the first-statement rule is strict.

So in practice I keep one "main" constructor that does the work and chain the rest into it. Beyond three or four parameters I switch to a builder.

Interviewers look for: the correct reverse order of the print statements, and knowing the compiler inserts super() when you don't write it.

IntermediatePredict the output: static blocks, instance blocks and constructors in a parent and child class.

So the rule is: static initialisation happens once per class, parent before child, when the class is first used. Then for every object, each class in the hierarchy runs its instance initialisers and then its constructor body, from the top of the hierarchy down.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1. Parent static block"); }
    { System.out.println("3. Parent instance block"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("4. Parent constructor"); }
}
static class Child extends Parent {
    static { System.out.println("2. Child static block"); }
    { System.out.println("5. Child instance block"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("6. Child constructor"); }
}

new Child();
System.out.println("--- second object ---");
new Child();
1. Parent static block
2. Child static block
3. Parent instance block
4. Parent constructor
5. Child instance block
6. Child constructor
--- second object ---
3. Parent instance block
4. Parent constructor
5. Child instance block
6. Child constructor

Here's how I walk through it. new Child() triggers class initialisation. Java initialises the superclass first, so both static blocks run, parent then child. Then the Child constructor starts, and its first hidden statement is super(). So Parent runs its instance block and constructor body. Control comes back to Child, and its instance block runs, then its constructor body. On the second object the static blocks don't run again, because a class is initialised only once.

One detail that often trips people up: instance blocks and field initialisers are copied by the compiler into each constructor, right after the super() call, in the order they appear in the source.

So in practice I avoid logic in static and instance blocks. It runs at surprising times, and a static block that throws gives you ExceptionInInitializerError and then NoClassDefFoundError on every later use of that class.

Interviewers look for: that static blocks don't repeat for the second object, and the "super() first, then my instance blocks, then my body" explanation.

IntermediateCan a constructor be private? Final? Static? Abstract?

So private: yes, and it's useful. Final, static and abstract: no, they're compile errors, and each one is forbidden for a clear reason.

Let me show you with a quick example of why private is useful.

static final class MathUtils {
    private MathUtils() { throw new AssertionError("no instances"); }  // utility class
    static int square(int x) { return x * x; }
}

static final class Temperature {
    private final double celsius;
    private Temperature(double c) { this.celsius = c; }
    static Temperature ofCelsius(double c)    { return new Temperature(c); }
    static Temperature ofFahrenheit(double f) { return new Temperature((f - 32) * 5 / 9); }
}
Temperature.ofFahrenheit(212);   // 100.0C

A private constructor is how I build singletons, utility classes that must never be instantiated, and static factory methods like ofFahrenheit. Static factories have a name, can cache instances, and can return a subtype.

ModifierAllowed?Why
private / protected / public / defaultYesControls who can create instances
finalNo: "modifier final not allowed here"Constructors aren't inherited, so there's nothing to stop overriding
staticNoA constructor exists to initialise an instance; static means "no instance"
abstractNoA constructor must have a body; abstract means "no body"
synchronizedNoNo other thread can see the object while it's being constructed, so there's nothing to lock

One catch with private constructors: they don't fully stop reflection. setAccessible(true) can still call them, which is why the utility class throws AssertionError and why an enum is the safest singleton.

So in practice I make constructors private whenever I want to control creation, and expose named factory methods instead.

AdvancedWhat's wrong with calling an overridable method from a constructor? Predict the output.

So the parent constructor runs before the child's fields are initialised. If the parent calls a method the child overrides, the child's version runs on a half-built object and sees default values, like null and 0.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Base {
    Base() { describe(); }
    void describe() { System.out.println("Base"); }
}
static class Derived extends Base {
    private String name = "Derived";
    private final int size = 42;     // compile-time constant
    private int count = 7;
    @Override void describe() { System.out.println(name + " " + size + " " + count); }
}

Derived d = new Derived();   // null 42 0
d.describe();                // Derived 42 7

Walking through it: new Derived() calls super() first. Base() calls describe(), and dynamic dispatch picks Derived.describe. But the Derived field initialisers haven't run yet, so name is null and count is 0. The interesting one is size: it's a final field with a constant initialiser, so the compiler inlines 42 directly into the method, and it prints correctly even though the field is technically unset. After construction finishes, everything prints normally.

In real code this shows up as a NullPointerException inside an overridden init() or template hook, which is very confusing to debug because the field "clearly" has a value.

So in practice constructors should call only private, final or static methods. If I need a hook, I use a factory method that constructs the object first and then calls the hook.

Interviewers look for: "fields of the subclass aren't initialised yet", and bonus points for spotting the constant-inlining detail on size.

9. Encapsulation & Access Modifiers

BasicWhat is encapsulation, and why does it matter?

So encapsulation means an object owns its state: the fields are private and the only way to change them is through methods that enforce the rules. The point isn't hiding for its own sake. It's protecting invariants, the things that must always be true.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Not encapsulated: anyone can break it
public class Account { public double balance; }
account.balance = -500;          // nothing stops this

// Encapsulated: the rule lives in one place
public class Account {
    private long balanceCents;
    public void withdraw(long cents) {
        if (cents <= 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("amount must be positive");
        if (cents > balanceCents) throw new IllegalStateException("insufficient funds");
        balanceCents -= cents;
    }
    public long balanceCents() { return balanceCents; }
}

With the first version, every caller in the codebase has to remember the rules, and one of them will forget. With the second, the rule is enforced once, and I can change the internal representation, say from double to long cents, without touching any caller.

So in practice I start every field as private, and I add methods that express behaviour (withdraw, deposit) instead of generic setters. A setter for every field is just a public field with extra steps.

Interviewers look for: "invariants" and "freedom to change the internals", not just "make fields private".

BasicExplain the four access modifiers. Who can see what?

So Java has four levels, from most to least restrictive: private, package-private (no keyword), protected, and public.

ModifierSame classSame packageSubclass, other packageEverywhere
privateYesNoNoNo
(default)YesYesNoNo
protectedYesYesYes, through inheritanceNo
publicYesYesYesYes

Two things people get wrong. First, protected is wider than default, not narrower, because it includes the whole package. Second, a top-level class can only be public or package-private. private and protected are allowed only on nested classes.

Since Java 9 there's also a level above public: modules. A public class in a package the module doesn't export is invisible to other modules. That's how the JDK hides its sun.* internals.

So in practice I use package-private a lot more than people expect. It's perfect for helper classes that only one package needs, and tests in the same package can still reach them.

IntermediateIs making fields private and adding getters and setters enough for encapsulation?

So no. If a getter returns a mutable object, like a List or a Date, the caller gets a reference to my internal state and can change it without going through any of my methods. The field is private, but the object inside it has escaped.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Team {
    private final List<String> members = new ArrayList<>();
    Team(String... names) { members.addAll(List.of(names)); }
    List<String> getMembers() { return members; }   // leaks the internal list
}
Team t = new Team("Asha", "Ben");
