t.getMembers().clear();
System.out.println(t.getMembers().size());      // 0  -- emptied from outside

static class SafeTeam {
    private final List<String> members;
    SafeTeam(List<String> names) { this.members = List.copyOf(names); }
    List<String> getMembers() { return members; }   // already unmodifiable
}
new SafeTeam(List.of("Asha", "Ben")).getMembers().clear();
// throws UnsupportedOperationException

The fix works in both directions. On the way in I copy, so the caller can't keep a handle to my list and change it later. On the way out I return something unmodifiable: either a List.copyOf (Java 10) stored once, or Collections.unmodifiableList(members) as a read-only view.

In a project I worked on, a cached config object handed out its internal Map, and one request handler called put on it "temporarily". Every later request saw the change. It took a while to find because the field was private and final, so everyone assumed it was safe.

So in practice I treat any getter that returns a mutable type as a leak until I've made it defensive.

Interviewers look for: defensive copies on both the way in and the way out, and knowing that final on the field doesn't help here.

IntermediateHow exactly does protected work across packages? What's the catch?

So in another package, a subclass can use a protected member only through a reference of its own type, meaning this or another instance of the subclass. It cannot reach the protected member on an arbitrary instance of the parent class.

Let me show you with a quick example.

package com.shop.model;
public class Account { protected double balance = 100; }

package com.shop.app;
import com.shop.model.Account;
public class Savings extends Account {
    void addInterest()        { balance *= 1.05; }                      // OK: inherited, via this
    void peek(Savings other)  { System.out.println(other.balance); }    // OK: my own type
    void steal(Account any)   { System.out.println(any.balance); }      // compile error
}
// error: balance has protected access in Account

The reasoning is that protected means "for my subclasses to use in their own implementation", not "for any subclass to poke at any account". If steal compiled, any class could extend Account just to read the balance of every Account object it's handed, which would make protected almost the same as public.

And remember, in the same package none of this applies, because protected includes package access.

So in practice I use protected sparingly, mostly for template-method hooks in abstract classes. It's part of the class's API for subclasses, so I can't change it freely later.

IntermediateCan one object access another object's private fields?

So yes, as long as both objects are the same class. Java's private is per class, not per object. Code inside the class can read the private fields of any instance of that class.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Money {
    private final long cents;
    Money(long cents) { this.cents = cents; }
    boolean greaterThan(Money other) { return this.cents > other.cents; }  // other's private field
}
new Money(500).greaterThan(new Money(300));   // true

That's exactly how equals, compareTo and copy constructors are normally written: they read other.field directly, without needing getters.

A related rule: an outer class and its nested classes can read each other's private members too. Before Java 11 the compiler made that work by generating hidden "access$000" bridge methods. Since Java 11 (nestmates, JEP 181) the JVM understands the relationship directly, so no bridge methods are needed.

So in practice this is a feature, not a loophole. Encapsulation protects the class's invariants from other code, and code in the same class is already trusted to maintain them.

Interviewers look for: the words "per class, not per object", and ideally the equals example.

AdvancedCan an overriding method have a different access modifier?

So it can make access wider but never narrower. A protected method can be overridden as public, but a public method can't be overridden as protected, package-private or private.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class A { public void run() {} }
static class B extends A { void run() {} }
// error: run() in B cannot override run() in A
//   attempting to assign weaker access privileges; was public

The reason is the Liskov substitution principle. Anywhere my code holds an A, it's allowed to call run(). If a B could hide run(), then passing a B where an A is expected would break a promise the type system already made. Widening is fine because it only makes more calls legal.

The classic real example is clone(). It's protected in Object, and classes that want callers to use it override it as public, usually with a covariant return type too.

The same "you can't break the parent's promise" logic explains two other rules: an override can't throw broader checked exceptions, and it can't change the return type except to a subtype.

So in practice I treat any override as a promise to be at least as usable as the parent, and I let @Override and the compiler catch it when I'm not.

10. Inheritance

BasicWhat is inheritance, and what does a subclass not inherit?

So inheritance is an IS-A relationship: a subclass gets the parent's fields and methods and can add or override behaviour. Dog extends Animal means every Dog can be used wherever an Animal is expected.

What a subclass doesn't inherit:

  • Constructors. They're never inherited. The subclass must call one through super(...), explicitly or implicitly.
  • Access to private members. Private fields are physically inside the subclass object, since the memory is allocated for them, but the subclass can't name them. It reaches them only through the parent's methods.
  • Package-private members, when the subclass is in a different package.
  • Static members belong to the class. They're accessible through the subclass name, but they aren't overridden, only hidden.
class Animal {
    private String name;
    protected Animal(String name) { this.name = name; }
    public String name() { return name; }
}
class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog(String name) { super(name); }         // must call the parent constructor
    String greet() { return "Woof, I am " + name(); }   // can't use this.name directly
}

Java supports single inheritance of classes (one extends) and multiple inheritance of types through interfaces. Every class ultimately extends Object.

So in practice I use inheritance only when the IS-A test is genuinely true and I control both classes. For code reuse alone, I prefer composition.

BasicWhy doesn't Java support multiple inheritance of classes?

So it's to avoid the diamond problem and the complexity that comes with it. If a class could extend two classes that both define the same method, or both carry state, the language would need rules for which one wins and how many copies of the shared ancestor's fields exist. Java's designers chose to keep that out.

Let me show you the problem it avoids.

//        Device (int batteryLevel; start())
//        /                      \
//   Camera extends Device    Phone extends Device
//     start() { ... }           start() { ... }
//        \                      /
//         SmartPhone extends Camera, Phone    // not legal Java
//
// new SmartPhone().start()  -> Camera's or Phone's?
// How many batteryLevel fields -- one or two?

C++ allows this and needs "virtual inheritance" to answer the second question. That's exactly the kind of complexity Java wanted to avoid.

What Java does allow is multiple inheritance of type through interfaces. A class can implement many interfaces. Before Java 8 that was trivially safe, because interfaces had no method bodies and no state. Since Java 8, interfaces can have default methods, so a mild diamond can happen with behaviour. But it still can't happen with state, because interfaces can't have instance fields. The compiler forces you to resolve it explicitly.

So in practice the answer is: multiple inheritance of state is banned, multiple inheritance of behaviour is allowed through default methods with explicit conflict resolution, and multiple inheritance of type has always been fine.

Interviewers look for: the three-way split of state, behaviour and type, and a lead-in to how default methods handle the diamond.

IntermediatePredict the output: a field and a method with the same name in parent and child.

So methods are polymorphic but fields are not. A method call is resolved from the real object's class at runtime. A field access is resolved from the reference's declared type at compile time. A child field with the same name doesn't override the parent's. It hides it, and the object ends up holding both.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Animal {
    String name = "animal";
    String sound() { return "..."; }
}
static class Dog extends Animal {
    String name = "dog";
    @Override String sound() { return "woof"; }
    void show() { System.out.println(name + " " + super.name + " " + super.sound()); }
}

Animal a = new Dog();
System.out.println(a.name);           // animal   (declared type decides)
System.out.println(a.sound());        // woof     (real object decides)
System.out.println(((Dog) a).name);   // dog
((Dog) a).show();                     // dog animal ...

Walking through it: a is declared as Animal, so a.name reads Animal's field. a.sound() goes through dynamic dispatch and finds Dog.sound. After the cast, the compiler sees a Dog, so it reads Dog's field. Inside show, super.name and super.sound() reach the parent's versions explicitly.

That's the super keyword's three jobs: call the parent constructor, call the parent's version of an overridden method, and reach a hidden field.

So in practice field hiding is always a bug waiting to happen. With private fields and accessor methods it can't occur, which is one more reason fields should be private.

IntermediateComposition vs inheritance. Why do people say "favour composition"?

So inheritance couples my class to the parent's implementation, not just its interface. If the parent calls its own overridable methods internally, my overrides get pulled into that in ways I can't see. Composition, where I hold the other object in a field and forward calls to it, depends only on its public contract.

Let me show you with a quick example. It's the classic one from Effective Java.

static class InstrumentedHashSet<E> extends HashSet<E> {
    int addCount = 0;
    @Override public boolean add(E e) { addCount++; return super.add(e); }
    @Override public boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) {
        addCount += c.size(); return super.addAll(c);
    }
}
var s = new InstrumentedHashSet<String>();
s.addAll(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
System.out.println(s.addCount);   // 6, not 3

static class InstrumentedSet<E> {           // composition
    private final Set<E> inner = new HashSet<>();
    int addCount = 0;
    boolean add(E e) { addCount++; return inner.add(e); }
    boolean addAll(Collection<? extends E> c) { addCount += c.size(); return inner.addAll(c); }
}
// same calls -> addCount is 3

Why 6? HashSet.addAll, inherited from AbstractCollection, loops and calls add for each element. That's my overridden add, so every element is counted twice. Nothing in my code shows that. It depends on an internal detail of the JDK that could change in any release.

The version with composition doesn't care how HashSet works internally. It can also wrap any Set, not just HashSet, if I take the set in the constructor.

InheritanceComposition
RelationshipIS-AHAS-A
CouplingTo the parent's implementationTo the public API only
Change at runtimeNo, fixed at compile timeYes, swap the field
Good forTrue subtypes, template methods in my own hierarchyReuse, decorators, strategies, wrapping third-party classes

So in practice I inherit only from classes designed for it, usually my own abstract classes, and I compose everything else.

Interviewers look for: the concrete "self-use" failure, not just the slogan. Being able to explain why it prints 6 is the whole answer.

IntermediateWhat happens if the parent class has no no-arg constructor?

So the subclass must call one of the parent's constructors explicitly with super(...). If it doesn't, the compiler inserts super(), finds no matching constructor, and fails.

static class Animal { Animal(String name) {} }

static class Dog extends Animal {
    Dog() { System.out.println("dog"); }
}
// error: constructor Animal in class Animal cannot be applied to given types;
//   required: String

static class Cat extends Animal {
    Cat() { super("cat"); }      // fixed
}

The same thing happens with no constructor at all in Dog: the default constructor also contains super(), so it fails too. The error points at the subclass even though the "cause" is the parent's design, which confuses people the first time.

I actually like this rule. A parent that requires a name is saying "an animal without a name is invalid", and the compiler forces every subclass to respect that.

So in practice, when I see this error, the fix is almost never to add a no-arg constructor to the parent. It's to pass the required values through super(...).

AdvancedHow do you stop a class from being extended? Where do sealed classes fit in?

So there are three levels. final stops all extension. A private constructor also stops it from outside, since a subclass can't call super(). And since Java 17, sealed allows extension only by a fixed list of classes I name.

static class MyString extends String {}
// error: cannot inherit from final String

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square, Rect {}
record Circle(double r) implements Shape {}         // records are implicitly final
record Square(double side) implements Shape {}
static non-sealed class Rect implements Shape { double w = 2, h = 3; }  // reopened on purpose

static double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {                 // no default needed: compiler knows every case
        case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();
        case Square q -> q.side() * q.side();
        case Rect r   -> r.w * r.h;
    };
}
area(new Square(4));   // 16.0

Each permitted subclass must pick one of final, sealed or non-sealed, so the hierarchy is deliberately closed or deliberately opened at every step.

The real payoff of sealed is the exhaustive switch (pattern matching for switch was finalised in Java 21). If I later add Triangle to the permits list, every switch without a Triangle case stops compiling. That's much better than a default branch that silently swallows the new type.

Why stop extension at all? Security and correctness. String is final so that nobody can pass a "String" that changes after it's been validated. And a class not designed for inheritance breaks in the way the HashSet counting example shows.

So in practice I make classes final by default, use sealed for domain types with a known set of variants (payment results, events, AST nodes), and open a class up only when I've designed it for extension.

Interviewers look for: the connection between sealed types and exhaustive switch in Java 21, not just the syntax.

11. Polymorphism

BasicWhat is polymorphism? Compile-time vs runtime polymorphism?

So polymorphism means one name, many behaviours. Compile-time polymorphism is method overloading: the compiler picks which method to call from the argument types. Runtime polymorphism is method overriding: the JVM picks the implementation from the actual object's class while the program runs.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Compile-time: chosen by argument types
static int area(int side)          { return side * side; }
static int area(int w, int h)      { return w * h; }

// Runtime: chosen by the real object
interface Notifier { void send(String msg); }
class EmailNotifier implements Notifier { public void send(String m) { /* SMTP */ } }
class SmsNotifier   implements Notifier { public void send(String m) { /* SMS gateway */ } }

Notifier n = user.prefersSms() ? new SmsNotifier() : new EmailNotifier();
n.send("Your order shipped");   // which send() runs is decided at runtime

The runtime version is the one that matters for design. The code that calls n.send is written once against the interface, and new notifier types plug in without changing it. That's the open/closed principle in practice, and it's how Spring injects different beans behind the same interface.

Some people call overloading "not real polymorphism", because nothing is decided at runtime. It's just the compiler choosing between different method names that happen to look the same. I'd mention that, but still call it static polymorphism, since that's the standard interview terminology.

So in practice when I say "polymorphism" in a design discussion, I mean programming to an interface and letting dynamic dispatch pick the implementation.

BasicOverloading vs overriding: what are the differences?

So overloading is the same method name with a different parameter list, usually in the same class, resolved at compile time. Overriding is a subclass replacing an inherited method with the same signature, resolved at runtime.

OverloadingOverriding
WhereSame class, or inherited plus newSubclass of the declaring class
ParametersMust differ (number, type or order)Must be the same
Return typeAnything; doesn't count toward the signatureSame, or a subtype (covariant)
Access modifierAnythingSame or wider
Checked exceptionsAnythingSame, narrower, or none
ResolvedCompile time, from declared typesRuntime, from the object's class
static / private / finalCan be overloadedCannot be overridden
class Printer {
    void print(String s)           { }
    void print(String s, int copies) { }   // overload
}
class ColorPrinter extends Printer {
    @Override void print(String s) { }      // override
}

One trap: changing only the return type isn't an overload. int size() and long size() in the same class is a compile error, because the compiler couldn't tell which one a call like size(); means.

So in practice I always put @Override on overrides. If I get the signature slightly wrong, say equals(Point p) instead of equals(Object o), I've accidentally overloaded instead of overridden, and the annotation turns that silent bug into a compile error.

Interviewers look for: return type and exception rules, and the equals(Point) accidental-overload trap.

IntermediateCan you override a static method? A private method? Predict the output.

So no to both. A static method with the same signature in a subclass hides the parent's method, and the call is picked from the reference's declared type. A private method isn't visible to the subclass at all, so a same-named method in the child is a brand-new, unrelated method.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Parent {
    static String who() { return "Parent.static"; }
    private String secret() { return "Parent.private"; }
    String callSecret() { return secret(); }
    String hello() { return "Parent.hello"; }
}
static class Child extends Parent {
    static String who() { return "Child.static"; }        // hides
    private String secret() { return "Child.private"; }   // unrelated new method
    @Override String hello() { return "Child.hello"; }     // overrides
}

Parent p = new Child();
System.out.println(p.who());          // Parent.static
System.out.println(p.hello());        // Child.hello
System.out.println(p.callSecret());   // Parent.private

Walking through it: p.who() compiles to Parent.who(), because static calls are bound to the declared type. The object is ignored. p.hello() is a real override, so the JVM dispatches to Child. callSecret lives in Parent and calls Parent's private method directly. Private methods aren't virtual, so Child.secret never gets a chance.

If you put @Override on either the static or the private method in Child, the compiler rejects it. That's the quickest proof that it isn't overriding. Also, calling a static method through an instance, like p.who(), compiles but IDEs warn about it for exactly this reason: it looks polymorphic and isn't.

So in practice I always call static methods through the class name, and I treat a matching private method name in a subclass as a coincidence.

Interviewers look for: the word "hiding", and the explanation that static binding uses the reference type.

IntermediatePredict the output: overloading with null, widening, boxing and varargs.

So the compiler picks an overload in three phases. First it tries exact match and widening, with no boxing and no varargs. Then it allows boxing and unboxing. Only then does it consider varargs. Within a phase, the most specific type wins.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static void print(Object o)  { System.out.println("Object"); }
static void print(String s)  { System.out.println("String"); }
static void take(long x)     { System.out.println("long"); }
static void take(Integer x)  { System.out.println("Integer"); }
static void take(int... x)   { System.out.println("varargs"); }

print(null);          // String   -- most specific: String is a subtype of Object
take(5);              // long     -- widening (phase 1) beats boxing (phase 2)
Object o = "hello";
print(o);             // Object   -- decided by the declared type, not the value
char c = 'a';
take(c);              // long     -- char widens to long

Walking through it: for print(null), both overloads accept null, and String is more specific than Object, so it wins. For take(5), most people guess Integer, but widening int to long is found in phase 1, so boxing is never even considered. Varargs is always the last resort. For print(o), overloading is compile-time, so the declared type Object decides, even though the value is a String.

And if the two candidates are unrelated, like print(String) and print(StringBuilder), then print(null) doesn't compile: "reference to print is ambiguous".

A real bug from this: List<Integer> has both remove(int index) and remove(Object o). list.remove(1) removes the element at index 1, not the value 1. You need list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1)).

So in practice I avoid overloads that differ only by boxed vs primitive or by related types. Different names are clearer.

Interviewers look for: the order "widening, then boxing, then varargs", and the List.remove example as a real consequence.

IntermediateWhat are covariant return types?

So since Java 5, an overriding method can declare a return type that's a subtype of the parent's return type. The override still satisfies the parent's contract, since a Dog is an Animal, but callers who know they have the subclass get the more specific type without a cast.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Animal {
    Animal copy() { return new Animal(); }
}
static class Dog extends Animal {
    @Override Dog copy() { return new Dog(); }   // narrower return type
    void bark() { System.out.println("woof"); }
}

Dog d = new Dog();
d.copy().bark();     // woof -- no (Dog) cast needed

Before Java 5 the override had to return Animal, and every caller wrote ((Dog) d.copy()).bark(). The classic real use is clone(): Object.clone() returns Object, but an overriding public Order clone() can return Order. Fluent builders in a class hierarchy use it too, so SubBuilder.name(...) returns SubBuilder and the chain keeps going.

Under the hood, the compiler generates a synthetic bridge method in Dog with the old signature Animal copy() that calls the new one. That's how the JVM, which matches methods by exact descriptor, still finds the override.

It only works for reference types. You can't narrow long to int, because primitives don't have a subtype relationship.

So in practice I use it for clone, copy methods and builders, anywhere returning the more specific type saves callers a cast.

AdvancedWhat are the exception rules when overriding a method?

So an override may throw the same checked exceptions, narrower ones, or none at all. It may not add new or broader checked exceptions. Unchecked exceptions aren't restricted.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Reader1 { void read() throws IOException {} }
static class Ok1 extends Reader1 { @Override void read() throws FileNotFoundException {} } // narrower
static class Ok2 extends Reader1 { @Override void read() {} }                             // none
static class Ok3 extends Reader1 { @Override void read() throws IllegalStateException {} } // unchecked
static class Bad extends Reader1 { @Override void read() throws Exception {} }
// error: read() in Bad cannot override read() in Reader1
//   overridden method does not throw Exception

The reason is the same substitution argument as with access modifiers. Code written against Reader1 has a catch (IOException e) and nothing else. If a subclass could throw Exception, that caller would get a checked exception it was never forced to handle.

This rule has a practical consequence I've hit in real code. When I implement an interface like Runnable, whose run() declares no exceptions, I can't let a checked exception escape. I have to catch it or wrap it in an unchecked one. That's why Callable.call() declares throws Exception: it was designed for tasks that can fail with checked exceptions.

Constructors are the opposite case. They aren't overridden, so a subclass constructor must declare everything its super(...) call can throw, and it may add more.

So in practice when I design an interface method, I think carefully about the throws clause, because it caps what every implementation can ever throw.

AdvancedHow does the JVM actually decide which overridden method to call?

So it's done in two steps. At compile time, javac picks the method signature from the declared type, which also settles any overloading, and emits an invokevirtual (or invokeinterface) instruction for that signature. At runtime, the JVM looks at the actual object's class and finds the most specific implementation of that signature, usually through a per-class method table called the vtable.

Animal a = new Dog();
a.sound();
// javac:   invokevirtual Animal.sound()Ljava/lang/String;   (signature fixed now)
// runtime: a's class is Dog -> Dog's vtable slot for sound() -> Dog.sound()

Each class's vtable starts as a copy of its parent's, and overridden methods replace their slot. So a virtual call is basically "load the object's class pointer, index into the table, jump". That's cheap.

The JIT makes it even cheaper. If profiling shows a call site only ever sees one class, which is called monomorphic, HotSpot inlines the method with a quick type guard. With two classes (bimorphic), it inlines both. Only with many classes (megamorphic) does it fall back to the table lookup. So in hot code, "virtual calls are slow" is mostly a myth.

This also explains the earlier puzzles. Static and private methods use invokestatic and invokespecial, which have no runtime lookup, so they can't be overridden. Fields have no vtable at all, so they're resolved from the declared type.

So in practice I don't avoid interfaces for performance. The JIT removes most of the dispatch cost, and when I suspect a megamorphic hot spot, I measure with JMH first.

Interviewers look for: the compile-time/runtime split (signature chosen statically, implementation chosen dynamically) and some awareness of JIT inlining.

12. Abstract Classes vs Interfaces

BasicWhat's the difference between an abstract class and an interface in modern Java?

So an abstract class is a partially built class. It can have state, constructors and any kind of method, and a class can extend only one. An interface is a contract. Since Java 8 it can have default and static methods, and since Java 9 private methods, but it still can't have instance fields or constructors, and a class can implement as many as it wants.

Abstract classInterface
Instance fields (state)YesNo: only public static final constants
ConstructorsYesNo
Method bodiesYes, any accessdefault and static (8), private (9)
Method accessAnyPublic, except private helpers
How many per classOne (extends)Many (implements)
RelationshipIS-A, shared implementationCAN-DO, a capability
Can be a lambda targetNoYes, if it has one abstract method

The big thing default methods didn't change is state. An interface still can't hold per-object data. So if my shared code needs fields, like a logger name, a counter or cached config, it has to live in an abstract class.

So in practice: an interface for the type that callers depend on, and optionally an abstract class that implements it to share code between implementations. The JDK does exactly this with List and AbstractList.

Interviewers look for: an answer updated for Java 8/9, not the pre-2014 "interfaces have no method bodies" line, and "state" as the real remaining difference.

BasicCan an abstract class have a constructor? Can you instantiate one?

So yes, it can have a constructor, and no, you can't call new on it directly. The constructor runs when a subclass is created, through super(...), and its job is to initialise the abstract class's own fields.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static abstract class Report {
    private final String title;
    protected Report(String title) { this.title = title; System.out.println("Report ctor: " + title); }
    abstract String body();
    final String render() { return "== " + title + " ==\n" + body(); }   // template method
}
static class SalesReport extends Report {
    SalesReport() { super("Sales"); }
    String body() { return "Total: 1200"; }
}

new SalesReport().render();
// Report ctor: Sales
// == Sales ==
// Total: 1200

Report quick = new Report("Adhoc") { String body() { return "anonymous subclass"; } };
// Report ctor: Adhoc  -- this is an anonymous subclass, not an instance of Report itself

I make the constructor protected, since only subclasses can use it anyway. The new Report("Adhoc") { ... } line looks like it instantiates the abstract class, but it actually creates an anonymous subclass and instantiates that.

Also, an abstract class doesn't need any abstract methods. Marking a class abstract just means "don't instantiate me directly". And a class with even one abstract method must be declared abstract.

So in practice this is the template method pattern: the abstract class fixes the algorithm in a final method, holds the shared state, and subclasses fill in the steps.

IntermediateWhy were default methods added in Java 8?

So the main reason was interface evolution. Java 8 needed to add methods like stream() and forEach() to Collection and Iterable. Without default methods, every class in the world implementing Collection, including thousands in third-party libraries, would have stopped compiling.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Simplified from the JDK
public interface Collection<E> extends Iterable<E> {
    // ...existing abstract methods...
    default Stream<E> stream() {
        return StreamSupport.stream(spliterator(), false);
    }
    default boolean removeIf(Predicate<? super E> filter) {
        boolean removed = false;
        for (Iterator<E> it = iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
            if (filter.test(it.next())) { it.remove(); removed = true; }
        }
        return removed;
    }
}

A default method is written purely in terms of the interface's other methods, like iterator() and spliterator(). So old implementations get it for free, and classes that can do better, like ArrayList with its own faster removeIf, override it.

The second reason is that it let the JDK add combinator methods to functional interfaces: Comparator.thenComparing, Predicate.and, Function.andThen. Those are what make lambdas pleasant to compose.

What default methods are not for is sneaking state or heavy logic into interfaces. They can't have fields, and if you find yourself writing complicated default methods, you probably want an abstract class.

So in practice I use default methods for backward-compatible API evolution and small convenience methods, and I keep them thin.

IntermediateWhat is the diamond problem with default methods, and how does Java resolve it?

So if a class implements two interfaces that both provide a default method with the same signature, the class doesn't compile until it overrides the method itself. Inside the override, it can call a specific parent with InterfaceName.super.method().

Let me show you with a quick example.

interface Camera { default String start() { return "camera on"; } }
interface Phone  { default String start() { return "phone on"; } }

static class SmartPhone implements Camera, Phone {}
// error: types Camera and Phone are incompatible;
//   class SmartPhone inherits unrelated defaults for start() from types Camera and Phone

static class SmartPhone implements Camera, Phone {
    @Override public String start() {
        return Camera.super.start() + " + " + Phone.super.start();
    }
}
new SmartPhone().start();   // camera on + phone on

There are two automatic rules before it ever gets to an error:

  1. Class wins. A method from a superclass always beats an interface default.
  2. More specific interface wins. If one interface extends the other, the sub-interface's default wins.
static class Device { public String start() { return "device on"; } }
static class Tablet extends Device implements Camera {}
new Tablet().start();       // device on   (class wins)

interface Base    { default String id() { return "Base"; } }
interface Special extends Base { default String id() { return "Special"; } }
static class Gadget implements Base, Special {}
new Gadget().id();          // Special     (more specific wins)

Only when neither rule settles it do you get the compile error. The key point is that Java never picks silently. Either there's a clear winner, or you decide.

So in practice this rarely bites, but when it does, I override the method and delegate explicitly to whichever parent I want, or combine them.

Interviewers look for: the exact X.super.method() syntax and the two rules, "class wins" and "more specific interface wins".

IntermediateAfter Java 8, when would you still choose an abstract class over an interface?

So I'd choose an abstract class when the shared code needs state, a constructor, non-public methods, or when I want to lock down a template method with final. Everywhere else I default to an interface, because a class can implement many interfaces but extend only one class.

My decision checklist:

I need...Use
A type that unrelated classes can adopt (Comparable, AutoCloseable)Interface
A lambda targetFunctional interface
Shared fields, like a repository or metrics referenceAbstract class
A constructor that enforces required valuesAbstract class
protected hooks or a final algorithm skeletonAbstract class
Both: a public contract plus shared codeInterface + abstract base class implementing it
public interface PaymentGateway { Receipt charge(Money amount); }  // what callers depend on

public abstract class BaseGateway implements PaymentGateway {    // what implementers reuse
    protected final HttpClient http;
    protected BaseGateway(HttpClient http) { this.http = http; }
    public final Receipt charge(Money amount) {                   // fixed flow
        validate(amount);
        return doCharge(amount);
    }
    protected abstract Receipt doCharge(Money amount);
    private void validate(Money m) { /* ... */ }
}

Callers and Spring wiring only see PaymentGateway. A new provider can extend BaseGateway for free plumbing, or implement the interface directly if it's completely different. It can also be mocked easily in tests.

So in practice the interface is the API and the abstract class is an optional implementation helper. I never make callers depend on the abstract class.

AdvancedWhat can an interface contain today? Explain static and private interface methods.

So a Java 21 interface can have abstract methods, default methods, static methods (Java 8), private and private static methods (Java 9), constants, and nested types. It still can't have instance fields, constructors, or protected members.

Let me show you with a quick example.

interface Validator {
    boolean validate(String s);                                   // abstract

    default Validator and(Validator other) {                      // Java 8
        return s -> validate(s) && other.validate(s);
    }
    static Validator notBlank() { return s -> !s.isBlank(); }     // Java 8 factory

    static Validator maxLen(int n) {
        return s -> log("checking " + s) && s.length() <= n;
    }
    private static boolean log(String m) {                         // Java 9 helper
        System.out.println(m); return true;
    }
}

Validator v = Validator.notBlank().and(Validator.maxLen(5));
v.validate("hello");   // prints "checking hello", returns true
v.validate("   ");     // returns false; short-circuits before maxLen runs

Static interface methods are for factories and utilities that belong with the type, like List.of, Comparator.comparing and Map.entry. Before Java 8 these lived in separate companion classes like Collections. One catch: static interface methods aren't inherited, so you must call Validator.notBlank(), never someImpl.notBlank().

Private methods exist so default methods can share code without making the helper part of the public API.

And since Java 17, interfaces can be sealed, which, combined with records, is how I model closed sets of alternatives.

So in practice a well-designed interface today often holds its contract, a few default combinators, and static factories, all in one place.

Interviewers look for: the correct version numbers (8 for default/static, 9 for private) and knowing static interface methods aren't inherited.

13. The Object Class: equals, hashCode, toString

BasicWhat methods does Object have, and which ones do you usually override?

So Object gives every class equals, hashCode, toString, getClass, clone, finalize, and the monitor methods wait, notify and notifyAll. The ones I override regularly are equals, hashCode and toString.

MethodDefault behaviourOverride?
equalsIdentity, same as ==For value objects
hashCodeIdentity-based numberAlways together with equals
toStringClassName@hexHashAlmost always; it's for logs and debugging
cloneProtected, shallow field copy, needs CloneableRarely; prefer copy constructors
finalizeDoes nothingNever; deprecated in Java 9, for removal since 18
getClassRuntime classCan't (it's final)
wait / notify / notifyAllLow-level thread coordinationCan't (final); prefer java.util.concurrent
System.out.println(new User());   // DefaultToString$User@ followed by a hex hash -- useless in a log

On finalize: it was meant for cleanup, but it runs at an unpredictable time, maybe never, and slows down garbage collection. For cleanup I use try-with-resources and AutoCloseable, or java.lang.ref.Cleaner (Java 9) as a safety net.

So in practice for data classes I don't write these by hand any more. A record generates equals, hashCode and toString correctly.

BasicWhat's the difference between == and equals()?

So == on references checks identity: are these the same object in memory? equals() checks logical equality: do these objects mean the same thing? It's whatever the class defines. For primitives, == just compares values.

String a = new String("java");
String b = new String("java");
a == b;          // false  two objects
a.equals(b);     // true   same characters

Integer x = 127, y = 127;
x == y;          // true   cached by Integer.valueOf (-128..127)
Integer p = 128, q = 128;
p == q;          // false  outside the cache: two objects
p.equals(q);     // true

The Integer case is the nasty one. Code that compares boxed IDs with == passes every test with small numbers and then fails in production once IDs pass 127. I've seen exactly this with Long user IDs coming out of a JPA entity.

If a class doesn't override equals, it inherits Object.equals, which is just ==. So equals is only "logical" if someone wrote it that way.

For a null-safe comparison I use Objects.equals(a, b). It returns true if both are null and doesn't throw if a is null.

So in practice: == for primitives, enums, and deliberate identity checks. equals for everything else.

Interviewers look for: the Integer cache trap as a real-world consequence, and Objects.equals for null safety.

IntermediateWhat is the equals/hashCode contract? What breaks if you override only one?

So the contract is: if two objects are equals, they must have the same hashCode. The reverse isn't required, since collisions are allowed. equals must also be reflexive, symmetric, transitive, consistent, and return false for null. Hash-based collections rely on this: they use hashCode to find the bucket first, and only then call equals inside that bucket.

Let me show you what breaks when you override only equals.

static class Point {                     // equals but NO hashCode
    final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof Point p && p.x == x && p.y == y;
    }
}
Set<Point> set = new HashSet<>();
set.add(new Point(1, 2));
new Point(1, 2).equals(new Point(1, 2));   // true
set.contains(new Point(1, 2));             // false
set.add(new Point(1, 2));
set.size();                                // 2  -- "duplicate" in a Set

Walking through it: the two points are equal, but each one uses the identity hash from Object, so they land in different buckets. The set never even calls equals. That's why contains says false and the set holds two "equal" elements. As a HashMap key, you'd put a value and then fail to get it back with an equal key.

The other way round, overriding only hashCode, is legal but pointless. Equal-looking objects land in the same bucket, but equals is still identity, so they're still treated as different. Adding two new HashOnly(1) objects to a set gives size 2.

@Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }   // the fix

So in practice I always override both together, generated from the same fields, and for plain data I use a record so I can't get it wrong.

Interviewers look for: "bucket first, then equals" as the mechanism, and the concrete symptom: contains false, duplicates in a Set, lost map entries.

IntermediateWrite a correct equals and hashCode. Should you use instanceof or getClass()?

So a correct equals checks identity, then type, then compares the significant fields, and hashCode uses exactly the same fields. On instanceof vs getClass(): instanceof is right for final classes, while getClass() is safer when subclasses may add fields, because instanceof can break symmetry.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static final class Employee {
    private final String id;
    private final String email;
    Employee(String id, String email) { this.id = id; this.email = email; }

    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        if (this == o) return true;                          // fast path
        if (!(o instanceof Employee other)) return false;    // Java 16 pattern; handles null too
        return id.equals(other.id) && Objects.equals(email, other.email);
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(id, email); }
    @Override public String toString() { return "Employee[id=" + id + ", email=" + email + "]"; }
}

The class is final, so instanceof is safe. Here's what goes wrong without that:

// Point.equals uses instanceof Point; ColorPoint extends Point and adds color
Point p = new Point(1, 2);
ColorPoint cp = new ColorPoint(1, 2, "red");
p.equals(cp);   // true   -- cp is an instanceof Point
cp.equals(p);   // false  -- p is not a ColorPoint
// symmetry broken: a HashSet may or may not treat them as duplicates

There's no way to extend an instantiable class, add a value field, and keep the equals contract with instanceof. getClass() != o.getClass() avoids the asymmetry, but then a ColorPoint is never equal to a plain Point, and it breaks Hibernate proxies, which are subclasses.

So in practice I make value classes final (or records) and use instanceof. For JPA entities I compare by ID only, handle proxies, and keep hashCode constant or ID-based. For everything else I let the IDE or a record generate it.

IntermediateWhat happens if you mutate an object after using it as a HashMap key?

So the entry gets lost. The map stored it in a bucket based on the old hash. After the mutation, lookups compute the new hash and go to a different bucket, and a lookup with the old value finds the right bucket but fails equals. The entry is still there, taking memory, but it's unreachable by key.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Key {
    String name;
    Key(String n) { name = n; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) { return o instanceof Key k && k.name.equals(name); }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return name.hashCode(); }
}

Map<Key, Integer> map = new HashMap<>();
Key k = new Key("alice");
map.put(k, 1);
k.name = "bob";

map.get(k);                  // null  -- looks in bob's bucket
map.get(new Key("alice"));   // null  -- right bucket, but equals fails ("bob" vs "alice")
map.size();                  // 1     -- the entry is stuck in there

This is a real way to get a memory leak. If the code keeps putting "new" keys and mutating them, the map grows forever and nothing can remove the old entries by key.

It's also the strongest argument for making keys immutable. String, Integer, enums and records with immutable components are all safe keys. The same rule applies to elements of a HashSet and to the fields a TreeMap comparator reads.

So in practice I only use immutable objects as keys. If a domain object is mutable, I key the map by its ID instead of the object.

Interviewers look for: that the entry still exists (size is 1) but is unreachable, and "keys must be immutable" as the fix.

IntermediateShallow copy vs deep copy: what's the difference, and how does clone() behave?

So a shallow copy creates a new object but copies references as they are, so nested objects are shared between the original and the copy. A deep copy also copies the nested mutable objects, so the two are fully independent. Object.clone() does a shallow copy.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Order implements Cloneable {
    String id;
    List<String> items = new ArrayList<>();
    Order(String id) { this.id = id; }

    @Override public Order clone() {                  // covariant return
        try { return (Order) super.clone(); }         // shallow
        catch (CloneNotSupportedException e) { throw new AssertionError(e); }
    }
    Order deepCopy() {
        Order o = new Order(id);
        o.items = new ArrayList<>(items);             // new list, same strings
        return o;
    }
}

Order original = new Order("A1");
original.items.add("pen");
Order shallow = original.clone();
Order deep = original.deepCopy();
shallow.items.add("book");

original.items;                    // [pen, book]  -- shared list changed
deep.items;                        // [pen]
original.items == shallow.items;   // true

Walking through it: super.clone() copies each field's value. For items, the value is a reference, so both orders point at one list. The deep copy creates a new list. The strings inside are still shared, but that's fine because strings are immutable. A deep copy only needs to go as far down as the mutable parts.

So in practice I don't use clone(). Cloneable is a marker interface with no clone method, super.clone() skips constructors, and it doesn't work with final fields that need new values. I use a copy constructor or a static copyOf factory, and immutable objects where possible, since those never need copying at all.

AdvancedWhat makes a good hashCode, and why does Objects.hash or 31 * h show up everywhere?

So a good hashCode is consistent with equals, cheap to compute, and spreads different objects across many values so buckets stay short. The 31 * h + field pattern mixes each field in, so that field order and every field affect the result.

@Override public int hashCode() {
    int h = id.hashCode();
    h = 31 * h + Objects.hashCode(email);   // null-safe
    h = 31 * h + Integer.hashCode(age);
    return h;
}
// Same idea as Objects.hash(id, email, age), without the varargs array

Why 31? It's an odd prime, so multiplying doesn't lose bits the way an even number would, and the JIT can compute 31 * h as (h << 5) - h. It's what String.hashCode uses.

What makes a bad one:

  • return 1; is legal, but every entry lands in one bucket. Since Java 8 HashMap turns long buckets into trees, so lookups become O(log n) instead of O(n), but that's still much slower than O(1).
  • Using a mutable field means the entry gets lost when that field changes.
  • Including a field that equals ignores breaks the contract: equal objects get different hashes.

Objects.hash is convenient but allocates a varargs array and boxes primitives on every call. On a hot path I write it out by hand, or cache it in a field for immutable objects, the way String does.

So in practice I generate it from exactly the fields used in equals, and for records I let the compiler do it.

Interviewers look for: "same fields as equals", awareness of the treeified-bucket fallback, and the cost of Objects.hash on hot paths.

14. Nested, Inner & Anonymous Classes

BasicWhat kinds of nested classes does Java have?

So there are four: static nested classes, inner classes (non-static members), local classes declared inside a method, and anonymous classes. The first question to ask about any of them is whether it holds a hidden reference to an instance of the outer class. Only the static nested class never does.

KindDeclaredHas outer instance?Typical use
Static nestedstatic class Node inside a classNoBuilders, map entries, helper types that belong with the outer class
Innerclass Itr inside a classYesIterators and views that need the outer object's data
LocalInside a method bodyYes, if in an instance methodRare; small helper used in one method
Anonymousnew Interface() { ... }Yes, if in an instance methodOne-off implementations; mostly replaced by lambdas
public class LinkedList<E> {
    private static class Node<E> { E item; Node<E> next; }   // static nested: no outer needed
    private class Itr implements Iterator<E> {               // inner: reads the list's head
        Node<E> cursor = head;
        public boolean hasNext() { return cursor != null; }
        public E next() { E e = cursor.item; cursor = cursor.next; return e; }
    }
    private Node<E> head;
}

The JDK's own collections follow exactly this split. HashMap.Node is static nested, while ArrayList's iterator is an inner class because it needs the list's array and modCount.

So in practice my default is static for any nested class. I drop the static only when the nested class genuinely needs the outer instance.

IntermediateStatic nested class vs inner class: how are they created, and what can each access?

So a static nested class is basically a top-level class that lives inside another for namespacing and access. You create it without any outer object. An inner class is tied to one outer instance, so you need that instance to create it, and it can read the outer object's fields directly, including private ones.

Let me show you with a quick example.

public class Nested {
    private int value = 10;
    static class StaticNested { int get() { return 1; } }
    class Inner { int get() { return value; } }        // reads Nested.this.value

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        StaticNested sn = new Nested.StaticNested();   // no outer object
        Nested outer = new Nested();
        Nested.Inner in = outer.new Inner();           // unusual syntax: outer.new
        System.out.println(sn.get() + " " + in.get()); // 1 10
    }
}

The outer.new Inner() syntax is the giveaway. The compiler passes outer into the inner class's constructor as a hidden parameter and stores it in a synthetic field. Inside Inner, value is really Nested.this.value.

A static nested class can still access the outer class's private static members, and the private instance members of any outer object you hand it. It just doesn't have an implicit one.

Before Java 16, inner classes couldn't declare static members. JEP 395, which delivered records, relaxed that, so now they can.

So in practice, if I see outer.new Inner() in a codebase, I check whether Inner really uses the outer instance. Usually it doesn't, and it should be static.

IntermediateHow can an inner class cause a memory leak?

So every inner class instance holds a hidden strong reference to its outer object. If the inner object outlives the outer one, for example because it's registered as a listener, submitted to a long-running executor, or stored in a static cache, it keeps the entire outer object and everything the outer object references alive.

Let me show you with a quick example, using reflection to show the hidden field.

public class InnerLeak {
    private final byte[] bigBuffer = new byte[50_000_000];
    class Task implements Runnable {
        public void run() { System.out.println("buffer " + bigBuffer.length); }
    }
    class Quiet implements Runnable {
        public void run() { System.out.println("quiet"); }
    }
    static class StaticTask implements Runnable {
        public void run() { System.out.println("static"); }
    }
}
// Declared fields, via reflection:
// Task: this$0/InnerLeak     <- hidden reference to the outer object
// Quiet:                     <- none: since JDK 18, javac drops it if unused
// StaticTask:                <- never has one

So if a Task gets stuck in a queue or a static list, it pins 50 MB through this$0, even though the code that created it is long gone. A heap dump shows exactly this chain: static list, then the Task, then this$0, then the huge outer object.

The classic real case is Android, where an inner Handler or AsyncTask held on to an Activity after rotation. On the server side, I've seen anonymous listeners registered on a singleton event bus from a request-scoped object and never unregistered. Each request leaked its whole object graph.

A modern detail: since JDK 18, javac doesn't emit this$0 for inner classes that never use the outer instance. That's the Quiet line. But you shouldn't rely on it, and anonymous classes or lambdas that do use outer fields still capture it.

So in practice I make nested classes static by default, pass in only the data they need, and always unregister listeners in a close() or lifecycle method.

Interviewers look for: the hidden this$0 reference, a concrete "outlives the outer object" scenario, and "make it static" as the fix.

IntermediateAnonymous class vs lambda: what's the difference? Predict the output.

So a lambda is not just shorter syntax for an anonymous class. An anonymous class creates a new class with its own this, and it can implement interfaces with several methods or extend classes. A lambda can only implement a functional interface, has no this of its own (this means the enclosing object), and is compiled with invokedynamic instead of a separate .class file.

Let me show you with a quick example.

public class ThisLambda {
    @Override public String toString() { return "outer"; }
    void demo() {
        Runnable anon = new Runnable() {
            @Override public void run() { System.out.println(this); }
            @Override public String toString() { return "anonymous"; }
        };
        Runnable lambda = () -> System.out.println(this);
        anon.run();     // anonymous
        lambda.run();   // outer
    }
}
Anonymous classLambda
thisThe anonymous instanceThe enclosing instance
TargetAny interface or classFunctional interface only
StateCan have fieldsNo fields
Compiled toOuter$1.classinvokedynamic + a private method
Captures outer thisIn instance context (javac may drop it if unused, JDK 18+)Only if it uses it
ShadowingCan redeclare outer variable namesCan't

Both can only capture local variables that are effectively final.

So in practice I use lambdas for functional interfaces and reach for an anonymous class only when I need state, several methods, or an abstract class. Those cases are rare now.

AdvancedWhy must local variables captured by a lambda or anonymous class be effectively final?

So a lambda doesn't capture the variable itself. It captures a copy of its value at the moment it's created. If Java allowed the local to change afterwards, the copy and the original would silently disagree. Requiring it to be effectively final guarantees they never can.

Let me show you with a quick example.

int count = 0;
Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(count);
count++;
// error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression
//        must be final or effectively final

Why a copy? Local variables live on the stack frame of the method, and that frame disappears when the method returns. But the lambda might run later, on another thread, long after the method is gone. So the value is copied into the lambda object. Once it's a copy, allowing writes would give confusing semantics and data races, since two threads would think they share a variable they don't actually share.

Fields are different. A lambda that uses this.count captures this, not a copy of the field, so it can read and write the field. That's also why it can be a thread-safety problem.

The workaround people use is the one-element array or an AtomicInteger:

final int[] counter = {0};
Runnable inc = () -> counter[0]++;   // the reference is final; the array contents aren't
inc.run(); inc.run();
counter[0];                          // 2

It compiles, but it's usually a sign I should use a stream reduction or a proper accumulator instead. With threads, it's a race condition waiting to happen unless I use AtomicInteger.

So in practice I treat the compile error as a hint that my code is mutating shared state from inside a lambda, and I restructure it.

Interviewers look for: "captures the value, not the variable", and why fields behave differently.

AdvancedImplement a Builder using a static nested class. Why static?

So a Builder is a mutable helper that collects the parameters and then creates an immutable object in one step. It's a static nested class because a builder must exist before the object it builds. An inner class would need an outer instance, and that instance doesn't exist yet.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static final class HttpRequest {
    private final String url; private final String method; private final int timeoutMs;
    private HttpRequest(Builder b) { url = b.url; method = b.method; timeoutMs = b.timeoutMs; }
    static Builder builder(String url) { return new Builder(url); }
    public String toString() { return method + " " + url + " (" + timeoutMs + "ms)"; }

    static final class Builder {
        private final String url;                 // required
        private String method = "GET";            // optional, with defaults
        private int timeoutMs = 5000;
        private Builder(String url) { this.url = url; }
        Builder method(String m) { method = m; return this; }
        Builder timeout(int ms) { timeoutMs = ms; return this; }
        HttpRequest build() { return new HttpRequest(this); }   // validate here
    }
}

HttpRequest.builder("/orders").method("POST").timeout(2000).build();
// POST /orders (2000ms)

A few details I'd point out. Required parameters go in builder(url), so you can't forget them. HttpRequest's constructor is private and takes the builder, which works because outer and nested classes can read each other's private fields. build() is the one place to validate the whole combination, for example "POST needs a body".

The alternative is the telescoping constructors I mentioned earlier, which become unreadable around four parameters. And new HttpRequest("/orders", "POST", 2000, true, false) gives no hint what true and false mean.

So in practice I use Lombok's @Builder or write one by hand for classes with many optional fields. For small value types, a record with a couple of static factories is usually enough.

15. Enums & Records

BasicWhat is an enum in Java, and what can it contain?

So an enum is a class with a fixed set of instances, created once when the class loads. Unlike C-style enums, a Java enum can have fields, constructors, methods, and even per-constant method bodies. The constructor is always private.

Let me show you with a quick example.

enum Plan {
    FREE(0, 1), PRO(999, 5), TEAM(2999, 50);

    private final int priceCents; private final int seats;
    Plan(int priceCents, int seats) { this.priceCents = priceCents; this.seats = seats; }
    int priceCents() { return priceCents; }
    boolean fits(int users) { return users <= seats; }
}

Plan.PRO.priceCents();              // 999
Plan.PRO.fits(8);                   // false
Plan.valueOf("TEAM").ordinal();     // 2
Plan.FREE == Plan.valueOf("FREE");  // true -- one instance per constant, so == is safe
Plan.valueOf("pro");                // IllegalArgumentException: No enum constant ...Plan.pro

String label = switch (Plan.PRO) {  // exhaustive switch expression (Java 14)
    case FREE -> "trial";
    case PRO, TEAM -> "paid";
};

Every enum extends java.lang.Enum, so it gets name(), ordinal(), compareTo(), values() and valueOf(), and it's automatically Comparable and Serializable. It can't extend another class, but it can implement interfaces.

For collections keyed by an enum, EnumMap and EnumSet are backed by an array and a bit vector, which makes them much faster than HashMap and HashSet.

So in practice I use an enum anywhere I'd otherwise have string or int constants, like statuses, plans or roles. The compiler then catches typos and missing switch cases for me.

IntermediateWhy is an enum the best way to implement a Singleton?

So an enum singleton is thread-safe, lazy enough, and it's the only version that's automatically safe against both serialisation and reflection creating a second instance. The JVM itself guarantees there's exactly one INSTANCE.

Let me show you with a quick example, comparing it with the classic private-constructor singleton.

enum Config {
    INSTANCE;
    private int hits;
    int hit() { return ++hits; }
}

static class ClassicSingleton implements Serializable {
    static final ClassicSingleton INSTANCE = new ClassicSingleton();
    private ClassicSingleton() {}
}

// Serialise and deserialise each one:
roundTrip(Config.INSTANCE) == Config.INSTANCE;                    // true
roundTrip(ClassicSingleton.INSTANCE) == ClassicSingleton.INSTANCE; // false -- a second instance!

// Reflection:
var c = ClassicSingleton.class.getDeclaredConstructor();
c.setAccessible(true);
c.newInstance() == ClassicSingleton.INSTANCE;                     // false -- another one
var ec = Config.class.getDeclaredConstructor(String.class, int.class);
ec.setAccessible(true);
ec.newInstance("HACK", 1);
// IllegalArgumentException: Cannot reflectively create enum objects

Walking through it: deserialisation normally creates a brand-new object, so the classic version needs a readResolve() method to return the real instance. Enums are serialised by name and resolved back to the existing constant, so there's nothing to add. For reflection, Constructor.newInstance has an explicit check that refuses enum classes.

The one limitation is that an enum can't extend a class. And if the singleton is expensive to create, it's created when the enum class initialises, which in practice is on first use anyway.

So in practice, in a Spring app I rarely write singletons at all, because the container manages single beans. When I do need a true JVM-wide singleton, I use an enum.

Interviewers look for: the serialisation and reflection points with evidence, not just "Effective Java says so".

IntermediateWhat is a record? What do you get for free, and what can't it do?

So a record (final in Java 16) is a transparent carrier for immutable data. From one line, the compiler generates private final fields, a canonical constructor, accessor methods named after the components, and equals, hashCode and toString based on all components.

Let me show you with a quick example.

record Money(long cents, String currency) {
    Money {                                          // compact constructor: validate / normalise
        if (cents < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative");
        currency = currency.toUpperCase();           // assigns the parameter; field set after
    }
    Money plus(Money o) { return new Money(cents + o.cents, currency); }
    static Money zero(String cur) { return new Money(0, cur); }
}

var a = new Money(500, "usd");
var b = new Money(500, "USD");
a;                       // Money[cents=500, currency=USD]
a.equals(b);             // true, and same hashCode
a.cents();               // accessor is cents(), not getCents()
new Money(-1, "usd");    // IllegalArgumentException: negative

What a record can't do: it can't extend a class (it implicitly extends java.lang.Record), it's implicitly final, and it can't declare extra instance fields. record Point(int x, int y) { int z; } fails with "field declaration must be static". It can implement interfaces and have static fields, static methods, instance methods and nested types.

A catch: records are only shallowly immutable.

record Leaky(List<String> items) {}
var list = new ArrayList<>(List.of("x"));
var leaky = new Leaky(list);
list.add("y");
leaky.items();   // [x, y]  -- changed from outside

record Team(String name, List<String> members) {
    Team { members = List.copyOf(members); }        // fix: defensive copy
}

So in practice I use records for DTOs, API responses, value objects, map keys, and multi-value returns. I always copy mutable components in the compact constructor.

Interviewers look for: the compact constructor, "shallow immutability" with the fix, and the no-extra-instance-fields rule.

IntermediateRecords vs Lombok vs a normal class: when do you use each?

So a record is a language feature for immutable data with value semantics. Lombok is an annotation processor that generates boilerplate for ordinary classes, including mutable ones. A hand-written class gives full control. I pick based on whether the thing is pure data, needs mutability or framework support, or has real behaviour.

recordLombok (@Value / @Data)Plain class
MutabilityImmutable (shallow)@Value immutable, @Data mutableYour choice
InheritanceCan't extend; finalCan extendCan extend
Extra instance fieldsNoYesYes
Accessorsname()getName()Whatever you write
BuilderHand-written@BuilderHand-written
DependencyNone, it's the languageBuild plugin + IDE plugin; hooks into javac internalsNone
JPA entityNo (needs no-arg ctor, mutability, proxies)Common, but avoid @Data on entitiesYes
Pattern matchingRecord patterns (Java 21)NoNo

A real example from a Spring Boot service: request and response DTOs became records, and Jackson has handled them natively since 2.12. JPA entities stayed plain classes with Lombok @Getter and @Setter, but not @Data, because its generated equals, hashCode and toString touch lazy relationships. That triggers extra queries and can even cause infinite recursion on bidirectional mappings.

So in practice my rule is: records for data by default, plain classes for entities and anything with identity or lifecycle, and Lombok only where it removes real noise on those plain classes.

IntermediateWhat are constant-specific methods in enums, and why is persisting ordinal() dangerous?

So each enum constant can have its own body that overrides an abstract method declared in the enum. That replaces a switch on the enum with polymorphism. ordinal() is just the declaration position, so if you store it, reordering or inserting a constant silently changes the meaning of every stored row.

Let me show you with a quick example.

enum Op {
    ADD("+") { int apply(int a, int b) { return a + b; } },
    MUL("*") { int apply(int a, int b) { return a * b; } };

    final String symbol;
    Op(String s) { symbol = s; }
    abstract int apply(int a, int b);
}
for (Op op : Op.values()) System.out.println("6 " + op.symbol + " 7 = " + op.apply(6, 7));
// 6 + 7 = 13
// 6 * 7 = 42

If I add SUB, the compiler forces me to implement apply. With a switch spread somewhere else in the codebase, I'd have to remember to find it.

On ordinals: JPA's @Enumerated defaults to ORDINAL. Someone adds PENDING at the top of OrderStatus, every existing 0 in the database now means PENDING instead of CREATED, and nothing fails. The data is just wrong. I've seen this in a code review and caught it before it shipped.

@Enumerated(EnumType.STRING)       // store "SHIPPED", not 2
private OrderStatus status;

So in practice I persist enums by name() or by an explicit code field, and I use ordinal() only inside things like EnumMap, which is what it was designed for.

Interviewers look for: the JPA EnumType.ORDINAL default as a concrete production risk.

AdvancedHow do records work with pattern matching in Java 21?

So Java 21 finalised record patterns and pattern matching for switch. A record pattern takes a record apart into its components right in the case label, and patterns can be nested. Combined with a sealed interface, the switch is checked for exhaustiveness, so no default is needed.

Let me show you with a quick example.

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Rect {}
record Point(int x, int y) {}
record Circle(Point center, int r) implements Shape {}
record Rect(Point topLeft, Point bottomRight) implements Shape {}

static String describe(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {
        case Circle(Point(var x, var y), var r) when r == 0 -> "dot at " + x + "," + y;
        case Circle(Point c, int r) -> "circle r=" + r + " at " + c;
        case Rect(Point(int x1, int y1), Point(int x2, int y2)) ->
            "rect " + (x2 - x1) + "x" + (y2 - y1);
    };
}
describe(new Circle(new Point(1, 2), 0));                    // dot at 1,2
describe(new Circle(new Point(0, 0), 5));                    // circle r=5 at Point[x=0, y=0]
describe(new Rect(new Point(0, 0), new Point(4, 3)));        // rect 4x3

Walking through it: the first case matches a circle, pulls out the nested point's coordinates, and uses a when guard. Case order matters, since the guarded case must come before the general Circle case, and the compiler rejects a case that an earlier one fully dominates. var works inside patterns.

This is Java's version of algebraic data types. Sealed interfaces give me "one of these", records give me "all of these fields", and the switch handles each shape of data. In a payments service, I modelled PaymentResult as Approved | Declined | NeedsReview this way, and adding a fourth outcome turned every unhandled switch into a compile error.

So in practice this is the modern alternative to the Visitor pattern for closed hierarchies. It's less code, and the logic stays in one readable place.

16. static, final & Immutability

BasicWhat's the difference between static and instance members?

So a static member belongs to the class: there's one copy, shared by every object, and it exists without any object. An instance member belongs to each object, with a separate copy per new. Static methods have no this, so they can't touch instance fields directly.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Visitor {
    static int total = 0;     // one copy for the class
    int mine = 0;             // one copy per object
    Visitor() { total++; mine++; }
}
new Visitor(); new Visitor(); Visitor c = new Visitor();
Visitor.total;   // 3
c.mine;          // 1

Visitor nothing = null;
nothing.total;   // 3 -- no NullPointerException: static access ignores the reference

That last line is a classic trick question. Static field access through an instance compiles to Visitor.total, so the null is never dereferenced.

And the most common compile error for beginners: calling an instance member from main.

public class StaticCtx {
    int count;
    public static void main(String[] args) { count++; }
}
// error: non-static variable count cannot be referenced from a static context

Static fields are also shared across threads, so a mutable static counter like total isn't thread-safe. Two threads doing total++ can lose updates.

So in practice I use static for constants, pure utility methods and factory methods. I avoid mutable static state, because it's global, hard to test, not thread-safe by default, and a common source of memory leaks when it's a growing collection.

BasicWhat does final mean on a variable, a method and a class?

So final means "can't be changed after this point", applied at three levels. A final variable can be assigned only once. A final method can't be overridden. A final class can't be extended.

OnMeaningExample
Local variable / parameterAssigned oncefinal int max = 10;
Instance fieldAssigned once, by the end of every constructorprivate final String id;
Static fieldAssigned once, in its declaration or a static blockstatic final int MAX;
MethodCan't be overriddenTemplate method in an abstract class
ClassCan't be extendedString, Integer, all records
static class Order {
    private final String id;              // "blank final": no initialiser
    Order(String id) { this.id = id; }
    Order() { this("GEN-1"); }            // still assigned exactly once via chaining
}
static final int MAX;
static { MAX = Integer.parseInt("100"); }
// new Order().id -> GEN-1, MAX -> 100

The compiler checks "definitely assigned exactly once". If one constructor path forgets to set id, or sets it twice, that's a compile error. That's a nice safety net for required fields.

Don't confuse it with finally, the block that always runs after try, or finalize(), the deprecated GC hook. They share a prefix and nothing else.

So in practice I make fields final by default, classes final unless designed for extension, and I don't bother marking every local variable final unless it helps readability.

IntermediateIf a reference is final, is the object immutable?

So no. final freezes the reference, not the object it points to. I can't make the variable point somewhere else, but I can freely change the object's contents through it.

Let me show you with a quick example.

final List<String> names = new ArrayList<>();
names.add("asha");            // fine: the object changes
names.add("ben");
System.out.println(names);    // [asha, ben]

names = new ArrayList<>();
// error: cannot assign a value to final variable names

This is the most common misunderstanding of final I see in code reviews: public static final List<String> ALLOWED = new ArrayList<>(); treated as a constant. Any code anywhere can call ALLOWED.add(...). A real constant needs both a final reference and an unmodifiable object:

public static final List<String> ALLOWED = List.of("admin", "editor");   // Java 9+

The same applies to arrays. static final int[] CODES can have its elements overwritten, which is why security guidelines say never to expose a public final array.

So in practice, "final" answers "can this variable be reassigned?" and "immutable" answers "can this object change?". For a real constant I need both.

Interviewers look for: the reference-vs-object distinction, and the public static final List "constant" trap.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between final and effectively final?

So a final variable is declared with the keyword. An effectively final variable is one without the keyword that's simply never reassigned after initialisation. Since Java 8 the compiler treats both the same for lambdas, anonymous and local classes: either kind can be captured.

Let me show you with a quick example.

String greeting = "hi";                        // no 'final', never reassigned
Runnable g = () -> System.out.println(greeting);
g.run();                                       // hi  -- effectively final, allowed

int count = 0;
Runnable r = () -> System.out.println(count);
count++;                                       // this line makes count NOT effectively final
// error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression must be final or effectively final

Notice that the error points at the lambda, even though the "problem" is the count++ after it. The compiler looks at the whole method: any reassignment anywhere, before or after the lambda, disqualifies the variable.

Before Java 8, anonymous classes required the explicit final keyword, so code was full of final parameters just to satisfy the compiler. Effectively final removed that noise without changing the rule.

A small detail: effectively final is also what try-with-resources accepts since Java 9. You can write try (existingStream) { ... } if the variable is effectively final.

So in practice I rarely write final on locals now. The compiler tracks it, and if a lambda needs a variable I'm reassigning, I copy it into a new variable first: var snapshot = count;.

IntermediateHow do you make a class immutable? Write one.

So there are five rules. Make the class final. Make all fields private final. Provide no setters. Copy mutable objects on the way in. Never hand out a mutable internal object on the way out. Changes are expressed as methods that return a new instance.

Let me show you with a quick example, deliberately using a mutable Date and a List to show the tricky parts.

public final class Booking {                                   // 1. can't be subclassed
    private final String guest;                                // 2. private final
    private final Date checkIn;
    private final List<String> rooms;

    public Booking(String guest, Date checkIn, List<String> rooms) {
        this.guest = Objects.requireNonNull(guest);
        this.checkIn = new Date(checkIn.getTime());            // 4. copy in
        this.rooms = List.copyOf(rooms);                       //    unmodifiable copy
    }
    public String guest() { return guest; }                    // 3. no setters
    public Date checkIn() { return new Date(checkIn.getTime()); }  // 5. copy out
    public List<String> rooms() { return rooms; }              //    already unmodifiable
    public Booking withGuest(String g) { return new Booking(g, checkIn, rooms); }  // "wither"
}

Date d = new Date(0);
List<String> rooms = new ArrayList<>(List.of("101"));
Booking b = new Booking("Asha", d, rooms);
d.setTime(999_999_999L);           // caller mutates its own Date
rooms.add("102");                  // and its own list
b.checkIn().setTime(123L);         // and the returned copy
b.checkIn().getTime() + " " + b.rooms();   // 0 [101]  -- Booking unaffected

Why final on the class? Otherwise a subclass could add mutable state or override guest() to return something different, and code that trusts Booking to be immutable would be wrong.

In modern code I'd use Instant or LocalDate instead of Date. They're immutable, so no copies are needed. And a record handles rules 1 to 3 for me, so the compact constructor only has to do the List.copyOf.

So in practice immutable objects are my default for value types: they're thread-safe without locks, safe as map keys, and easy to reason about.

Interviewers look for: defensive copying in both directions, and why the class itself must be final. Just "private final fields, no setters" isn't a complete answer.

IntermediateWhen exactly does a static block run? Predict the output.

So a static block runs once, when the class is initialised. That happens on first active use: creating an instance, calling a static method, or reading or writing a static field that isn't a compile-time constant. Just loading or referencing the class isn't enough.
