Let me show you with a quick example.

static class Holder {
    static { System.out.println("Holder loaded"); }
    static final int CONSTANT = 10;     // compile-time constant
    static int counter = 5;
    static void hello() { System.out.println("hello"); }
}

System.out.println("start");
System.out.println(Holder.CONSTANT);
System.out.println(Holder.counter);
Holder.hello();
start
10
Holder loaded
5
hello

Walking through it: Holder.CONSTANT is a static final primitive with a constant value, so javac copies 10 straight into the calling code. The class is never touched, and the static block doesn't run. Holder.counter is a real field read, so it triggers initialisation, and "Holder loaded" prints just before 5. By the time we call hello(), the class is already initialised.

That constant inlining has a real-world side effect: if you change a public static final int in a library and don't recompile the code that uses it, callers keep the old value.

This lazy initialisation is what powers the initialisation-on-demand holder idiom, a thread-safe lazy singleton with no locks. The JVM guarantees class initialisation happens once, under a lock.

So in practice I keep static blocks tiny, and I remember that constants don't trigger them.

AdvancedWhy are immutable objects thread-safe? What does final guarantee in the Java Memory Model?

So immutable objects are thread-safe because there are no writes after construction, and data races need at least one write. But there's a subtle part: another thread must also see the fully constructed values, not defaults. The JMM's final field rule guarantees exactly that. Once a constructor finishes, any thread that sees a reference to the object sees the correct values of its final fields, even without synchronisation.

class Config {                 // NOT safe without final or proper publication
    int port;
    Config() { port = 8080; }
}
static Config shared;          // thread A: shared = new Config();
// thread B may see shared != null but shared.port == 0 (reordered writes)

final class Config {           // safe
    private final int port;
    Config() { port = 8080; }
}
// any thread that sees the reference sees port == 8080

Walking through the broken version: the JIT or CPU can reorder "write port" and "publish the reference", so thread B may get the reference before the field write is visible. With final, the JMM puts a freeze at the end of the constructor, so final fields are visible before the reference can be.

Two conditions for this guarantee. First, this must not escape during construction, for example by registering a listener in the constructor. Second, the guarantee covers the final field and everything reachable through it as of the end of the constructor, so later changes to a referenced mutable list are not covered.

That's why String is safe to share even through a data race, and why immutable objects don't need volatile or locks. Only the reference to the current snapshot does, if you swap it: I usually hold it in an AtomicReference or a volatile field.

So in practice my concurrency pattern of choice is immutable snapshots plus an atomic swap of the reference. It's much simpler than locking fine-grained mutable state.

Interviewers look for: safe publication and the final field freeze, plus the "don't let this escape the constructor" condition.

Part 3 — Core APIs

17. Exceptions

BasicWhat's the difference between checked and unchecked exceptions? When would you use each?

So a checked exception is anything that extends Exception but not RuntimeException, and the compiler forces the caller to either catch it or declare it with throws. An unchecked exception extends RuntimeException (or Error), and the compiler doesn't make you handle it.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Checked: the caller must deal with it
String read(Path p) throws IOException {
    return Files.readString(p);
}

// Unchecked: signals a bug in the caller
void setAge(int age) {
    if (age < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("age must be >= 0: " + age);
    this.age = age;
}

The rule I follow is about whether the caller can realistically recover. A missing file, a network timeout, a failed payment: those are expected conditions a caller might retry or work around, so a checked exception documents that in the signature. A null argument, an index out of bounds, an illegal state: those are programming errors. No caller should "handle" them; the code should be fixed. Those are unchecked.

In practice, most modern code and frameworks lean unchecked. Spring wraps SQLException into its unchecked DataAccessException, and checked exceptions don't play well with lambdas, because Function can't throw them. So in practice I use checked exceptions sparingly, at boundaries where recovery is a real option, and unchecked everywhere else.

Interviewers look for: the "can the caller recover?" rule, not just the class hierarchy. Mentioning the lambda friction is a nice senior touch.

Basicfinal vs finally vs finalize?

So these three just share a spelling; they're unrelated. final is a keyword that stops reassignment, overriding or subclassing. finally is a block that runs after try/catch no matter how they exit. finalize() was a method on Object the GC might call before reclaiming an object, and it's deprecated.

Let me show you with a quick example.

final int max = 10;              // variable: can't be reassigned
final class Money { }            // class: can't be extended
public final void audit() { }    // method: can't be overridden

Connection c = pool.get();
try {
    use(c);
} finally {
    c.close();                   // runs on success, exception, or return
}

@Override
protected void finalize() { }    // deprecated since Java 9, marked for removal in 18

finalize was a bad idea from the start: there's no guarantee it ever runs, it runs on an unpredictable GC thread, it slows collection, and it can even resurrect the object. JEP 421 in Java 18 deprecated it for removal.

So in practice, for cleanup I use try-with-resources, and if I really need a safety net for native resources I use java.lang.ref.Cleaner (Java 9). I never write finalize.

Interviewers look for: knowing finalize is deprecated and naming the replacement: try-with-resources, and Cleaner as a backstop.

BasicWhat's the difference between Error and Exception?

So both extend Throwable, but an Error means the JVM itself is in trouble, like OutOfMemoryError or StackOverflowError, while an Exception is a problem in the application that code can reasonably catch.

Let me show you the hierarchy.

Throwable
├── Error                     (unchecked; don't catch)
│   ├── OutOfMemoryError
│   ├── StackOverflowError
│   └── NoClassDefFoundError
└── Exception                 (checked, unless a RuntimeException)
    ├── IOException, SQLException, InterruptedException ...
    └── RuntimeException      (unchecked)
        ├── NullPointerException
        ├── IllegalArgumentException
        └── IllegalStateException

The practical point is that I never write catch (Throwable t) or catch (Error e) in business code. If the heap is exhausted, there's usually nothing sensible to do, and catching it can leave the app half-working, which is worse than crashing and restarting. The one place catching Throwable makes sense is the very top of a thread or a framework boundary, where I log it and let the process or task die cleanly.

A classic interview trap: StackOverflowError from infinite recursion is an Error, not an exception, so a catch (Exception e) won't see it.

So in practice I catch specific exceptions, and treat Errors as "restart the JVM and investigate".

IntermediateDoes a finally block always run?

So almost always. It runs whether the try completes normally, throws, returns, or hits break/continue. But there are a few cases where it doesn't: System.exit(), the JVM crashing or being killed, and the thread never actually leaving the try.

Let me show you the most common one.

public static void main(String[] args) {
    try {
        System.out.println("in try");
        System.exit(0);
    } finally {
        System.out.println("finally");
    }
}
// prints only: in try

When I ran this, "finally" never printed. System.exit starts JVM shutdown; the current thread never returns from the call, so the finally is never reached. Shutdown hooks do run, which is where you'd put last-chance cleanup.

The full list I'd give:

  • System.exit() or Runtime.halt() in the try or catch.
  • kill -9, power loss, or a native JVM crash.
  • An infinite loop or a deadlock inside the try, since it never exits.
  • A daemon thread still in its try when the JVM exits.

So in practice I rely on finally (or try-with-resources) for releasing locks and connections, but never for anything that must survive a hard kill. That needs a transaction or an idempotent retry.

Interviewers look for: System.exit as the headline case, plus daemon threads. That shows you know how the JVM shuts down.

IntermediatePredict the output: return in both try and finally. 
static int a() { try { return 1; } finally { return 2; } }

static int b() {
    int x = 1;
    try { return x; } finally { x = 99; }
}

static StringBuilder c() {
    StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder("try");
    try { return sb; } finally { sb.append("+finally"); }
}

static int d() {
    try { throw new RuntimeException("boom"); } finally { return 3; }
}

So the outputs are 2, 1, try+finally, and 3. I ran all four to confirm.

Here's what happens. When the try hits return, the JVM evaluates the value and stashes it, then runs finally.

  • a(): finally has its own return 2, which replaces the stashed 1.
  • b(): the stashed value is the int 1. Changing x afterwards doesn't touch the copy, so it returns 1.
  • c(): the stashed value is a reference. Finally mutates the same object, so the caller sees the change.
  • d(): the worst one. The return in finally silently swallows the exception. "boom" is gone, no stack trace, nothing.

That last case is why javac -Xlint warns "finally clause cannot complete normally". So in practice I never return, throw, or break out of a finally. It should only clean up.

Interviewers look for: the "value is captured before finally runs" explanation, and spotting that d() loses the exception.

IntermediateHow does try-with-resources work, and what are suppressed exceptions?

So try-with-resources (Java 7) automatically calls close() on anything that implements AutoCloseable, in reverse order of opening, even if the body throws. And if the body throws and close throws too, the body's exception wins and the close exception is attached to it as a "suppressed" exception instead of being lost.

Let me show you with a quick example.

record Res(String name) implements AutoCloseable {
    Res { System.out.println("open " + name); }
    public void close() {
        System.out.println("close " + name);
        throw new IllegalStateException("close failed: " + name);
    }
}

try (Res a = new Res("A"); Res b = new Res("B")) {
    System.out.println("body");
    throw new RuntimeException("body failed");
} catch (RuntimeException e) {
    System.out.println("caught: " + e.getMessage());
    for (Throwable s : e.getSuppressed())
        System.out.println("suppressed: " + s.getMessage());
}
open A
open B
body
close B
close A
caught: body failed
suppressed: close failed: B
suppressed: close failed: A

Notice B closes before A, and both close failures are still reachable via getSuppressed(). With an old hand-written try/finally, a throwing close() would have replaced the real error, and I'd be debugging "close failed" when the actual problem was in the body.

Since Java 9 I can also use an effectively final variable declared outside: try (conn) { … }. So in practice every stream, connection, lock wrapper and executor (ExecutorService is AutoCloseable since Java 19) goes in a try-with-resources.

Interviewers look for: reverse close order and the suppressed-exception mechanism. Most candidates only say "it closes things".

AdvancedHow do you design custom exceptions? What are the best practices?

So I create a custom exception when the caller needs to react to that specific failure differently, or when I want to carry domain data like an order id. I name it after the problem, extend RuntimeException by default, always offer a constructor that takes a cause, and never lose the original exception.

Let me show you with a quick example.

public class OrderNotFoundException extends RuntimeException {
    private final long orderId;

    public OrderNotFoundException(long orderId, Throwable cause) {
        super("Order " + orderId + " not found", cause);
        this.orderId = orderId;
    }
    public long orderId() { return orderId; }
}

try {
    repo.load(id);
} catch (SQLException e) {
    throw new OrderNotFoundException(id, e);   // translate, keep the cause
}
// message: Order 42 not found | cause: java.sql.SQLException: no rows

The practices I stick to:

  • Chain the cause. throw new X("msg") inside a catch throws away the real stack trace. That's the number one mistake I see in code reviews.
  • Translate at layer boundaries. The service shouldn't leak SQLException to the controller.
  • Useful message with the ids involved, and no secrets or passwords.
  • Small hierarchy. One base like PaymentException with a few subclasses, not fifty classes.
  • Don't log and rethrow. Either handle it or throw it; doing both prints the same trace three times.
  • Don't use exceptions for control flow. Filling in a stack trace is expensive.

In my last project we mapped a small set of these to HTTP codes in one @ControllerAdvice, so OrderNotFoundException became a 404 without any try/catch in the controllers.

Interviewers look for: cause chaining and "log or rethrow, not both". Those two separate people who've debugged production from people who haven't.

18. Generics

BasicWhy do we need generics? What problem do they solve?

So generics (Java 5) move type errors from runtime to compile time. Before them, a List held Object, you cast everything coming out, and a wrong element only blew up with a ClassCastException far away from where it was added.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Before Java 5
List names = new ArrayList();
names.add("Asha");
names.add(42);                     // compiles fine
String s = (String) names.get(1);  // ClassCastException at runtime

// With generics
List<String> names2 = new ArrayList<>();
names2.add("Asha");
names2.add(42);                    // compile error
String t = names2.get(0);          // no cast needed

So I get three things: type safety, no casts, and reusable code. I can write one Box<T> or one <T> T firstOrDefault(List<T>, T) and it works for any type while staying safe.

One limitation to mention up front: type parameters must be reference types, so it's List<Integer>, never List<int>, and you pay for boxing. That's why the streams API has IntStream.

So in practice I use generics everywhere I'd otherwise write Object, and I treat any unchecked-cast warning as a bug until proven otherwise.

IntermediateWhat is type erasure? What can't you do because of it?

So type erasure means generic type information exists only at compile time. The compiler checks the types, inserts casts, and then erases T to its bound (usually Object). At runtime a List<String> and a List<Integer> are the exact same class.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<String> s = new ArrayList<>();
List<Integer> i = new ArrayList<>();
System.out.println(s.getClass() == i.getClass());  // true
System.out.println(s.getClass().getName());        // java.util.ArrayList

Java did it this way for backward compatibility: generic code in Java 5 had to run alongside pre-generics bytecode and libraries.

The consequences, which are the real interview answer:

  • No new T() and no new T[10]. The runtime doesn't know what T is.
  • No obj instanceof List<String>; only instanceof List<?>.
  • No overloading on type arguments: void f(List<String>) and void f(List<Integer>) is a "name clash, same erasure" compile error.
  • No primitives as type arguments.
  • A static field can't use the class's T, since there's only one class.

The standard workaround is to pass a Class<T> token, which is exactly why Jackson asks for readValue(json, User.class), and a TypeReference for List<User>. So in practice, when I need the type at runtime, I pass it in explicitly.

Interviewers look for: "compile-time only, for backward compatibility", plus two or three concrete consequences.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between List<Object>, List<?> and a raw List?

So List<Object> is a list that holds anything, but you can't assign a List<String> to it. List<?> is "a list of some specific type I don't know": you can pass any list in, read elements as Object, but you can't add anything except null. A raw List turns type checking off entirely.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<String> names = new ArrayList<>();

List<Object> objs = names;   // compile error: generics are invariant
List<?> any = names;         // fine
any.add("x");                // compile error: unknown type
Object first = any.get(0);   // reading is fine

List raw = names;            // compiles, with only a warning
raw.add(42);                 // heap pollution: an Integer in a List<String>
String s = names.get(0);     // ClassCastException, far from the bug

When I ran the raw-type part, names.size() was 1 and the read threw ClassCastException. The compiler's hidden cast was the one that failed, on a line that looks perfectly safe.

The reason List<Object> can't accept a List<String>: if it could, I could then add an Integer through the List<Object> reference and corrupt the string list.

So in practice: List<?> for methods that only read, like printing or counting; List<Object> when I genuinely store mixed types; raw types never, except in legacy code.

Interviewers look for: the word "invariant", and the heap pollution example for raw types.

IntermediateExplain PECS. When do you use extends and when super?

So PECS stands for "Producer Extends, Consumer Super". If a parameter produces values that my method reads, I use ? extends T. If it consumes values that my method writes into it, I use ? super T. If it does both, I use plain T.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Producer: I only read Numbers out of it
static double sum(List<? extends Number> nums) {
    double s = 0;
    for (Number n : nums) s += n.doubleValue();
    return s;
}

// Consumer: I only put Integers into it
static void fill(List<? super Integer> out) {
    for (int i = 1; i <= 3; i++) out.add(i);
}

sum(List.of(1, 2.5, 3L));        // 6.5, works for Integer, Double, Long
fill(new ArrayList<Number>());   // [1, 2, 3]
fill(new ArrayList<Object>());   // [1, 2, 3]

Why the restrictions? With ? extends Number the list might really be a List<Double>, so adding an Integer would be unsafe; I can only read. With ? super Integer it might be a List<Object>, so I can safely add Integers but only read back Object.

The JDK's own example is Collections.copy(List<? super T> dest, List<? extends T> src): source produces, destination consumes.

So in practice I apply PECS to public API parameters so callers aren't forced into exact types. I don't use wildcards in return types, because that pushes the pain onto every caller.

Interviewers look for: the Collections.copy signature, and "no wildcards in return types".

AdvancedWhy can't you create a generic array like new T[] or new List<String>[10]?

So it's because arrays and generics follow opposite rules. Arrays are covariant and reified: a String[] is an Object[], and the array checks every store at runtime. Generics are invariant and erased: the runtime has no idea what T is. Mixing the two would let the array's runtime check pass things it should reject.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Arrays check at runtime
Object[] arr = new String[2];
arr[0] = 1;          // ArrayStoreException: java.lang.Integer

// If generic arrays were allowed:
List<String>[] lists = new List<String>[1];   // compile error, and here's why
Object[] o = lists;
o[0] = List.of(42);          // runtime check only sees "List", passes
String s = lists[0].get(0);  // ClassCastException, with no warning anywhere

The array can only check "is this a List?", because <String> was erased. So the type system would have a hole with no compiler warning. Java refuses to create such arrays to keep that guarantee.

Workarounds I actually use:

  • Just use List<List<String>>. Almost always the right answer.
  • Inside a generic class, (T[]) new Object[n] with @SuppressWarnings("unchecked"), kept private. That's what ArrayList does internally.
  • Array.newInstance(clazz, n) when I have a Class<T>.

So in practice I prefer lists over arrays in any generic code.

Interviewers look for: "covariant and reified vs invariant and erased", and the ArrayStoreException example.

AdvancedWhat does <T extends Comparable<? super T>> mean, and why not just <T extends Comparable<T>>?

So it means "T must be comparable to itself or to one of its supertypes". The ? super T part matters when the compareTo is inherited from a parent class instead of being declared for T itself.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static <T extends Comparable<? super T>> T max(List<? extends T> list) {
    T best = list.get(0);
    for (T t : list) if (t.compareTo(best) > 0) best = t;
    return best;
}

LocalDate d = max(List.of(LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 1), LocalDate.of(2025, 6, 1)));
// 2025-06-01

Here's the catch: LocalDate does not implement Comparable<LocalDate>. It implements Comparable<ChronoLocalDate>, its interface. With the stricter bound <T extends Comparable<T>>, this call wouldn't compile. With ? super T, it works, because ChronoLocalDate is a supertype of LocalDate.

The same thing happens in my own code: if Employee implements Comparable<Employee> and Manager extends Employee, then Manager is only Comparable<Employee>. Sorting a List<Manager> needs the super version.

It's also just PECS again: a Comparable consumes T values in compareTo, so it gets super. That's why Collections.sort is declared as <T extends Comparable<? super T>> void sort(List<T> list).

So in practice, whenever I write a generic utility that compares T, I copy that bound from the JDK.

19. The Collections Framework

BasicWalk me through the Collections hierarchy. When do you pick a List, Set, Queue or Map?

So there are two roots. Collection has List, Set and Queue under it, and Map is separate because it stores key-value pairs rather than single elements.

Let me show you how I choose.

InterfaceKey propertyDefault choiceOthers
ListOrdered by index, duplicates allowedArrayListLinkedList (rarely), CopyOnWriteArrayList
SetNo duplicatesHashSetLinkedHashSet (insertion order), TreeSet (sorted)
Queue / DequeProcess in orderArrayDequePriorityQueue (by priority), BlockingQueue (threads)
MapUnique keys to valuesHashMapLinkedHashMap, TreeMap, ConcurrentHashMap

My decision process is: do I need lookup by key? Map. Do I need uniqueness? Set. Do I need position or duplicates? List. Do I process items first-in-first-out or by priority? Queue. Then I pick the implementation by ordering needs: none, insertion order, or sorted.

Java 21 added SequencedCollection and SequencedMap (JEP 431), so List, Deque, LinkedHashSet and LinkedHashMap now share getFirst(), getLast() and reversed(). No more list.get(list.size() - 1).

So in practice I declare variables as the interface (List<Order> orders = new ArrayList<>()) so I can swap implementations later, and I use ArrayDeque instead of the legacy Stack class.

Interviewers look for: Map not being a Collection, ArrayDeque over Stack, and bonus points for Java 21 sequenced collections.

BasicArrayList vs LinkedList. Which would you use?

So ArrayList is backed by a resizable array, and LinkedList is a doubly linked list of nodes. In theory LinkedList wins on inserts in the middle; in practice ArrayList wins almost every real workload, so it's my default.

Let me show you the complexities.

OperationArrayListLinkedList
get(i)O(1)O(n), walks from the nearer end
add at endAmortised O(1)O(1)
add/remove at frontO(n), shifts elementsO(1)
add/remove in middleO(n) shiftO(n) to find the spot, then O(1)
Memory per elementOne referenceNode object with 3 references + header

The part people miss: inserting in the middle of a LinkedList is only O(1) once you're there, and getting there is O(n). Meanwhile ArrayList's "O(n) shift" is a single System.arraycopy over contiguous memory, which the CPU cache loves. LinkedList nodes are scattered around the heap, so every hop is a potential cache miss, plus much more GC pressure.

ArrayList starts at capacity 10 on the first add and grows by 1.5x, so if I know the size I pass it to the constructor to avoid repeated copies.

So in practice I use ArrayList for lists, and if I need fast adds and removes at both ends I use ArrayDeque, not LinkedList. I honestly can't remember the last time LinkedList was the right answer.

Interviewers look for: cache locality and "O(1) insert only after an O(n) walk". Big-O alone isn't enough here.

IntermediateHow does HashSet work internally?

So a HashSet is just a HashMap in disguise. Every element you add becomes a key in an internal HashMap, and the value is a shared dummy object called PRESENT. So all the uniqueness comes from HashMap's key rules: hashCode to find the bucket, equals to check for a duplicate.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Simplified from the JDK source
public class HashSet<E> {
    private transient HashMap<E, Object> map;
    private static final Object PRESENT = new Object();

    public boolean add(E e) {
        return map.put(e, PRESENT) == null;   // null means the key was new
    }
}

Set<String> set = new HashSet<>();
set.add("a");   // true
set.add("a");   // false, already present
set.size();     // 1

That's why add returns a boolean: put returns the previous value, which is null only for a new key.

The same pattern repeats across the JDK: LinkedHashSet is backed by a LinkedHashMap, and TreeSet by a TreeMap. So a TreeSet uses compareTo for uniqueness, not equals, which can surprise people.

So in practice, everything I know about HashMap applies directly: O(1) average add and contains, one null allowed, no ordering, and elements must have correct equals and hashCode and must not be mutated while in the set.

Interviewers look for: "backed by a HashMap with a dummy value", and why add returns false for duplicates.

IntermediateHashMap vs Hashtable vs ConcurrentHashMap vs TreeMap vs LinkedHashMap?

So they differ on three axes: thread safety, ordering, and nulls. HashMap is the fast unordered default. LinkedHashMap keeps insertion (or access) order. TreeMap keeps keys sorted. ConcurrentHashMap is the thread-safe one. Hashtable is legacy and I'd never pick it for new code.

Let me show you with a table.

Orderget/putNull key / valueThread-safe
HashMapNoneO(1) avgOne null key, null valuesNo
LinkedHashMapInsertion or accessO(1) avgSame as HashMapNo
TreeMapSorted by keyO(log n)No null key (natural order); null values OKNo
HashtableNoneO(1) avgNeitherYes, one lock on every method
ConcurrentHashMapNoneO(1) avgNeitherYes, lock-free reads, per-bin locking
for (String k : List.of("banana", "apple", "cherry")) { lhm.put(k, 1); tm.put(k, 1); }
lhm.keySet();   // [banana, apple, cherry]  insertion order
tm.keySet();    // [apple, banana, cherry]  sorted

Why is Hashtable bad? Every method is synchronized on the whole table, so all threads queue up. ConcurrentHashMap (Java 8+) reads without locking, uses CAS to fill an empty bin, and only locks the single bin being written.

Why no nulls in ConcurrentHashMap? Because get(k) == null would be ambiguous: "absent" or "mapped to null"? In a single thread you could check containsKey, but between those two calls another thread could change things.

So in practice: HashMap by default, LinkedHashMap for predictable output or an LRU cache, TreeMap for range queries like floorKey and headMap, ConcurrentHashMap for shared state.

Interviewers look for: the reason CHM disallows nulls. It's the follow-up almost every time.

IntermediateWhat are the pitfalls of Arrays.asList?

So Arrays.asList doesn't copy anything. It returns a fixed-size view over the original array. That gives three traps: you can't add or remove, writes go straight through to the array (both ways), and passing a primitive array gives you a list with one element.

Let me show you with a quick example.

String[] arr = {"x", "y", "z"};
List<String> view = Arrays.asList(arr);

view.set(0, "CHANGED");
System.out.println(arr[0]);      // CHANGED   list writes into the array
arr[2] = "Z";
System.out.println(view);        // [CHANGED, y, Z]   and the other way round

view.add("w");                   // UnsupportedOperationException

int[] prim = {1, 2, 3};
Arrays.asList(prim).size();      // 1   it's a List<int[]>
Integer[] boxed = {1, 2, 3};
Arrays.asList(boxed).size();     // 3

The confusing part is that the returned class is also called ArrayList, but it's the private nested java.util.Arrays$ArrayList, not java.util.ArrayList. It supports set but inherits add and remove from AbstractList, which just throw.

The primitive case happens because the method is asList(T... a), and T can't be int, so the whole int[] becomes the single T.

So in practice, when I want a real mutable list I write new ArrayList<>(Arrays.asList(arr)), for a fixed list I use List.of, and for primitives Arrays.stream(prim).boxed().toList().

Interviewers look for: all three traps, especially the int[] one, which shows up in "what's wrong with this code" rounds.

IntermediateIs List.of immutable? How is it different from Collections.unmodifiableList?

So yes, List.of (Java 9) returns a truly immutable list: no add, remove or set, and it rejects nulls. Collections.unmodifiableList is different: it's a read-only view over another list, so if someone changes the original, you see the change.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<String> fixed = List.of("a", "b");
fixed.set(0, "z");          // UnsupportedOperationException
List.of("a", null);         // NullPointerException
fixed.contains(null);       // NullPointerException too

List<String> src = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a"));
List<String> unmod = Collections.unmodifiableList(src);
List<String> copy = List.copyOf(src);
src.add("b");
System.out.println(unmod + " " + copy);   // [a, b] [a]

So the unmodifiable view changed under me, while List.copyOf (Java 10) took a real snapshot. The contains(null) throwing surprises people; I've seen it break code that was migrated from Arrays.asList.

One more nuance: "immutable" is shallow. List.of(sb) with a StringBuilder inside still lets you mutate the builder. And Stream.toList() (Java 16) also returns an unmodifiable list, though that one does allow nulls.

So in practice I return List.copyOf(internal) from getters to protect my class's state, use List.of for constants, and remember that neither accepts nulls.

Interviewers look for: view vs copy, and the null behaviour. Mentioning shallow immutability is a bonus.

AdvancedBuild an LRU cache using the Collections framework.

So the quickest correct answer is LinkedHashMap in access order mode, plus overriding removeEldestEntry. LinkedHashMap already keeps a doubly linked list through its entries, and in access-order mode every get moves that entry to the end, so the head is always the least recently used.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class LruCache<K, V> extends LinkedHashMap<K, V> {
    private final int capacity;

    LruCache(int capacity) {
        super(16, 0.75f, true);             // true = access order
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }

    @Override
    protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<K, V> eldest) {
        return size() > capacity;           // called after every put
    }
}

LruCache<String, Integer> lru = new LruCache<>(2);
lru.put("a", 1);
lru.put("b", 2);
lru.get("a");          // "a" becomes most recent
lru.put("c", 3);       // evicts "b"
lru.keySet();          // [a, c]

Walking through it: after the two puts the order is a, b. The get("a") moves a to the end, giving b, a. Putting c makes the size 3, so removeEldestEntry returns true and b goes. Every operation is O(1).

If the interviewer says "without LinkedHashMap", I build the same thing by hand: a HashMap<K, Node> for lookup plus my own doubly linked list with dummy head and tail, moving a node to the tail on access and dropping from the head on overflow.

This one isn't thread-safe. For a shared cache in production I'd use Caffeine rather than wrapping this in synchronized.

Interviewers look for: the true access-order flag. Without it you've built a FIFO cache, not an LRU.

20. How HashMap Works Inside

BasicHow does HashMap work internally? Walk me through put and get.

So a HashMap is an array of buckets. On put it hashes the key, uses the hash to pick a bucket index, and stores a node with the key, value and hash there. On get it does the same hash, goes straight to that bucket, and uses equals to find the exact key. That's why it's O(1) on average.

Let me show you the steps for put(k, v).

// 1. Spread the hash so high bits affect the index
int h = k.hashCode();
int hash = h ^ (h >>> 16);

// 2. Pick a bucket (table length is always a power of two)
int index = (table.length - 1) & hash;     // same as hash % n, but faster

// 3. In that bucket:
//    empty                        -> place a new Node
//    a node with same hash and
//      equal key                  -> replace the value, return the old one
//    otherwise                    -> append to the bucket's list (or tree)

// 4. if (++size > threshold) resize();      // threshold = capacity * 0.75

The table is lazily created on the first put with capacity 16. A null key is allowed and always hashes to 0, so it lives in bucket 0.

Why the h ^ (h >>> 16) step? With a table of 16, only the low 4 bits of the hash choose the bucket. Keys whose hashCodes differ only in high bits would all collide. XOR-ing the top half into the bottom half mixes that information in cheaply.

So in practice HashMap is only as good as the key's hashCode and equals. With a good distribution each bucket holds zero or one node and both operations are constant time.

Interviewers look for: the order hashCode, then index, then equals. Candidates who mention the hash spreading and the power-of-two mask stand out.

IntermediateWhat happens on a collision?

So a collision is when two different keys land in the same bucket. HashMap handles it with chaining: the bucket holds a linked list of nodes, and a lookup walks that list comparing each node's hash first and then equals. Since Java 8, if one bucket gets too long it turns into a red-black tree.

Let me show you with a quick example.

"Aa".hashCode();   // 2112
"BB".hashCode();   // 2112   different strings, same hash

map.put("Aa", 1);
map.put("BB", 2);  // same bucket; "BB".equals("Aa") is false,
// so a second node is appended to the chain
map.get("BB");     // go to bucket, skip "Aa" (not equal), return 2

Two details that matter. First, on each node it compares the stored hash int before calling equals, because an int compare is much cheaper than a full equals on a long string. Second, Java 8 appends new nodes at the tail. Java 7 inserted at the head, and during a concurrent resize that could reverse links into a cycle, and get would spin at 100% CPU forever. That was a famous production bug when people shared a plain HashMap across threads.

The cost: a lookup becomes O(length of chain). With many collisions it degrades toward O(n), which is why Java 8 added treeification to cap it at O(log n).

So in practice collisions are normal and cheap in small numbers; what I worry about is a bad hashCode that sends everything to a few buckets.

IntermediateWhat is the load factor? When and how does a HashMap resize?

So the load factor (default 0.75) decides how full the table can get before it grows. The threshold is capacity times load factor. When the number of entries goes past the threshold, the table doubles and every entry is redistributed.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Default: capacity 16, threshold 16 * 0.75 = 12
Map<String, Integer> m = new HashMap<>();
// after 12 entries  -> table length 16
// the 13th entry    -> table length 32, new threshold 24

I confirmed this by reading the internal table through reflection: 16 after the 12th put, 32 after the 13th.

The resize trick in Java 8 is neat. Because the size doubles, each entry either stays at the same index or moves to index + oldCapacity, decided by a single bit of its hash. So each bucket splits into a "low" list and a "high" list without recomputing anything, and the relative order is preserved.

Why 0.75? It's the trade-off between space and time. A lower factor means fewer collisions but more wasted slots; a higher one saves memory but lengthens chains.

Resizing is O(n) and allocates a new array, so if I know I'm loading 10,000 entries I pre-size. Since Java 19 there's HashMap.newHashMap(10_000), which does the capacity math for me. Before that, new HashMap<>(10_000) was a classic mistake, because 10,000 is the capacity, not the number of entries it holds before resizing.

Interviewers look for: the 12 then 13 numbers, and the "same index or index + oldCap" split.

IntermediateWhy is String a good HashMap key? What makes a good key in general?

So String is ideal because it's immutable, it overrides equals and hashCode consistently, and it caches its hash code. Once a String is in a bucket, nothing can ever change the hash that put it there.

Let me show you what the JDK does.

// Simplified from java.lang.String
private int hash;          // 0 until first computed
private boolean hashIsZero;

public int hashCode() {
    int h = hash;
    if (h == 0 && !hashIsZero) {
        h = computeHash();     // s[0]*31^(n-1) + ... + s[n-1]
        if (h == 0) hashIsZero = true; else hash = h;
    }
    return h;
}

So the O(length) hash is paid once per String object, then every later lookup is a field read. That matters a lot for maps keyed by long ids.

My checklist for any key class:

  • Immutable, or at least the fields used in hashCode never change.
  • equals and hashCode overridden together and based on the same fields.
  • Well-distributed hash, so keys spread across buckets.
  • Ideally Comparable, which helps when a bucket turns into a tree.

Records (Java 16) tick most of these for free: final fields, generated equals and hashCode. So in practice my composite keys are records like record OrderKey(long customerId, LocalDate day), as long as the components are immutable too.

Interviewers look for: immutability and the cached hash. Mentioning records as keys is a modern touch.

IntermediateWhat's wrong with this code? (mutable key) 
class Point {
    int x, y;
    // equals and hashCode based on x and y
}

Map<Point, String> m = new HashMap<>();
Point p = new Point(1, 2);
m.put(p, "home");
p.x = 99;

m.get(p);                  // ?
m.get(new Point(1, 2));    // ?
m.containsKey(p);          // ?
m.size();                  // ?

So the key was mutated after it went into the map. The outputs are null, null, false, and 1. The entry is still in there, but I can't reach it with either the new or the old key.

Here's what happens. The node was stored in the bucket for hash(1, 2), and it kept that hash. Now get(p) computes hash(99, 2), goes to a different bucket, and finds nothing. get(new Point(1, 2)) goes to the right bucket and the stored hash matches, but equals compares against the mutated object, whose x is now 99, so it fails too. The entry is orphaned: it's a memory leak that also breaks containsKey and remove.

I've seen this in real code where an entity with a generated id was put into a HashSet before saving; the save set the id, the hashCode changed, and the set "lost" it.

The fixes: make the key immutable (a record with final fields), or base hashCode only on fields that never change. If I must change a key, I remove it, mutate, and put it back.

Interviewers look for: why the original key fails too. That's what proves you know the stored hash and the equals check both matter.

AdvancedWhen does a HashMap bucket turn into a tree? Why not always?

So since Java 8, a bucket's linked list is converted to a red-black tree when adding a node makes the chain longer than 8, which means on the 9th node, and the table has at least 64 slots. If the table is smaller than 64, HashMap resizes instead, because a long chain in a small table usually just means the table is too small.

Let me show you what I measured, with a key whose hashCode always returns 7 so every entry collides.

record Key(int id) implements Comparable<Key> {
    @Override public int hashCode() { return 7; }
    public int compareTo(Key o) { return Integer.compare(id, o.id); }
}

// new HashMap<>(64)
// entries 1..8  -> bucket is a plain Node chain
// entry 9       -> bucket becomes TreeNode

// new HashMap<>(16)
// entry 9       -> table resizes to 32, still a chain
// entry 10      -> table resizes to 64, still a chain
// entry 11      -> bucket becomes TreeNode

The constants are TREEIFY_THRESHOLD = 8, MIN_TREEIFY_CAPACITY = 64, and UNTREEIFY_THRESHOLD = 6: when a resize splits a tree bin down to 6 or fewer nodes, it goes back to a list.

Why not always use trees? A TreeNode is roughly twice the size of a Node, and for short chains a linear scan is faster. With a decent hash and load factor 0.75, bucket sizes follow a Poisson distribution, and the JDK comments put the chance of a chain reaching 8 at about 0.00000006. So treeification is really a defence against terrible hash codes, or against attackers deliberately sending colliding keys (hash-flooding a web server's parameter map), turning O(n) worst case into O(log n).

The tree orders nodes by hash, then by compareTo if the keys are Comparable, then by a tie-breaker. So Comparable keys get the full benefit.

Interviewers look for: the 64 capacity condition. Most people say only "8" and miss that a small table resizes instead.

AdvancedWhat happens if you override equals but not hashCode?

So hash-based collections break. Two objects that are "equal" get different identity hash codes, land in different buckets, and HashMap never even calls equals to compare them. You get duplicates in a Set and failed lookups in a Map.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class NoHash {
    int id;
    NoHash(int id) { this.id = id; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof NoHash n && n.id == id;
    }
    // no hashCode override
}

Set<NoHash> s = new HashSet<>();
s.add(new NoHash(1));
s.add(new NoHash(1));
s.size();                     // 2
s.contains(new NoHash(1));    // false

When I ran it, the set had two "equal" elements and contains returned false. That's because Object.hashCode is identity-based, so each instance gets its own value, and the lookup goes to a bucket where the stored object isn't.

The contract is one-directional: if a.equals(b), then a.hashCode() == b.hashCode() must hold. The reverse isn't required; collisions are allowed.

The opposite mistake, overriding hashCode but not equals, is also broken: objects land in the same bucket but equals falls back to ==, so you still get duplicates.

So in practice I never write these by hand. I use a record, or let the IDE generate both from the same fields, or Objects.hash(x, y). And I don't use Lombok's @Data on JPA entities, because a hashCode based on a mutable id causes exactly the mutable-key bug.

Interviewers look for: "HashMap checks the hash before equals, so equals never gets called". That's the mechanism, not just the rule.

21. Comparable, Comparator & Sorting

BasicComparable vs Comparator. When do you use which?

So Comparable defines a class's one natural ordering, implemented inside the class with compareTo. Comparator is an external strategy object with compare(a, b), so I can have as many orderings as I want without touching the class.

Let me show you with a quick example.

record Emp(String name, String dept, int salary) implements Comparable<Emp> {
    public int compareTo(Emp o) { return name.compareTo(o.name); }   // natural order
}

Collections.sort(emps);        // uses compareTo
// [Asha, Kabir, Meera, Ravi]

emps.sort(Comparator.comparing(Emp::salary));   // a different order, no class change
ComparableComparator
Packagejava.langjava.util
MethodcompareTo(T o)compare(T a, T b)
WhereInside the classOutside, as a lambda or object
How manyOne natural orderAny number
Used by defaultTreeMap, TreeSet, Collections.sort(list)When passed explicitly

I implement Comparable when there's one obvious order that everyone would agree on: numbers, dates, a version string. I use a Comparator for everything else, and whenever I can't modify the class, like a third-party DTO.

So in practice most of my sorting is Comparators built with Comparator.comparing, because business code usually sorts the same objects several different ways.

IntermediateSort employees by department, then salary descending, then name.

So I'd chain Comparator factory methods from Java 8: comparing for the first key, thenComparing for each tie-breaker, and reverseOrder() on just the salary key.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<Emp> emps = new ArrayList<>(List.of(
        new Emp("Ravi", "Eng", 120), new Emp("Asha", "Ops", 90),
        new Emp("Meera", "Eng", 150), new Emp("Kabir", "Ops", 90)));

emps.sort(Comparator.comparing(Emp::dept)
    .thenComparing(Emp::salary, Comparator.reverseOrder())
    .thenComparing(Emp::name));

// [Eng:Meera, Eng:Ravi, Ops:Asha, Ops:Kabir]

Walking through it: Eng comes before Ops. Within Eng, Meera's 150 beats Ravi's 120 because salary is reversed. Within Ops, Asha and Kabir both earn 90, so the name tie-breaker puts Asha first.

A common mistake is writing .thenComparing(Emp::salary).reversed(). reversed() applies to the whole chain built so far, so it would flip the department order too. Passing reverseOrder() as the key comparator reverses only that key.

Other helpers I use: comparingInt to avoid boxing, and nullsFirst/nullsLast for fields that can be null:

list.sort(Comparator.nullsFirst(Comparator.naturalOrder()));
// [b, null, a] -> [null, a, b]

So in practice I keep these comparators as constants, like Emp.BY_DEPT_THEN_PAY, so the same order is reused everywhere.

Interviewers look for: where reversed() applies. It's a common live-coding bug.

IntermediateWhat's wrong with this comparator: (a, b) -> a - b?

So it overflows. Subtracting two ints can wrap around past Integer.MAX_VALUE and flip the sign, so a very negative number can come out as "greater" than a positive one.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Comparator<Integer> bad = (a, b) -> a - b;
List<Integer> nums = new ArrayList<>(List.of(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 5, -3));

bad.compare(Integer.MIN_VALUE, 5);   // 2147483643  (positive!)
nums.sort(bad);
System.out.println(nums);            // [-3, 5, -2147483648]   wrong

nums.sort(Integer::compare);
System.out.println(nums);            // [-2147483648, -3, 5]   correct

MIN_VALUE - 5 should be hugely negative, but it wraps to 2147483643, so the comparator says MIN_VALUE is bigger than 5. The list ends up sorted wrongly, with no exception.

It's sneaky because it works in every unit test with small values, and then fails in production on ids, timestamps, or anything near the extremes. With a bigger list, an inconsistent comparator can even make TimSort throw "Comparison method violates its general contract!".

The fix is always Integer.compare(a, b), Long.compare, or Comparator.comparingInt(...). Same rule inside a compareTo.

So in practice I treat any subtraction inside compare as a code-review red flag.

Interviewers look for: naming overflow immediately and the Integer.compare fix.

AdvancedWhat does "consistent with equals" mean, and why does it matter for TreeSet?

So an ordering is consistent with equals when compare(a, b) == 0 exactly when a.equals(b). It matters because TreeSet and TreeMap never call equals. They decide "is this a duplicate?" purely by compareTo or the Comparator returning 0.

Let me show you with a quick example.

var a = new BigDecimal("1.0");
var b = new BigDecimal("1.00");
// a.equals(b)     -> false  (different scale)
// a.compareTo(b)  -> 0      (same value)

new HashSet<>(List.of(a, b)).size();   // 2
new TreeSet<>(List.of(a, b)).size();   // 1

Set<String> ci = new TreeSet<>(String.CASE_INSENSITIVE_ORDER);
ci.add("Java");
ci.add("JAVA");
System.out.println(ci);                // [Java]

Same two objects, different set sizes, depending on which method the collection trusts. Sometimes that's exactly what I want, like the case-insensitive set. But it bites when it's accidental.

The real bug I've seen: a TreeSet of employees with a comparator on salary only. Two different people with the same salary compared as 0, so the second one silently disappeared from the set, and a report was short by a few rows.

So in practice, when I write a comparator for a TreeSet or TreeMap, I always end the chain with a unique tie-breaker like the id, so that 0 really means "same element".

Interviewers look for: "TreeSet uses compareTo, not equals", and the BigDecimal example.

AdvancedWhich algorithm does Java use for sorting? Is it stable?

So it depends on what you're sorting. Arrays.sort on primitives uses Dual-Pivot Quicksort, which isn't stable. Sorting objects, whether Arrays.sort(Object[]), Collections.sort or List.sort, uses TimSort, which is stable and O(n log n) worst case.

Let me show you why stability matters.

// Already sorted by name: [Asha(Ops,90), Kabir(Ops,90), Meera(Eng,150), Ravi(Eng,120)]
emps.sort(Comparator.comparing(Emp::dept));
// Stable: within each dept, the name order survives
// [Meera, Ravi, Asha, Kabir]

Stability means equal elements keep their relative order. For objects that's important, because it lets you sort by one key after another and keep the earlier ordering. For primitives it doesn't matter: two equal ints are indistinguishable, so Java uses the faster, non-stable quicksort there.

TimSort is a hybrid of merge sort and insertion sort. It finds already-sorted "runs" in the data and merges them, so on nearly-sorted input it approaches O(n). It needs up to n/2 extra space. It also checks the comparator's contract, which is why a broken comparator can throw IllegalArgumentException: Comparison method violates its general contract!

Two extras: Arrays.parallelSort (Java 8) splits large arrays across the fork/join pool, and List.sort on an ArrayList sorts the backing array in place, while the default implementation copies to an array, sorts, and writes back.

So in practice I rely on stability for multi-key sorts, but I still prefer one explicit thenComparing chain because it's clearer.

22. Iterators, Fail-fast & Fail-safe

BasicWhat's the difference between Iterator, ListIterator and a for-each loop?

So a for-each loop over a collection is just syntax sugar: the compiler turns it into an Iterator with hasNext() and next(). Using the Iterator directly gives you one extra power, remove(). ListIterator, only for Lists, adds going backwards, the current index, and set and add while iterating.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<String> li = new ArrayList<>(List.of("x", "y"));
for (ListIterator<String> it = li.listIterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
    String v = it.next();
    it.set(v.toUpperCase());   // replace current element
    it.add(v + "!");           // insert after it
}
System.out.println(li);        // [X, x!, Y, y!]
for-eachIteratorListIterator
Works onArrays and any IterableAny CollectionLists only
Remove while loopingNo (CME)remove()remove()
Replace / insertNoNoset(), add()
DirectionForwardForwardBoth, with index

Any class that implements Iterable can be used in a for-each, which is how I make my own tree or range types loop-friendly.

So in practice I use for-each for reading, removeIf for filtering in place, and ListIterator only for the rare in-place edit or insert during a walk.

IntermediateFail-fast vs fail-safe iterators?

So a fail-fast iterator throws ConcurrentModificationException as soon as it notices the collection was structurally changed by anything other than itself. ArrayList, HashMap and most java.util collections work this way. A fail-safe iterator never throws: it either works on a snapshot (CopyOnWriteArrayList) or is "weakly consistent" (ConcurrentHashMap), tolerating changes during iteration.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Fail-fast
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3, 4, 5));
for (Integer n : list) if (n % 2 == 0) list.remove(n);
// ConcurrentModificationException

// Snapshot: the loop sees the original 3 elements only
List<Integer> cow = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(List.of(1, 2, 3));
for (Integer n : cow) cow.add(n * 10);
System.out.println(cow);    // [1, 2, 3, 10, 20, 30]

// Weakly consistent: removing while iterating is fine
Map<String, Integer> chm = new ConcurrentHashMap<>(Map.of("a", 1, "b", 2));
for (String k : chm.keySet()) chm.remove(k);
System.out.println(chm);    // {}
Fail-fastSnapshot (COW)Weakly consistent (CHM)
Throws CMEYesNoNo
Sees later changesn/aNoMaybe
CostCheapCopies array on every writeCheap
iterator.remove()YesUnsupportedOperationExceptionYes

So in practice, CopyOnWriteArrayList is great for read-heavy lists like event listeners, and terrible for write-heavy ones. ConcurrentHashMap is my general-purpose choice when threads iterate and modify.

Interviewers look for: that "fail-safe" really means two different mechanisms, snapshot and weakly consistent.

IntermediateYou get a ConcurrentModificationException removing items in a loop. How do you fix it?

So the CME comes from calling list.remove() while a for-each's hidden iterator is walking the list. The fix is to remove through the iterator itself, or better, use removeIf (Java 8), which does it safely and in one pass.

Let me show you the options.

// Broken
for (Integer n : list) if (n % 2 == 0) list.remove(n);   // CME

// Fix 1: Iterator.remove()
for (Iterator<Integer> it = a.iterator(); it.hasNext(); )
    if (it.next() % 2 == 0) it.remove();
// [1, 3, 5]

// Fix 2: removeIf (my default)
b.removeIf(n -> n % 2 == 0);
// [1, 3, 5]

// Maps too
hm.entrySet().removeIf(e -> e.getValue() > 1);

// Fix 3: build a new list
List<Integer> odds = list.stream().filter(n -> n % 2 != 0).toList();

I prefer removeIf because on an ArrayList it's O(n): it marks what to remove and compacts the array once. Calling it.remove() in a loop on an ArrayList shifts the tail every time, which is O(n²) for many removals.

Another old trick is a backwards index loop, for (int i = list.size() - 1; i >= 0; i--), which works because removing at i doesn't shift the indexes you haven't visited yet.

And importantly: if the CME appears with multiple threads, none of these fixes help. That's a thread-safety bug, and the fix is a concurrent collection or proper locking.

Interviewers look for: removeIf, and recognising that a CME in multi-threaded code means a concurrency problem, not a loop problem.

AdvancedPredict the output: removing the second-to-last element in a for-each. 
List<String> s = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
for (String x : s) {
    if (x.equals("b")) s.remove(x);
}
System.out.println(s);

So surprisingly, this doesn't throw. It prints [a, c], and I verified that. But "c" is never visited by the loop, so if the condition also needed to check "c", it would silently be skipped.

Here's what happens inside ArrayList's iterator. It keeps a cursor (the next index), and hasNext() is simply cursor != size. The modification check lives in next(), not in hasNext().

  • After reading "a", cursor = 1. After reading "b", cursor = 2.
  • Removing "b" shrinks size from 3 to 2.
  • hasNext() checks 2 != 2, which is false, so the loop ends.
  • next() is never called again, so the modCount check never runs.

Remove "a" instead and you get a CME, because the loop continues and calls next().

The deeper lesson is that fail-fast is best effort. The Javadoc says explicitly that you can't rely on the exception for correctness. It exists to catch bugs, not to guarantee detection. So in practice I never modify a collection inside a for-each, even when it "works".

Interviewers look for: the cursor and size explanation, and the phrase "best effort".

AdvancedHow is fail-fast actually implemented?

So every fail-fast collection has an int field called modCount that it increments on every structural change, meaning an add or remove, not a set. When you create an iterator, it copies that value into expectedModCount. Every next() compares the two, and if they differ, it throws.

Let me show you the simplified source.

// Inside ArrayList
protected transient int modCount = 0;   // inherited from AbstractList

public boolean add(E e) { modCount++; /* ... */ }

private class Itr implements Iterator<E> {
    int cursor;
    int expectedModCount = modCount;

    public E next() {
        if (modCount != expectedModCount)
            throw new ConcurrentModificationException();
        // ...
    }

    public void remove() {
        ArrayList.this.remove(lastRet);
        expectedModCount = modCount;     // stays in sync, so no CME
    }
}

That last method explains why iterator.remove() is safe: it updates the collection and then resyncs its own expected count.

Why is it best effort? modCount isn't volatile, and there's no locking. Another thread's increment may not be visible, or two increments could race. So across threads you might get the exception, corrupted data, or nothing at all. It's a debugging aid for single-threaded misuse, not a concurrency control.

Also notice set(i, x) doesn't bump modCount, so replacing elements during a for-each is allowed. Same for HashMap.put on an existing key: updating a value isn't structural, adding a new key is.

So in practice, knowing this helps me read a CME stack trace: the throw is in next(), but the bug is whatever modified the collection before it.

23. Optional

BasicWhat is Optional and why was it added?

So Optional<T> (Java 8) is a container that holds either one value or nothing. Its purpose is to make "this method might not return a result" part of the method signature, so the caller is forced to think about the empty case instead of getting surprised by a NullPointerException.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Before: the signature hides that null is possible
User findUser(String id);

// After: the signature says it
Optional<User> findUser(String id);

String city = findUser("1")
    .map(User::address)
    .map(Address::city)
    .orElse("unknown");      // "Pune"

findUser("2").map(User::address).map(Address::city).orElse("unknown");
// "unknown"

That chain replaces three nested null checks. If any step produces nothing, the rest are skipped and the default kicks in.

The ways to create one:

  • Optional.of(x): x must not be null, or it throws NPE immediately.
  • Optional.ofNullable(x): empty if x is null. This is the one for wrapping legacy code.
  • Optional.empty().

Brian Goetz has described it as intended for return types, for cases where "no result" is a legitimate answer. It wasn't designed as a general replacement for null everywhere.

So in practice I return Optional from finder methods like findById, which is exactly what Spring Data does, and I use plain values and validation everywhere else.

IntermediateorElse vs orElseGet. What's the difference?

So orElse(value) takes an already-computed value, which means its argument is always evaluated, even when the Optional has a value. orElseGet(supplier) takes a lambda that's only called when the Optional is empty.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static String expensiveDefault() {
    System.out.println("  computing default...");
    return "guest";
}

Optional<String> name = Optional.of("laeba");

name.orElse(expensiveDefault());          // prints "computing default..." then laeba
name.orElseGet(Opt::expensiveDefault);    // prints just laeba

When I ran it, orElse printed "computing default..." even though the value was present. That's just Java's normal rule: method arguments are evaluated before the call.

It sounds harmless until the default is a database query, a remote call, or something with side effects. I've seen repo.findByEmail(e).orElse(repo.save(new User(e))) in a code review; it inserted a new user on every call, even when one already existed, and then the unique constraint started failing.

The rule I follow: orElse for constants and cheap values like orElse("unknown") or orElse(0); orElseGet for anything that computes, allocates, or has side effects. For the "throw if missing" case, orElseThrow(() -> new NotFoundException(id)), or the no-arg orElseThrow() from Java 10.

Interviewers look for: "the argument is always evaluated", ideally with a side-effect example like the save bug.

Intermediatemap vs flatMap on Optional, and what else was added after Java 8?

So map takes a function that returns a plain value and wraps it for you. flatMap takes a function that already returns an Optional, and doesn't wrap it again. Use map with the wrong kind of function and you end up with Optional<Optional<T>>.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Optional<String> name = Optional.of("laeba");

name.map(n -> Optional.of(n.toUpperCase()));      // Optional[Optional[LAEBA]]
name.flatMap(n -> Optional.of(n.toUpperCase()));  // Optional[LAEBA]

// Java 9
findUser("2").ifPresentOrElse(
    u -> System.out.println(u.name()),
    () -> System.out.println("not found"));       // not found

findUser("2").or(() -> findUser("1"))             // fallback to another Optional
    .map(User::name);                            // Optional[Laeba]

ids.stream()
    .map(this::findUser)                         // Stream<Optional<User>>
    .flatMap(Optional::stream)                   // drop the empties
    .map(User::name)
    .toList();                                   // [Laeba]

// Java 10 / 11
findUser("2").orElseThrow();    // NoSuchElementException: No value present
findUser("2").isEmpty();        // true

The rule is the same as streams: if my function returns an Optional, like a repository lookup, I use flatMap.

The additions by version: Java 9 brought ifPresentOrElse, or and stream; Java 10 the no-arg orElseThrow(), which is the honest name for get(); Java 11 isEmpty(). Optional::stream is my favourite, because it cleanly turns "a list of lookups" into "the ones that were found".

So in practice I write chains of map, flatMap and filter, and finish with one terminal call.

IntermediateWhat are the common Optional anti-patterns?

So the main ones are: calling get() without checking, using isPresent() plus get() as a verbose null check, using Optional for fields and parameters, wrapping collections, and returning null from a method that returns Optional.

Let me show you the bad and good versions.

// 1. get() without a check: just an NPE with a different name
String n = findUser(id).get().name();
String n2 = findUser(id).map(User::name).orElseThrow(() -> new NotFound(id));

// 2. isPresent + get: a null check with extra steps
if (opt.isPresent()) { use(opt.get()); }
opt.ifPresent(this::use);

// 3. Optional field or parameter
class User { Optional<String> middleName; }        // not Serializable, extra object
void send(Optional<String> cc) { }                // caller can still pass null
// instead: a nullable field with an Optional getter, or overloads

// 4. Optional of a collection
Optional<List<Order>> orders();                     // two ways to say "none"
List<Order> orders();                               // return an empty list

// 5. Returning null from an Optional method
Optional<User> find() { return null; }              // defeats the whole point

A few reasons behind these. Optional isn't Serializable (it's a final class that doesn't implement it, so even instanceof Serializable won't compile), which breaks it as a JPA or DTO field. As a parameter it adds noise and a third state: present, empty, or null. And an empty list already means "nothing", so wrapping it just adds a second check.

Also avoid Optional<Integer> in hot code; OptionalInt skips the boxing.

So in practice: Optional as a return type, handled with map, orElse, orElseGet or orElseThrow, and almost never anywhere else.

Interviewers look for: "return types only" and at least three concrete anti-patterns with the fixes.

AdvancedRefactor this null-check pyramid using Optional. Would you always do that? 
String getCity(Order order) {
    if (order != null) {
        Customer c = order.getCustomer();
        if (c != null) {
            Address a = c.getAddress();
            if (a != null && a.getCity() != null) {
                return a.getCity().toUpperCase();
            }
        }
    }
    return "UNKNOWN";
}

So I'd flatten it into one chain, where each map quietly turns a null result into an empty Optional and short-circuits the rest.

String getCity(Order order) {
    return Optional.ofNullable(order)
        .map(Order::getCustomer)
        .map(Customer::getAddress)
        .map(Address::getCity)
        .map(String::toUpperCase)
        .orElse("UNKNOWN");
}

This works because map uses ofNullable on the function's result: if getCustomer() returns null, the chain becomes empty and every later step is skipped. So the behaviour is identical to the pyramid, with one exit point.

Would I always do it? No. The Optional allocations are usually trivial and escape analysis often removes them, but in a tight loop I'd keep plain null checks. More importantly, if my own code has this pyramid, the real fix is upstream: make getCustomer() never return null, validate at construction, or return Optional<Address> from the getter that can legitimately be empty. The chain is great for data I don't control, like DTOs from another service.

And in Java 21 I sometimes prefer pattern matching when I need to branch on the types rather than just default.

So in practice I use the Optional chain at the edges of the system and design the core so that nulls don't reach it.

Interviewers look for: a correct chain, plus the judgement that fixing the design beats wrapping every null.

Part 4 — Functional Java

24. Lambdas & Functional Interfaces

BasicWhat is a functional interface?

So a functional interface is any interface with exactly one abstract method. That single method is what makes it a target for a lambda or a method reference: the lambda body simply becomes the implementation of that method. It came with Java 8, together with lambdas.

Let me show you with a quick example.

@FunctionalInterface
interface Validator {
    boolean validate(String s);                    // the one abstract method

    default Validator and(Validator other) {       // default: doesn't count
        return s -> validate(s) && other.validate(s);
    }
    static Validator notEmpty() {                  // static: doesn't count
        return s -> !s.isEmpty();
    }
    boolean equals(Object o);                      // from Object: doesn't count
}

Validator v = Validator.notEmpty().and(s -> s.length() <= 5);
v.validate("java");     // true
v.validate("kotlin");   // false

The rule is about abstract methods only. Default methods, static methods, and abstract methods that just redeclare something public from Object like equals or toString don't count. That's why Comparator is a functional interface even though it declares equals and has dozens of default methods.

The @FunctionalInterface annotation is optional. Any single-abstract-method interface works with lambdas, including old ones like Runnable. But I always add it on my own interfaces, because then the compiler fails the build if someone later adds a second abstract method and silently breaks every lambda that uses it.

Interviewers look for: "exactly one abstract method", and knowing that default, static and Object methods don't count.

BasicWhich built-in functional interfaces do you use most, and what are they for?

So java.util.function has around 40 interfaces, but they're all variations of four shapes: something that takes and returns (Function), something that tests (Predicate), something that only takes (Consumer) and something that only gives (Supplier).

InterfaceMethodShapeTypical use
Function<T,R>applyT → Rmap
Predicate<T>testT → booleanfilter
Consumer<T>acceptT → voidforEach
Supplier<T>get() → TorElseGet, lazy values
UnaryOperator<T>applyT → TreplaceAll
BiFunction<T,U,R>apply(T, U) → RMap.merge, compute
BinaryOperator<T>apply(T, T) → Treduce

Then there are primitive versions like IntPredicate, ToIntFunction and IntBinaryOperator. They exist purely to avoid boxing: a Function<Integer,Integer> boxes every value, while IntUnaryOperator works on raw ints. On a hot path over millions of elements that difference is measurable.

They also compose, which is handy:

Function<Integer, Integer> plus2  = x -> x + 2;
Function<Integer, Integer> times3 = x -> x * 3;

plus2.andThen(times3).apply(4);   // 18  -> (4 + 2) * 3
plus2.compose(times3).apply(4);   // 14  -> (4 * 3) + 2

Predicate<String> isLong = s -> s.length() > 3;
isLong.negate().test("hi");       // true

So in practice I only write my own functional interface when none of the built-ins fit, usually because I need a checked exception or a more meaningful name in a public API.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between a lambda and an anonymous class?

So they look interchangeable, but they're different in three ways that matter: what this means, how they're compiled, and what they can do. A lambda is just a function; an anonymous class is a real class with its own scope, fields and identity.

Let me show you the this difference, because it's the one people get wrong.

public class Demo {
    String name = "outer";

    void run() {
        Runnable anon = new Runnable() {
            String name = "anon";
            public void run() { System.out.println("anonymous: " + this.name); }
        };
        Runnable lambda = () -> System.out.println("lambda: " + this.name);
        anon.run();
        lambda.run();
    }
}
// anonymous: anon
// lambda: outer

Inside an anonymous class, this is the anonymous object. Inside a lambda, this is the enclosing instance, because a lambda doesn't introduce a new scope. For the same reason a lambda can't declare a local variable that shadows one from the enclosing method, while an anonymous class can.

LambdaAnonymous class
TargetFunctional interfaces onlyAny interface or class, any number of methods
thisEnclosing instanceThe anonymous object
StateNo fieldsCan have fields
Compilationinvokedynamic, no extra .class fileA separate Outer$1.class

On compilation: an anonymous class creates a new object every time the expression runs. A non-capturing lambda is linked once via invokedynamic and the JVM typically reuses a single instance, so it's cheaper.

So in practice I use lambdas by default, and switch to an anonymous class only when I need state, more than one method, or to extend an abstract class.

Interviewers look for: the this difference. Mentioning invokedynamic is a strong bonus.

IntermediateWhat does "effectively final" mean, and why can't a lambda modify a local variable?

So "effectively final" means a local variable that's never reassigned after it's initialised, even though it isn't marked final. Lambdas and anonymous classes (since Java 8) can only capture local variables that are final or effectively final.

Let me show you the error.

int count = 0;
List<String> names = List.of("a", "b", "c");
names.forEach(n -> count++);
// error: local variables referenced from a lambda expression
//        must be final or effectively final

The reason is how capture works. A lambda doesn't get a reference to the variable; it gets a copy of its value. Local variables live on the stack of the method, and the lambda can outlive that method, for example if it's handed to another thread or stored in a field. If Java allowed changing count, you'd have two copies drifting apart, and on top of that a data race if the lambda runs on another thread. Forcing the variable to be effectively final makes the copy indistinguishable from the original.

Note that this only applies to local variables. Fields and array elements can be changed, because the lambda captures a reference to the object, not the value.

int[] box = {0};
names.forEach(n -> box[0]++);   // compiles, box[0] == 3, but it's a hack

long count2 = names.stream().count();   // the right way: 3

So in practice, when I feel the urge to mutate a counter inside a lambda, I take it as a sign I should be using a stream reduction like count, sum or a collector instead. The array or AtomicInteger trick works, but it's not thread-safe with parallel streams unless it's the atomic one.

Interviewers look for: "the lambda captures a copy of the value", and knowing that fields are not restricted.

IntermediateWhere do lambdas help in real code, and when would you avoid them?

So lambdas shine when behaviour is the parameter: sorting with a comparator, filtering, callbacks, strategy objects, lazy defaults. I avoid them when the body grows beyond a few lines or when a stack trace needs to be readable.

Let me give you a real example. In my last project we had a pricing service with a big switch over discount types. We replaced it with a map of strategies:

Map<String, UnaryOperator<BigDecimal>> discounts = Map.of(
    "NONE",    p -> p,
    "TEN_OFF", p -> p.multiply(new BigDecimal("0.90")),
    "FLAT_50", p -> p.subtract(new BigDecimal("50")).max(BigDecimal.ZERO)
);

BigDecimal finalPrice = discounts.getOrDefault(code, p -> p).apply(price);

Adding a new discount became one line, and each strategy was trivially unit-testable.

Where I'm careful:

  • Long lambdas: if it's more than three or four lines I extract a named private method and pass a method reference. The name documents intent, and the stack trace shows a real method name instead of lambda$main$0.
  • Checked exceptions: the built-in interfaces don't declare any, so I/O inside a lambda gets ugly.
  • Debugging: stepping through a long chained pipeline in a debugger is harder than through a loop.

So in practice: small, pure, stateless lambdas everywhere; anything with real logic gets a name.

AdvancedHow do you handle checked exceptions inside a lambda?

So the problem is that Function.apply doesn't declare any checked exceptions, so a lambda passed to map can't throw IOException. There are three real options: catch inside the lambda, wrap it in an unchecked exception, or define your own functional interface that declares throws.

Let me show you the wrapper approach I usually use.

@FunctionalInterface
interface ThrowingFunction<T, R> {
    R apply(T t) throws Exception;
}

static <T, R> Function<T, R> unchecked(ThrowingFunction<T, R> f) {
    return t -> {
        try { return f.apply(t); }
        catch (Exception e) { throw new RuntimeException(e); }
    };
}

try {
    List.of("1", "2", "x").stream()
        .map(unchecked(s -> {
            if (s.equals("x")) throw new IOException("bad file " + s);
            return Integer.parseInt(s);
        }))
        .forEach(System.out::println);
} catch (RuntimeException e) {
    System.out.println("wrapped: " + e.getCause());
}
// 1
// 2
// wrapped: java.io.IOException: bad file x

For I/O specifically, I'd wrap in UncheckedIOException instead of a plain RuntimeException, so the caller can still catch it precisely. That's what Files.lines itself does.

The other choice is design-level: if a failure for one element shouldn't stop the others, I don't throw at all. I map each element to a result object, like a small record with either a value or an error, and partition the results afterwards.

So in practice: wrap and rethrow when one failure should abort everything, and turn failures into data when you want to process the whole batch and report errors at the end.

Interviewers look for: why it fails (the interface's signature), a clean wrapper, and preserving the cause.

25. The Streams API

BasicWhat is a stream, and how is it different from a collection?

So a collection is a data structure that stores elements. A stream, added in Java 8, doesn't store anything: it's a pipeline that describes a computation over a source, like a collection, an array, a file or a generator.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<String> src = new ArrayList<>(List.of("b", "a"));
List<String> sorted = src.stream().sorted().toList();

System.out.println(src + " " + sorted);   // [b, a] [a, b]

The source list is untouched. The differences I'd list:

  • No storage: a stream pulls elements from its source as needed.
  • Functional: operations produce a new result; they don't modify the source.
  • Lazy: nothing runs until a terminal operation asks for a result.
  • Single use: once consumed, a stream can't be traversed again.
  • Possibly unbounded: Stream.iterate or Stream.generate can be infinite, which a collection never can.
  • Internal iteration: I say what to do; the library controls how it loops, which is what makes parallel() possible.

So in practice I think of collections as "data" and streams as "a query over data." I use a stream to transform, filter or aggregate, and a plain loop when I need early break with complex state or to mutate things as I go.

BasicWhat's the difference between intermediate and terminal operations?

So intermediate operations return another stream and are lazy: they just add a stage to the pipeline. A terminal operation produces a result or a side effect, and it's what actually triggers the whole pipeline to run.

Intermediate (lazy, return Stream)Terminal (eager, end the stream)
filter, map, flatMap, peekcollect, toList, reduce
distinct, sorted (stateful)forEach, count, min, max
limit, skip, takeWhile, dropWhilefindFirst, findAny, anyMatch, allMatch, noneMatch

Let me show you that intermediate operations really don't run on their own.

Stream<String> s = Stream.of("a", "bb", "ccc")
    .peek(x -> System.out.println("peek " + x))
    .filter(x -> x.length() > 1);
System.out.println("pipeline built");
// pipeline built        <- the only output; peek never ran

Two sub-categories are worth mentioning. Stateful intermediate operations like sorted and distinct need to see many or all elements before emitting, which matters for memory and for parallel performance. Short-circuiting operations like limit, findFirst and anyMatch can stop early, which is what lets you work with infinite streams.

So in practice: if a pipeline "does nothing", the first thing I check is whether it has a terminal operation.

Interviewers look for: the word "lazy", and that the terminal operation triggers execution.

IntermediatePredict the output. How does lazy evaluation actually process elements?

So streams don't run stage by stage over the whole list. Each element travels through the entire pipeline one at a time, vertically, and short-circuiting operations stop the pull as soon as they have enough.

List<String> result = Stream.of("one", "two", "three", "four")
    .peek(x -> System.out.println("peek: " + x))
    .filter(x -> { System.out.println("filter: " + x); return x.length() > 3; })
    .map(x -> { System.out.println("map: " + x); return x.toUpperCase(); })
    .limit(1)
    .toList();
System.out.println(result);

The output is:

peek: one
filter: one
peek: two
filter: two
peek: three
filter: three
map: three
