[THREE]

Let me walk through it. "one" goes through peek, fails the filter, and is dropped. Same for "two". "three" passes the filter, gets mapped, and reaches limit(1), which now has what it needs, so the pipeline stops. "four" is never even looked at. map ran once, not four times.

A second trap: since Java 9, count() can skip the pipeline entirely if it can compute the size directly from the source.

long n = List.of(1, 2, 3).stream()
    .peek(x -> System.out.println("counted " + x))
    .count();
System.out.println("count = " + n);
// count = 3        <- peek never printed

So in practice I never put real logic in peek. It's a debugging aid, and whether it runs depends on what the rest of the pipeline needs.

Interviewers look for: element-by-element (vertical) processing, limit stopping the pull, and that peek is not guaranteed to run.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between map and flatMap?

So map is one-to-one: each element becomes exactly one new element. flatMap is one-to-many: each element becomes a stream of zero or more elements, and all those streams are flattened into one.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<List<Integer>> nested = List.of(List.of(1, 2), List.of(3), List.of());

nested.stream().map(List::size).toList();        // [2, 1, 0]
nested.stream().flatMap(List::stream).toList();  // [1, 2, 3]

List<String> lines = List.of("hello world", "java streams");

lines.stream().map(l -> l.split(" ")).toList();  // List<String[]>, size 2
lines.stream()
     .flatMap(l -> Arrays.stream(l.split(" ")))
     .toList();                                   // [hello, world, java, streams]

The classic mistake is the third line. People want words and use map with split, and end up with a stream of arrays. Whenever my mapping function returns a collection, array or stream and I want the contents, that's the signal for flatMap.

The same idea exists on Optional: if the mapping function itself returns an Optional, map gives you Optional<Optional<T>>, and flatMap gives you Optional<T>.

So in practice: map to transform, flatMap to unpack. Real examples from my work are "all order lines across all orders" and "all roles across all users".

Interviewers look for: the split-into-words example and the Stream<String[]> trap.

IntermediateCan a stream be reused?

No. A stream can be consumed only once. After a terminal operation it's closed, and any further operation throws IllegalStateException.

Stream<Integer> nums = Stream.of(1, 2, 3);
System.out.println(nums.count());   // 3
nums.count();
// IllegalStateException: stream has already been operated upon or closed

It's by design. A stream can wrap a one-shot source like a file, a network socket or an iterator, and there's no general way to rewind those. So Java treats every stream as single-pass. The same exception also comes up if you call two intermediate operations on the same stream reference, since each one "uses" it.

If I need to run several pipelines over the same data, I keep the source and create a fresh stream each time, or use a Supplier:

Supplier<Stream<Integer>> nums2 = () -> Stream.of(1, 2, 3);
nums2.get().count();                     // 3
nums2.get().mapToInt(i -> i).sum();      // 6

If the source is expensive, like a big file or a database query, I'd rather do one pass and compute everything together, for example with Collectors.teeing (Java 12) or summaryStatistics(), than re-read it twice.

So in practice I never store a stream in a field or return it to be used twice. I store or return the collection, or a supplier of the stream.

IntermediatefindFirst vs findAny. When does the difference matter?

So findFirst returns the first element in encounter order. findAny is allowed to return any matching element. On a sequential stream they almost always give the same answer; the difference shows up with parallel streams.

Let me show you with a quick example.

List<Integer> list = IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 1000).boxed().toList();

list.stream().filter(x -> x % 7 == 0).findFirst();        // Optional[7], always

list.parallelStream().filter(x -> x % 7 == 0).findAny();  // some multiple of 7, not necessarily 7

When I ran the parallel findAny 200 times I got several different answers. Each worker thread handles a chunk of the list, and whichever thread finds a match first wins.

Why use findAny then? Because in parallel, findFirst has to coordinate: even if a thread working on the second half finds a match early, the pipeline has to wait to make sure nothing earlier matches. findAny can return as soon as any thread finds something.

Both return an Optional, because the stream might be empty or nothing might match.

So in practice: I use findFirst when order is part of the meaning, like "earliest failed payment", and findAny when I just need an example, like "is there any admin, give me one", especially on a parallel stream.

AdvancedWhat are the pitfalls of parallel streams?

So parallel() looks like a free speed-up, but it often isn't. It splits the work across the common ForkJoinPool, and it's only a win when the data is large, splits cheaply, the per-element work is CPU-heavy and independent, and there's no shared mutable state.

Let me show you the most common bug, which is shared mutable state.

List<Integer> unsafe = new ArrayList<>();
IntStream.range(0, 10_000).parallel().forEach(unsafe::add);
// size is often less than 10,000, or ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException

List<Integer> safe = IntStream.range(0, 10_000).parallel().boxed().toList();
// always 10,000, in order

I ran the unsafe version 20 times and 18 runs were wrong. ArrayList isn't thread-safe. The fix is to let the stream build the result with a collector, never to add into an outside collection.

The other pitfalls I watch for:

  • Shared common pool: every parallel stream in the JVM uses the same ForkJoinPool.commonPool(), sized to CPU cores minus one. In one service we had a parallel stream doing blocking HTTP calls. It starved the pool, and unrelated parallel code across the app slowed down. Blocking I/O never belongs in a parallel stream.
  • Bad sources: ArrayList, arrays and IntStream.range split well. LinkedList, Stream.iterate and I/O-based streams split badly.
  • Order-dependent operations: findFirst, limit and sorted are expensive in parallel. forEach doesn't respect order; forEachOrdered does, but gives up much of the gain.
  • Small data: splitting and merging costs more than it saves for a few thousand cheap elements.
  • Non-associative reduce: reduce in parallel needs an associative function and a true identity value, or results change between runs.

So in practice I default to sequential, and only go parallel after measuring with a real benchmark, like JMH, on production-sized data.

Interviewers look for: shared mutable state, the common ForkJoinPool, and "measure first".

26. Collectors & Method References

BasicWhat are the four kinds of method references?

So a method reference is shorthand for a lambda that just calls one existing method. There are four kinds, and the difference is where the receiver object comes from.

Function<String, Integer> parse = Integer::parseInt;   // 1. static method
String prefix = "Hi ";
Function<String, String> greet = prefix::concat;       // 2. bound instance: a specific object
Function<String, Integer> len = String::length;        // 3. unbound instance: the argument is the receiver
Supplier<List<String>> mk = ArrayList::new;             // 4. constructor

parse.apply("42");    // 42
greet.apply("Ana");   // "Hi Ana"
len.apply("four");    // 4
mk.get();             // []

The one people mix up is 2 vs 3. With prefix::concat, the object is fixed when the reference is created, and the argument is passed to it. With String::length, there's no object yet: the first parameter becomes the receiver. So String::length is s -> s.length(), and a two-argument version like BiFunction<String,String,Boolean> eq = String::equalsIgnoreCase is (a, b) -> a.equalsIgnoreCase(b).

Array constructors work too: int[]::new is n -> new int[n], which is why you see toArray(String[]::new).

One subtle difference from a lambda: with a bound reference like obj::method, obj is evaluated immediately, so if it's null you get the NullPointerException when creating the reference, not when calling it.

So in practice I use a method reference when it's clearer than the lambda, and a lambda when the reference would hide what's happening.

Interviewers look for: the bound vs unbound distinction, explained with the implicit first parameter.

IntermediateWrite it: group employees by department and find the average salary.

So this is groupingBy with a downstream collector. The classifier picks the key, and the downstream collector decides what to do with each group's elements.

record Employee(String name, String dept, double salary) {}

List<Employee> emps = List.of(
    new Employee("Asha",  "Eng",   120_000),
    new Employee("Ravi",  "Eng",   100_000),
    new Employee("Meera", "Sales",  70_000),
    new Employee("John",  "Sales",  90_000),
    new Employee("Li",    "HR",     60_000),
    new Employee("Sam",   "Eng",   120_000));

Map<String, Double> avg = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
        Employee::dept,
        TreeMap::new,                                // sorted keys
        Collectors.averagingDouble(Employee::salary)));
// {Eng=113333.33333333333, HR=60000.0, Sales=80000.0}

I passed TreeMap::new so the output order is predictable; the plain two-argument version gives a HashMap with no guaranteed order.

Interviewers usually follow up with variations, and they're all just different downstream collectors:

// count per department
groupingBy(Employee::dept, counting())             // {Eng=3, HR=1, Sales=2}

// names per department
groupingBy(Employee::dept, mapping(Employee::name, toList()))
                                                   // {Eng=[Asha, Ravi, Sam], HR=[Li], Sales=[Meera, John]}

// highest paid per department
groupingBy(Employee::dept, maxBy(comparingDouble(Employee::salary)))
                                                   // Eng -> Asha, HR -> Li, Sales -> John

Note that maxBy returns Optional<Employee> per group. If I don't want that, I wrap it with collectingAndThen(maxBy(...), Optional::get), which is safe here because a group only exists if it has at least one element.

For real money I'd use BigDecimal, not double, but for this exercise double is what's expected.

Interviewers look for: fluency with downstream collectors, and awareness that counting() returns Long.

IntermediateWrite it: find the second highest salary.

So the key detail is distinct(). "Second highest salary" means the second highest distinct value, and if two people share the top salary, skipping one element without de-duplicating gives the wrong answer.

Optional<Double> second = emps.stream()
    .map(Employee::salary)
    .distinct()
    .sorted(Comparator.reverseOrder())
    .skip(1)
    .findFirst();
// Optional[100000.0]

// Without distinct() -- WRONG when there's a tie at the top:
// Asha and Sam both earn 120,000, so this returns Optional[120000.0]

Using the same employee list as before, Asha and Sam both earn 120,000, so the naive version returns 120,000 again. That's exactly the trap interviewers set.

I return an Optional because with fewer than two distinct salaries there is no answer, and I'd rather make the caller decide than return 0 or throw. The same pattern works on an int array:

int[] arr = {5, 9, 3, 9, 7};
Arrays.stream(arr).boxed()
    .distinct()
    .sorted(Comparator.reverseOrder())
    .skip(1)
    .findFirst()
    .orElse(-1);          // 7

If they ask for the employee record, not just the number, I'd find the salary this way and then filter employees with that salary, since several people could share it.

Complexity is O(n log n) because of the sort. If they push for O(n), I'd drop streams and do a single loop tracking the top two distinct values. So in practice I give the stream version first for readability and mention the loop as the optimisation.

IntermediateWrite it: count the frequency of each character in a string.

So I'd turn the string into a stream of characters and use groupingBy with counting(). The one detail is the map type: I use LinkedHashMap so the result keeps first-appearance order, which also makes follow-up questions easy.

String text = "programming";

Map<Character, Long> freq = text.chars()               // IntStream of code units
    .mapToObj(c -> (char) c)                             // box to Character
    .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
        Function.identity(),
        LinkedHashMap::new,
        Collectors.counting()));
// {p=1, r=2, o=1, g=2, a=1, m=2, i=1, n=1}

Two things to point out. chars() gives an IntStream, so I cast back to char before boxing, otherwise the keys would be numbers. And counting() returns Long, not Integer, which trips people up when they declare Map<Character, Integer> and get a compile error.

Because the map is ordered, the common follow-ups are one line each:

// first non-repeating character
freq.entrySet().stream()
    .filter(e -> e.getValue() == 1)
    .map(Map.Entry::getKey)
    .findFirst();                          // Optional[p]

// most frequent character
freq.entrySet().stream()
    .max(Map.Entry.comparingByValue());    // r=2 (first of the ties)

The same groupingBy plus counting() pattern counts words: split on spaces and group by the word itself.

So in practice, in an interview I'd write the stream version, but if they ask for the fastest version on lowercase ASCII, I'd use an int[26] array, which avoids boxing entirely.

Interviewers look for: the cast from int to char, Long from counting, and choosing LinkedHashMap on purpose.

IntermediateWrite it: remove duplicates from a list while keeping the original order.

So there are two clean ways: stream().distinct() or a LinkedHashSet. Both keep the first occurrence of each element in its original position. The mistake to avoid is a plain HashSet, which removes duplicates but gives no ordering guarantee.

List<Integer> withDups = List.of(4, 1, 4, 2, 1, 3);

withDups.stream().distinct().toList();          // [4, 1, 2, 3]
new ArrayList<>(new LinkedHashSet<>(withDups)); // [4, 1, 2, 3]

Both rely on equals and hashCode, so for my own classes those must be implemented correctly. Records do that automatically.

The harder follow-up is "remove duplicates by a field", like keeping one employee per department. distinct() can't do that because it compares whole objects. I use toMap with a merge function that keeps the first, into a LinkedHashMap:

List<Employee> onePerDept = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.toMap(
        Employee::dept,
        e -> e,
        (first, second) -> first,       // keep the first one seen
        LinkedHashMap::new))
    .values().stream().toList();
// Asha, Meera, Li

So in practice: distinct() when I'm already in a stream, LinkedHashSet when I just have a list, and toMap with a merge function when uniqueness is by key.

AdvancedWhat happens with Collectors.toMap when there's a duplicate key?

So the two-argument toMap throws IllegalStateException on a duplicate key. It refuses to guess which value you want. This is a very common production bug, because the code works in testing with clean data and fails the first time real data has a duplicate.

emps.stream().collect(Collectors.toMap(Employee::dept, Employee::name));
// IllegalStateException: Duplicate key Eng
//   (attempted merging values Asha and Ravi)

The fix is the three-argument version with a merge function, and optionally a fourth argument for the map type:

Map<String, String> merged = emps.stream()
    .collect(Collectors.toMap(
        Employee::dept,
        Employee::name,
        (a, b) -> a + "," + b,      // or (a, b) -> a to keep the first
        TreeMap::new));
// {Eng=Asha,Ravi,Sam, HR=Li, Sales=Meera,John}

The second trap is null values. toMap rejects a null value with a NullPointerException, even though HashMap itself allows null values. If a value can be null, I map it to a default or filter it out first.

In my last project we had a job that built a map of user email to user. It ran fine for months and then crashed because two accounts had been created with the same email. We fixed it by deciding the business rule explicitly, keeping the most recently active account, in the merge function, and adding a log line when a duplicate was merged.

So in practice, every time I write toMap I ask "can this key repeat?" If yes, I either write a merge function with a real rule, or switch to groupingBy if I actually want all the values.

Interviewers look for: the exception type, the merge function, and the null-value NPE as a bonus.

AdvancedStream.toList() vs Collectors.toList() vs toUnmodifiableList(). Are they the same?

No, and the differences matter when code is migrated. Collectors.toList() (Java 8) returns a list with no guarantees, which in practice is a mutable ArrayList. Stream.toList() (Java 16) returns an unmodifiable list that allows nulls. Collectors.toUnmodifiableList() (Java 10) returns an unmodifiable list that rejects nulls.

List<Integer> a = Stream.of(1, 2).collect(Collectors.toList());
a.add(3);                       // works: [1, 2, 3]

List<Integer> b = Stream.of(1, 2).toList();
b.add(3);                       // UnsupportedOperationException

Stream.of(1, null).toList();                                // [1, null]
Stream.of(1, null).collect(Collectors.toUnmodifiableList()); // NullPointerException
SinceMutableNulls
Collectors.toList()8Yes in practice (not guaranteed)Allowed
Collectors.toUnmodifiableList()10NoNPE
Stream.toList()16NoAllowed

The real risk: someone does a "cleanup" refactor replacing .collect(Collectors.toList()) with .toList() everywhere. It compiles, tests pass, and then some caller three layers away does list.add(...) or Collections.sort(list) and blows up at runtime.

A related one: partitioningBy always returns both true and false keys, even when one is empty, while groupingBy on a boolean only includes keys that actually occurred.

So in practice I use toList() for new code, since immutable results are safer, and when I need to mutate I say so explicitly with Collectors.toCollection(ArrayList::new).

27. Date & Time API

BasicWhy was java.util.Date replaced? LocalDate vs Date.

So java.util.Date was badly designed: it's mutable, not thread-safe, its name is misleading, and its API is full of traps. Java 8 added java.time, based on Joda-Time, where every type is immutable and thread-safe and each one represents exactly one concept.

Let me show you the traps.

new Date(2024, 1, 15);
// Fri Feb 15 00:00:00 UTC 3924
//   year is offset from 1900, month is 0-based

Date shared = new Date(0);
shared.setTime(86_400_000L);  // anyone holding this reference sees the change

LocalDate jan15 = LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 15);   // month is 1-based, year is real
LocalDate next  = jan15.plusMonths(1);          // 2024-02-15, a NEW object
jan15.plusDays(10);                             // result ignored...
System.out.println(jan15);                      // 2024-01-15, unchanged

The name is the other problem. Date isn't a date at all; it's an instant, a number of milliseconds since 1970 UTC, which prints itself in the JVM's default time zone. LocalDate really is just a date, with no time and no zone, like a birthday.

Mutability caused real bugs: a class exposing a Date field through a getter could be modified by any caller, so you needed defensive copies everywhere. And SimpleDateFormat isn't thread-safe, which produced corrupted dates when it was shared as a static field.

So in practice I only touch Date at the boundary with old libraries, converting with date.toInstant() and Date.from(instant), and use java.time everywhere else.

Interviewers look for: mutability and thread safety as the main reasons, and that Date is really an instant.

BasicLocalDateTime, ZonedDateTime, OffsetDateTime, Instant. Which one do you use when?

So they differ in how much context they carry. Instant is a point on the global timeline in UTC. The "Local" types have no zone at all. ZonedDateTime adds a real region with its daylight-saving rules, and OffsetDateTime adds only a fixed offset.

Instant inst = Instant.parse("2024-03-10T14:30:00Z");

inst.atZone(ZoneId.of("Asia/Kolkata"));      // 2024-03-10T20:00+05:30[Asia/Kolkata]
inst.atZone(ZoneId.of("America/New_York"));  // 2024-03-10T10:30-04:00[America/New_York]
inst.atOffset(ZoneOffset.ofHours(2));        // 2024-03-10T16:30+02:00
LocalDateTime.ofInstant(inst, ZoneId.of("Asia/Kolkata"));  // 2024-03-10T20:00  (zone dropped)
TypeRepresentsUse for
InstantMoment in UTCTimestamps: createdAt, event logs, storing in DB
LocalDateDate onlyBirthdays, holidays, due dates
LocalDateTimeDate + time, no zone"Meeting at 10:00" before you know where
ZonedDateTimeDate + time + regionScheduling and calendar math for real people
OffsetDateTimeDate + time + fixed offsetAPIs, JSON, JDBC TIMESTAMP WITH TIME ZONE

The mistake I see most is using LocalDateTime for timestamps. It has no zone, so "2024-03-10T20:00" could be any of 24-plus moments. If servers run in different zones, or during a DST change, the data becomes ambiguous.

So in practice: store and transmit Instant (or OffsetDateTime in UTC), and convert to ZonedDateTime only at the edge, when showing it to a user in their zone.

IntermediateHow do you handle time zones and daylight saving time correctly?

So the rules I follow are: store everything as UTC instants, keep the user's zone as a ZoneId like "Europe/London" rather than a fixed offset, and do calendar arithmetic in ZonedDateTime, which knows the DST rules.

Let me show you why "one day" and "24 hours" aren't the same thing. In New York, clocks jumped forward at 2 a.m. on 10 March 2024.

ZonedDateTime before = ZonedDateTime.of(2024, 3, 9, 12, 0, 0, 0,
                                        ZoneId.of("America/New_York"));

before.plusDays(1);    // 2024-03-10T12:00-04:00  same wall-clock time
before.plusHours(24);  // 2024-03-10T13:00-04:00  exactly 24 real hours
Duration.between(before, before.plusDays(1));   // PT23H  that "day" was 23 hours

ZonedDateTime.of(2024, 3, 10, 2, 30, 0, 0, ZoneId.of("America/New_York"));
// 2024-03-10T03:30-04:00  2:30 didn't exist, so it was shifted forward

So plusDays means calendar days, which is what a user expects for "remind me same time tomorrow". plusHours means physical time, which is what you want for "session expires in 24 hours".

In a previous project we had a daily report scheduled with a fixed offset of +05:30 for India, which was fine. Then we reused the same code for a UK customer with +00:00, and twice a year their report ran an hour off. Switching from ZoneOffset to ZoneId.of("Europe/London") fixed it, because a region ID tracks DST and a fixed offset doesn't.

So in practice: UTC in the database, ZoneId per user, and I never rely on the server's default zone. I set -Duser.timezone=UTC on servers so nothing depends on where the box happens to be.

Interviewers look for: "store UTC", region ID vs fixed offset, and a DST example.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between Duration and Period?

So Duration is an exact amount of time in seconds and nanoseconds, meant for machine time. Period is an amount in years, months and days, meant for human calendar time. The difference is that "one month" or even "one day" doesn't have a fixed length.

Period age = Period.between(LocalDate.of(1995, 8, 20), LocalDate.of(2024, 3, 10));
// P28Y6M19D  -> age.getYears() == 28

ChronoUnit.DAYS.between(LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 1), LocalDate.of(2024, 3, 1));  // 60

Duration.ofMinutes(135);         // PT2H15M
Period.ofMonths(1).plusDays(3);  // P1M3D

LocalDate.of(2024, 1, 31).plusMonths(1);  // 2024-02-29  (clamped to month end)
LocalDate.of(2023, 1, 31).plusMonths(1);  // 2023-02-28

A few things I'd point out. To compute someone's age, Period.between(...).getYears() is correct; dividing days by 365 is not, because of leap years. And Period isn't normalised to a total: getDays() returns just the days part (19 above), not the total days. For total days I use ChronoUnit.DAYS.between.

Month arithmetic clamps to the end of the month, as you see with 31 January plus one month. That's usually right for billing dates, but I always mention it to product people because it surprises them.

So in practice: Duration for timeouts, SLAs and measuring elapsed time; Period for subscriptions, ages and "30 days from now" on a calendar.

IntermediateHow do you format and parse dates? Why is DateTimeFormatter better than SimpleDateFormat?

So DateTimeFormatter is immutable and thread-safe, so I can keep one as a static final constant and share it across threads. SimpleDateFormat keeps internal mutable state while parsing, so sharing one instance across threads gives wrong dates or random exceptions.

static final DateTimeFormatter F =
    DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("dd MMM yyyy, HH:mm", Locale.ENGLISH);

LocalDateTime.of(2024, 3, 10, 9, 5).format(F);     // "10 Mar 2024, 09:05"
LocalDate.parse("10/03/2024", DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("dd/MM/yyyy"));  // 2024-03-10
LocalDate.parse("2024-02-30");                     // DateTimeParseException

// The famous pattern bug: YYYY is week-based year, not calendar year
LocalDate d = LocalDate.of(2024, 12, 30);
d.format(DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("YYYY-MM-dd"));  // 2025-12-30  wrong!
d.format(DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("yyyy-MM-dd"));  // 2024-12-30

That YYYY bug is worth knowing. For the last few days of December, the week-based year is already next year, so the date prints a year ahead. It only shows up at year end, which is why it has caused real outages in production systems.

Other things I keep in mind: MM is month and mm is minutes; HH is 24-hour and hh is 12-hour. I always pass a Locale when using month names, otherwise the output depends on the server's language. And for APIs I prefer the ISO formats, which are the default for toString and parse.

So in practice: ISO-8601 for anything machine-to-machine, static final formatters with an explicit locale for display.

Interviewers look for: thread safety of the formatter, and bonus points for the YYYY vs yyyy trap.

AdvancedHow do you make code that depends on the current time testable?

So I never call LocalDate.now() or Instant.now() with no arguments inside business logic. I inject a java.time.Clock, and in tests I pass a fixed clock. Every now() method in java.time has an overload that takes a Clock.

class SubscriptionService {
    private final Clock clock;
    SubscriptionService(Clock clock) { this.clock = clock; }

    boolean isExpired(LocalDate expiry) {
        return LocalDate.now(clock).isAfter(expiry);
    }
}

// production
new SubscriptionService(Clock.systemUTC());

// test: freeze time at a tricky moment
Clock fixed = Clock.fixed(Instant.parse("2024-12-31T23:59:00Z"), ZoneOffset.UTC);
LocalDate.now(fixed);                                       // 2024-12-31
new SubscriptionService(fixed).isExpired(LocalDate.of(2024, 12, 30));   // true

Without this, tests around expiry, year end or DST are either impossible or flaky. I've seen a test suite that failed only between midnight and 1 a.m., because a test built "today" in one place and compared it with "today" computed a moment later in a different zone.

A fixed clock lets me test exact boundaries: one second before expiry, the moment of expiry, leap day, the DST gap. Clock.offset(base, Duration.ofDays(30)) lets me "jump forward" in a test. In Spring, I just declare a Clock bean and inject it.

So in practice, time is a dependency like any other: inject it, and never read it from a static call deep inside the logic.

Part 5 — Concurrency

28. Threads & Their Lifecycle

BasicWhat's the difference between a process and a thread?

So a process is a running program with its own memory space, managed by the OS. A thread is a path of execution inside a process. All threads of a process share its heap, but each thread has its own stack and program counter.

ProcessThread
MemoryOwn, isolated address spaceShares heap with other threads; own stack
Creation costHeavyMuch lighter (a platform thread still costs ~1 MB stack reserve)
CommunicationIPC: pipes, sockets, filesShared objects in memory
FailureA crash is isolatedAn OutOfMemoryError or corrupted state can affect every thread
In JavaOne JVM = one processThread, executors, virtual threads

In Java terms: when I start a JVM, that's one OS process. Inside it there's the main thread plus JVM threads for GC, JIT compilation and so on. Each Java platform thread maps one-to-one to an OS thread. Since Java 21, virtual threads are the exception: many virtual threads are scheduled on a small pool of OS threads.

The shared heap is both the benefit and the danger. Threads can communicate simply by reading and writing the same objects, which is fast, but that's also where race conditions and visibility problems come from.

So in practice, the reason we even talk about synchronized, volatile and concurrent collections is that threads share memory. Processes don't have that problem, but they pay for it in communication cost.

BasicHow do you create a thread? Runnable vs Callable.

So there are a few ways: extend Thread, pass a Runnable to a Thread, or, the way I actually do it, submit a Runnable or Callable to an ExecutorService. Since Java 21 there's also Thread.ofVirtual().

The difference between the two task types: Runnable.run() returns nothing and can't throw checked exceptions. Callable.call() returns a value and can throw any exception. You get the result or the exception back through a Future.

Thread t = new Thread(() -> System.out.println("hello"), "worker-1");
t.start();

Callable<Integer> task = () -> 6 * 7;
try (ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(2)) {   // try-with-resources: Java 19+
    Future<Integer> f = pool.submit(task);
    System.out.println(f.get());            // 42

    Future<?> r = pool.submit(() -> { throw new IllegalStateException("boom"); });
    try { r.get(); }
    catch (ExecutionException e) {
        System.out.println(e.getCause());   // java.lang.IllegalStateException: boom
    }
}

Note that the exception thrown inside the task doesn't disappear; it's wrapped in ExecutionException and rethrown when I call get(). That's a classic bug: people submit a task and never call get(), so failures are silently swallowed.

I prefer implementing Runnable over extending Thread, because it separates the task from the mechanism that runs it, and Java has single inheritance.

So in practice I almost never create raw threads. I use an executor, Callable when I need a result, and CompletableFuture when I want to compose results.

Interviewers look for: return value, checked exceptions, and how Future delivers the exception.

BasicWhat's the difference between calling start() and run()?

So start() creates a new thread and that new thread calls run(). Calling run() directly is just a normal method call on the current thread; no new thread is created at all.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Thread t = new Thread(
    () -> System.out.println("running in " + Thread.currentThread().getName()),
    "worker-1");

t.run();     // running in main
t.start();   // running in worker-1
t.join();

t.start();   // IllegalThreadStateException

The first call prints main, which proves it ran on the calling thread. That's the bug interviewers want you to spot: code that calls run() looks like it's working, but everything happens sequentially, and there's no concurrency at all.

The last line matters too. A thread can be started only once. Once it has terminated, it can't be restarted; calling start() again throws IllegalThreadStateException. If I need to run the same work again, I create a new thread or, better, submit the task to an executor again.

So in practice: start() to launch, join() to wait for it, and never run() unless I actually want to execute the task inline, for example in a unit test.

IntermediateWhat are the thread states in Java?

So Thread.State has six values: NEW, RUNNABLE, BLOCKED, WAITING, TIMED_WAITING and TERMINATED.

StateMeaningHow it gets there
NEWCreated, not startednew Thread(...)
RUNNABLERunning or ready to runstart(); also while doing blocking I/O
BLOCKEDWaiting to enter a synchronized blockAnother thread holds the monitor
WAITINGWaiting indefinitely for another threadwait(), join(), LockSupport.park()
TIMED_WAITINGWaiting with a timeoutsleep(ms), wait(ms), join(ms)
TERMINATEDFinishedrun() returned or threw

Let me show you a sequence I verified:

Object lock = new Object();
Thread s = new Thread(() -> {
    synchronized (lock) {
        try { lock.wait(); } catch (InterruptedException e) {}
    }
});
s.getState();        // NEW
s.start();
// after a moment:   WAITING        (inside wait())

synchronized (lock) {
    lock.notify();
    // s is now      BLOCKED        (woken, but needs the lock back, which main holds)
}
s.join();
s.getState();        // TERMINATED

Two subtleties interviewers like. First, there's no separate "RUNNING" state; a thread using the CPU and one waiting for a CPU are both RUNNABLE. Second, a thread blocked on socket I/O also shows as RUNNABLE, which surprises people reading thread dumps.

So in practice, when I read a thread dump during an incident, many threads BLOCKED on the same monitor means lock contention, and many WAITING on a pool's queue usually just means idle workers.

Interviewers look for: BLOCKED vs WAITING, and that a notified thread goes to BLOCKED before it can run again.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between sleep() and wait()?

So the key difference is the lock. Thread.sleep() pauses the current thread and keeps any locks it holds. wait() must be called while holding an object's monitor, and it releases that monitor while waiting, so other threads can get in and change the state.

sleep()wait()
Defined onThread (static)Object
LockKeeps itReleases it, reacquires before returning
Needs synchronized?NoYes, else IllegalMonitorStateException
Wakes up onTimeout or interruptnotify/notifyAll, timeout, interrupt, or spuriously
PurposePauseWait for a condition set by another thread

Let me show you the correct wait pattern:

class Mailbox {
    private String message;

    synchronized void put(String m) { message = m; notifyAll(); }

    synchronized String take() throws InterruptedException {
        while (message == null) wait();   // while, not if
        String m = message; message = null; return m;
    }
}

I always wait in a while loop, never an if. Threads can wake up spuriously, and with notifyAll another thread might grab the message first, so I must recheck the condition after waking.

The bug to avoid is sleeping inside a synchronized block to "wait for something": every other thread needing that lock stalls for the full sleep.

So in practice I rarely use wait/notify directly anymore. A BlockingQueue, CountDownLatch or CompletableFuture expresses the same thing more safely.

Interviewers look for: "wait releases the lock, sleep doesn't", and the while loop.

IntermediateHow do you stop a thread?

So you can't safely force a thread to stop. Thread.stop() was deprecated long ago because it kills the thread mid-operation and can leave shared objects half-updated; in Java 20+ it just throws UnsupportedOperationException. The correct way is cooperative: call interrupt(), and have the thread check its interrupted status and exit cleanly.

Thread worker = new Thread(() -> {
    while (!Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted()) {
        try {
            Thread.sleep(50);          // do a unit of work
        } catch (InterruptedException e) {
            // the flag was CLEARED when the exception was thrown
            System.out.println("interrupted during sleep, flag now: "
                + Thread.currentThread().isInterrupted());   // false
            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();              // restore it
        }
    }
    System.out.println("worker exiting cleanly");
});
worker.start();
Thread.sleep(200);
worker.interrupt();
worker.join();

What interrupt() does: it sets a flag. If the thread is blocked in sleep, wait, join or a blocking queue call, that call throws InterruptedException right away and the flag is cleared. That's the trap in the example: if I just caught the exception and continued, the loop condition would still see false and never exit. So I restore the flag.

The worst thing you can do is an empty catch (InterruptedException e) {}. It swallows the stop request, and things like executor.shutdownNow() stop working.

So in practice: in library code I either rethrow InterruptedException or restore the flag; with executors I call shutdownNow() or future.cancel(true), which interrupt the worker for me. For a simple loop a volatile boolean running flag also works, but it can't wake a thread out of sleep, so interrupt is better.

Interviewers look for: cooperative cancellation, the flag being cleared, and restoring it.

IntermediateWhat is a daemon thread?

So a daemon thread is a background thread that doesn't keep the JVM alive. The JVM exits when all non-daemon threads have finished, and any daemon threads still running are simply abandoned. The garbage collector threads are daemon threads.

Thread d = new Thread(() -> {
    while (true) {
        try { Thread.sleep(1000); } catch (InterruptedException e) { return; }
    }
});
d.setDaemon(true);       // must be called BEFORE start()
d.start();

d.isDaemon();                          // true
Thread.currentThread().isDaemon();     // false  (main is a user thread)
Thread.ofVirtual().unstarted(() -> {}).isDaemon();   // true  (virtual threads always are)
// main returns -> JVM exits, even though d loops forever

Some rules: setDaemon must be called before the thread starts, otherwise it throws IllegalThreadStateException. A new thread inherits the daemon status of the thread that created it. And virtual threads (Java 21) are always daemon.

The danger is that daemon threads are killed abruptly: no finally blocks run and no buffers are flushed. In one project, an audit logger ran on a daemon thread writing to a buffered file, and on shutdown the last few seconds of audit events were lost. We fixed it by making it a normal thread and stopping it from a shutdown hook, which flushed and closed the file.

So in practice, daemon threads are fine for things that are safe to lose, like a cache-refresh or metrics sampler, but never for work that must complete, like writes or sending messages.

29. synchronized, volatile & the Java Memory Model

BasicWhy is count++ not thread-safe?

So count++ looks like one operation but it's three: read the value, add one, write it back. If two threads interleave those steps, both read the same old value and one increment is lost. That's called a race condition, specifically a lost update.

Thread A: read count (5)
Thread B: read count (5)
Thread A: write 6
Thread B: write 6        <- one increment lost

Let me show you what happens with two threads each incrementing 100,000 times.

static int count = 0;
static volatile int volCount = 0;
static int syncCount = 0;
static final AtomicInteger atomic = new AtomicInteger();
static synchronized void inc() { syncCount++; }

// two threads, each running the loop 100,000 times:
count++;                  // plain:        164827  (different every run)
volCount++;               // volatile:     194303  (still wrong!)
inc();                    // synchronized: 200000
atomic.incrementAndGet(); // atomic:       200000

The interesting line is volatile. People assume it fixes this, but it doesn't. volatile guarantees each read sees the latest write, but it doesn't make read-modify-write atomic. Two threads can still both read 5 and both write 6.

The fixes are synchronized, which makes the three steps mutually exclusive, or AtomicInteger, which does it with a single hardware compare-and-swap instruction and no lock.

So in practice, for a simple counter I use AtomicInteger, or LongAdder if many threads hammer it; and when several fields must change together, I use a lock.

Interviewers look for: read-modify-write, and knowing that volatile alone doesn't fix it.

Basicsynchronized method vs synchronized block. Which do you prefer?

So both acquire a monitor lock, and both give mutual exclusion and visibility. The differences are which lock and how much code it covers. A synchronized instance method locks this for the whole method; a synchronized block lets me choose the lock object and lock only the critical section.

// Method: locks 'this' for everything, including work that doesn't need it
public synchronized void add(String item, int qty) { ... }

// Block: private lock, minimal critical section
class Inventory {
    private final Object lock = new Object();
    private final Map<String, Integer> stock = new HashMap<>();

    void add(String item, int qty) {
        String key = item.trim().toLowerCase();   // no lock needed
        synchronized (lock) {
            stock.merge(key, qty, Integer::sum);  // only the shared state
        }
    }
}

I prefer the block with a private final lock for two reasons. First, less time holding the lock means less contention. Second, and more important, this is public: any outside code can do synchronized (inventory) and interfere with my locking, or even cause a deadlock. Nobody outside can touch a private lock object.

Both are reentrant: a thread already holding the lock can enter another synchronized section on the same lock without deadlocking itself.

So in practice, for small classes where the whole method is the critical section, a synchronized method is fine and more readable. For anything shared widely or with expensive non-shared work, I use a block on a private lock.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between a class-level lock and an object-level lock?

So an object-level lock is the monitor of one instance: a synchronized instance method or synchronized (this). A class-level lock is the monitor of the Class object: a static synchronized method or synchronized (Counter.class). There's one class lock per class, but one object lock per instance, and the two are completely independent.

Let me show you with timings I measured. Each method sleeps 300 ms while holding its lock.

class Counter {
    synchronized void instanceMethod() throws InterruptedException { Thread.sleep(300); }
    static synchronized void staticMethod() throws InterruptedException { Thread.sleep(300); }
}

// thread 1: c1.instanceMethod()   thread 2: c2.instanceMethod()   -> ~300 ms (parallel)
// thread 1: Counter.staticMethod() thread 2: Counter.staticMethod() -> ~600 ms (serialised)
// thread 1: c1.instanceMethod()   thread 2: Counter.staticMethod() -> ~300 ms (parallel!)

The first case runs in parallel because the two threads lock different objects. The second is serialised because there's only one class lock. The third is the trick question: instance and static synchronized methods don't block each other, because they use different locks.

That third case causes real bugs. If an instance method and a static method both modify the same static field, it isn't protected at all, even though both methods say synchronized.

So in practice: protect instance state with an instance lock, protect static state with the class lock, and make sure every access to a given piece of state uses the same lock.

Interviewers look for: that static and instance synchronized methods don't block each other.

Intermediatevolatile vs synchronized vs atomic variables. When do you use each?

So they solve different parts of the problem. volatile gives visibility and ordering but not atomicity. synchronized gives visibility and mutual exclusion over any block of code. Atomic classes give lock-free atomicity for single-variable updates.

Let me show you the visibility problem that volatile fixes.

static boolean running = true;             // NOT volatile

Thread spinner = new Thread(() -> { long n = 0; while (running) n++; });
spinner.start();
Thread.sleep(500);
running = false;
spinner.join(2000);
spinner.isAlive();    // true -- it never saw the change

static volatile boolean vRunning = true;   // same code with volatile
// isAlive() -> false, it stops immediately

Without volatile, the JIT is allowed to hoist the read of running out of the loop, so the thread spins forever. On my machine it was still running after two seconds. With volatile, every read goes to main memory.

VisibilityAtomic compound opsBlocks?Use for
volatileYesNoNoFlags, publishing an immutable object
AtomicInteger etc.YesSingle variableNo (CAS)Counters, sequence numbers
synchronizedYesAny code blockYesInvariants across several fields

So in practice: volatile when one thread writes and others only read, like a shutdown flag; atomics for counters and single-variable updates; a lock whenever two or more fields must change together, like a balance and a transaction list.

Interviewers look for: visibility vs atomicity, and a correct use case for each.

IntermediateWhat is the happens-before relationship?

So happens-before is the core rule of the Java Memory Model. If action A happens-before action B, then everything A wrote is guaranteed to be visible to B, and they won't appear reordered. Without a happens-before edge between two threads, the JVM and CPU are allowed to reorder and cache, and one thread may see stale or partial values.

The main rules that create an edge:

  • Program order: within one thread, each action happens-before the next.
  • Monitor lock: unlocking a monitor happens-before every later lock of the same monitor.
  • volatile: a write to a volatile field happens-before every later read of that field.
  • Thread start and join: t.start() happens-before anything in t; everything in t happens-before t.join() returns.
  • Transitivity: if A → B and B → C, then A → C.

Let me show you how transitivity makes safe publication work.

static int data;                   // plain field
static volatile boolean ready;

// writer thread               // reader thread
data = 42;                     while (!ready) Thread.onSpinWait();
ready = true;                  System.out.println(data);   // guaranteed 42

data = 42 happens-before ready = true by program order. The volatile write happens-before the reader's volatile read. So by transitivity, the reader must see 42, even though data isn't volatile. If ready weren't volatile, the reader could legally see ready == true and data == 0.

The same rule is why things like ConcurrentHashMap.put, BlockingQueue.put and Future.get are safe hand-off points: their docs state the happens-before guarantee.

So in practice I don't reason about caches and CPU reordering. I just ask: "Is there a happens-before edge between this write and that read?" If not, it's a bug.

AdvancedExplain double-checked locking. Why does it need volatile?

So double-checked locking is a lazy singleton pattern that avoids taking the lock on every call: check without the lock, lock only if it looks null, then check again inside. It's broken without volatile, because another thread can see a non-null reference to an object whose constructor hasn't finished yet.

class Singleton {
    private static volatile Singleton instance;     // volatile is essential
    private Singleton() {}

    static Singleton getInstance() {
        Singleton local = instance;                 // one volatile read on the fast path
        if (local == null) {                        // 1st check, no lock
            synchronized (Singleton.class) {
                local = instance;
                if (local == null) {                // 2nd check, with lock
                    instance = local = new Singleton();
                }
            }
        }
        return local;
    }
}

Why volatile? new Singleton() is really three steps: allocate memory, run the constructor, assign the reference. Without a happens-before edge, the JIT or CPU may assign the reference before the constructor finishes. A second thread doing the first, unlocked check sees non-null, skips the lock, and uses a half-built object. volatile forbids that reordering and creates the happens-before edge. This has been correct since the memory model was fixed in Java 5.

The second check is needed because two threads can both pass the first check; the one that gets the lock second must not create another instance.

That said, I'd rarely write this in real code. The holder idiom is simpler and just as lazy, because the JVM guarantees class initialisation is thread-safe:

class Holder {
    private static class Lazy { static final Holder INSTANCE = new Holder(); }
    static Holder get() { return Lazy.INSTANCE; }
}

So in practice: an enum or the holder idiom for singletons, and double-checked locking only for lazy instance fields where those don't apply.

Interviewers look for: the reordering explanation (reference published before construction finishes), not just "add volatile".

AdvancedWhat's wrong with this code? synchronized (count) where count is an Integer.

So the bug is that the code locks on the object that it's changing. count++ on an Integer creates a new Integer object and reassigns the field, so different threads end up locking different objects, and there's no mutual exclusion at all.

static Integer count = 0;

Runnable r = () -> {
    for (int i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) {
        synchronized (count) { count++; }   // lock object changes every iteration
    }
};
// two threads -> count: 158064  (should be 200000)

// javac even warns:
// warning: [synchronization] attempt to synchronize on an instance of a value-based class

Let me walk through it. Thread A locks the Integer 5, increments, and the field now points to 6. Thread B, which had already read the field, is still locking 5, or it locks 6 while A is locking 7. The lock protects nothing.

There are related traps with the same root cause:

  • Locking on a String literal: synchronized ("LOCK"). Literals are interned, so any unrelated library that does the same shares your lock, which can cause mysterious contention or deadlocks.
  • Locking on a boxed small number: Integer.valueOf caches -128 to 127, so those objects are shared JVM-wide.
  • Locking on a non-final field: if it's ever reassigned, threads lock different objects.

So in practice my rule is: lock on a private final Object lock = new Object(), and nothing else. For this counter I'd just use an AtomicInteger.

30. Locks & Atomic Variables

BasicWhat are atomic variables and how does AtomicInteger work?

So atomic classes in java.util.concurrent.atomic, like AtomicInteger, AtomicLong, AtomicBoolean and AtomicReference, let you do read-modify-write operations on a single variable atomically without a lock. Under the hood they use a volatile field plus the CPU's compare-and-swap instruction.

AtomicInteger ai = new AtomicInteger(5);

ai.compareAndSet(5, 6);                    // true   was 5, now 6
ai.compareAndSet(5, 7);                    // false  it's 6, not 5; nothing changes
ai.get();                                  // 6
ai.updateAndGet(v -> v * 10);              // 60
ai.accumulateAndGet(3, Integer::sum);      // 63
ai.incrementAndGet();                      // 64

Conceptually, incrementAndGet is a retry loop: read the current value, compute value + 1, then CAS it in only if nobody changed it meanwhile; if someone did, read again and retry. On modern JVMs the increment actually compiles to a single atomic hardware instruction.

One thing about updateAndGet and accumulateAndGet (Java 8): the function may be called more than once if there's contention, so it must be side-effect free. Don't log or call a service inside it.

The limitation is that atomics only protect one variable. If I need to update a balance and a history list together, two atomics don't make that combination atomic. That's when I need a lock, or I put both fields into one immutable object and swap it with AtomicReference.

So in practice: atomics for counters, IDs, flags and one-time initialisation; locks for multi-field invariants.

IntermediateReentrantLock vs synchronized. When would you use ReentrantLock?

So both give mutual exclusion, reentrancy and the same visibility guarantees. synchronized is simpler and releases the lock automatically. ReentrantLock, from Java 5, is more flexible: you can try to lock with a timeout, lock interruptibly, choose fairness, and have multiple wait conditions. The cost is that you must unlock manually in a finally.

ReentrantLock lock = new ReentrantLock();

lock.lock();
try {
    // critical section
} finally {
    lock.unlock();        // forget this and the lock is held forever
}

// Things synchronized can't do:
if (lock.tryLock(100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)) { ... }   // give up instead of blocking forever
lock.lockInterruptibly();                               // can be cancelled while waiting
new ReentrantLock(true);                                // fair: longest waiter goes first
Condition notEmpty = lock.newCondition();               // several wait-sets per lock

When I tested tryLock(100 ms) from a second thread while the first held the lock, it returned false after the timeout instead of hanging. That's the main reason I'd pick it: in a service calling out under a lock, I'd rather fail fast and return an error than have request threads pile up forever.

synchronizedReentrantLock
ReleaseAutomaticManual, in finally
Timeout / tryNotryLock
Interruptible waitNolockInterruptibly
FairnessNoOptional
ConditionsOne (wait/notify)Many (newCondition)

On virtual threads: before Java 24, blocking inside synchronized pinned the virtual thread to its carrier, so many Java 21 codebases switched hot locks to ReentrantLock. Java 24 removed most of that pinning.

So in practice I default to synchronized for simple cases, and use ReentrantLock when I need timeouts, interruptibility, fairness or multiple conditions.

Interviewers look for: unlock in finally, and concrete features like tryLock and Condition.

IntermediateWhat is CAS (compare-and-swap)? What is the ABA problem?

So CAS is a single atomic CPU instruction that says: "if the value at this memory location is still X, set it to Y, and tell me whether it worked." It's the foundation of all lock-free code in Java: atomics, ConcurrentHashMap, ConcurrentLinkedQueue and most of java.util.concurrent.

A lock-free update is a retry loop built on CAS:

// roughly what AtomicInteger does for incrementAndGet
int prev, next;
do {
    prev = value.get();
    next = prev + 1;
} while (!value.compareAndSet(prev, next));   // someone changed it? try again

Compared with a lock, no thread is ever blocked or put to sleep; a thread that loses the race just retries. Under low to moderate contention that's faster. Under very high contention, threads can waste CPU spinning and retrying.

The ABA problem: CAS only checks the value, not the history. If a value goes A → B → A while my thread is between its read and its CAS, the CAS succeeds even though the state changed underneath. For numbers it rarely matters, but for a lock-free stack built from node references it can corrupt the structure. The fix is to CAS on a value plus a version stamp:

AtomicStampedReference<String> ref = new AtomicStampedReference<>("A", 0);
int stamp = ref.getStamp();              // I read "A" with stamp 0

ref.compareAndSet("A", "B", 0, 1);       // another thread: A -> B
ref.compareAndSet("B", "A", 1, 2);       // and back:       B -> A

ref.compareAndSet("A", "C", stamp, stamp + 1);   // false: value is A, but stamp is 2

So in practice I rarely write CAS loops by hand; I use atomics and concurrent collections that already do it correctly. But understanding CAS explains why they're fast and what their limits are.

Interviewers look for: the retry loop, "no blocking", and ABA with the stamped-reference fix.

IntermediateWhen would you use a ReadWriteLock or a StampedLock?

So a ReentrantReadWriteLock has two locks: many threads can hold the read lock at the same time, but the write lock is exclusive. It helps when reads vastly outnumber writes and each read takes some time, like a config cache or a lookup table that refreshes occasionally.

class Cache<K, V> {
    private final Map<K, V> map = new HashMap<>();
    private final ReadWriteLock rw = new ReentrantReadWriteLock();

    V get(K key) {
        rw.readLock().lock();
        try { return map.get(key); } finally { rw.readLock().unlock(); }
    }
    void put(K key, V value) {
        rw.writeLock().lock();
        try { map.put(key, value); } finally { rw.writeLock().unlock(); }
    }
}

Some caveats. You can downgrade (hold write, take read, release write) but you can't upgrade from read to write; trying deadlocks. And for very short reads like a single HashMap.get, the bookkeeping of the read lock can cost more than a plain lock. In fact, for this exact example I'd use a ConcurrentHashMap instead.

StampedLock (Java 8) adds an optimistic read: you read without locking at all, then validate that no write happened. If one did, you retry with a real read lock. That's very fast for read-heavy data like coordinates or small value objects. But it's not reentrant, doesn't support conditions, and is easy to misuse.

So in practice: ConcurrentHashMap or an immutable snapshot in a volatile field first; ReadWriteLock when reads are long and writes rare; StampedLock only after profiling shows lock contention on a read path.

AdvancedAtomicLong vs LongAdder. When is LongAdder better?

So AtomicLong is one variable that every thread CASes. Under heavy contention, most CAS attempts fail and retry, and the cache line bounces between CPU cores. LongAdder (Java 8) spreads the count across several internal cells, so threads mostly update different cells, and sum() adds them up when you read.

LongAdder adder = new LongAdder();
// two threads, 100,000 increments each:
adder.increment();
adder.sum();                // 200000

// typical use: per-key hit counters
ConcurrentHashMap<String, LongAdder> hits = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
hits.computeIfAbsent("/home", k -> new LongAdder()).increment();

The trade-off:

  • Writes: LongAdder scales much better when many threads increment at the same time.
  • Reads: sum() is slower, and it's not an atomic snapshot: if others are writing during the sum, you get a value that may not have existed at any single instant.
  • API: LongAdder has no compareAndSet or incrementAndGet. You can't use it to generate unique IDs.
  • Memory: more cells, more memory.

In a metrics library at work we counted requests per endpoint with AtomicLong, and under load the profiler showed the counters themselves as a hotspot. Switching to LongAdder removed it, because we write constantly and read only when the metrics endpoint is scraped.

So in practice: LongAdder for statistics that are written often and read rarely; AtomicLong when I need the exact current value, CAS, or sequence numbers.

Interviewers look for: contention on a single variable, striping across cells, and the non-atomic sum().

AdvancedHow do you wait for a condition with ReentrantLock? Show Condition in use.

So a Condition is the ReentrantLock version of wait/notify. await() releases the lock and waits; signal() or signalAll() wakes waiters, which reacquire the lock before continuing. The advantage over wait/notify is that one lock can have several conditions, so I can wake only the threads that care.

class Account {
    private final Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();
    private final Condition funds = lock.newCondition();
    private int balance;

    void withdraw(int amt) throws InterruptedException {
        lock.lockInterruptibly();
        try {
            while (balance < amt) funds.await();   // releases the lock while waiting
            balance -= amt;
        } finally { lock.unlock(); }
    }

    void deposit(int amt) {
        lock.lock();
        try {
            balance += amt;
            funds.signalAll();
        } finally { lock.unlock(); }
    }
}
// withdraw(50) blocks on an empty account; deposit(80) wakes it; balance ends at 30

Let me walk through it. The withdrawing thread takes the lock, sees there isn't enough money, and awaits, which releases the lock. The depositing thread can then take the lock, add money and signal. The withdrawer wakes, reacquires the lock, rechecks the while condition, and proceeds.

The same rules as wait apply: always await in a loop because of spurious wake-ups, and only call await or signal while holding the lock, or you get IllegalMonitorStateException.

The classic use of two conditions is a bounded buffer: notFull for producers and notEmpty for consumers. That's exactly how ArrayBlockingQueue is implemented inside the JDK.

So in practice, unless I'm building a synchronizer myself, I use ArrayBlockingQueue or Semaphore directly, which already wrap this pattern correctly.

31. Executors & Thread Pools

BasicWhy use a thread pool instead of creating a new Thread for every task?

So a platform thread is expensive: it maps to an OS thread, reserves around 1 MB of stack, and takes real time to create and tear down. A pool creates a fixed set of threads once and reuses them, and it gives me a place to control how much work runs at the same time.

Let me show you the difference in code:

// Per-request thread: unbounded, no reuse, no back-pressure
new Thread(() -> handle(request)).start();

// Pool: 8 reusable workers, extra tasks wait in a queue
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(8);
pool.submit(() -> handle(request));

With the first version, a traffic spike of 10,000 requests means 10,000 OS threads. The machine spends its time context-switching, memory runs out, and eventually I get OutOfMemoryError: unable to create native thread. With the pool, at most 8 run at once and the rest queue up.

The other benefits are separation of concerns (I submit tasks, the executor decides how they run), results and exceptions through Future, graceful shutdown, and metrics like active count and queue size.

So in practice I never call new Thread in application code. For CPU work I use a bounded pool. For blocking I/O on Java 21, I use virtual threads, which are cheap enough that pooling them is pointless.

BasicWhat's the difference between submit() and execute()?

So execute(Runnable) comes from the Executor interface and returns nothing. submit() comes from ExecutorService, accepts a Runnable or a Callable, and returns a Future. The difference that bites people is what happens to exceptions.

Let me show you with a quick example:

ExecutorService pool = Executors.newSingleThreadExecutor();

Future<?> f = pool.submit(() -> { throw new IllegalStateException("boom"); });
// nothing is printed: the exception is stored inside the Future
try { f.get(); }
catch (ExecutionException e) { System.out.println("get() -> " + e.getCause()); }
// get() -> java.lang.IllegalStateException: boom

pool.execute(() -> { throw new IllegalStateException("boom2"); });
// Exception in thread "pool-1-thread-1" java.lang.IllegalStateException: boom2
//     at ... (stack trace from the uncaught exception handler)

With submit, the task is wrapped in a FutureTask, which catches the exception and keeps it until someone calls get(). If nobody ever calls get(), the failure disappears silently. With execute, the exception escapes, the uncaught exception handler prints it, and the pool replaces that worker thread.

I've debugged exactly this: a nightly job "succeeded" for weeks while every task was failing, because we used submit and threw the futures away.

So in practice: fire-and-forget uses execute, or a try/catch with logging inside the task. If I need a result, I use submit and always call get().

Interviewers look for: "submit swallows exceptions until get()". It's the thing most candidates miss.

IntermediateExplain the ThreadPoolExecutor parameters. In what order does it use threads and the queue?

So the constructor takes core pool size, maximum pool size, keep-alive time, a work queue, a thread factory and a rejection handler. The part people get wrong is the order: it fills the core threads first, then the queue, and only creates extra threads up to the max once the queue is full.

Let me show you with a pool of core 2, max 4 and a queue of 2, where every task blocks so nothing finishes:

ThreadPoolExecutor pool = new ThreadPoolExecutor(
    2, 4, 30, TimeUnit.SECONDS,
    new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(2),
    new ThreadPoolExecutor.AbortPolicy());

// task 1: threads=1 queued=0
// task 2: threads=2 queued=0   core full
// task 3: threads=2 queued=1   goes to the queue
// task 4: threads=2 queued=2   queue full
// task 5: threads=3 queued=2   now an extra thread
// task 6: threads=4 queued=2   max reached
// task 7: rejected             RejectedExecutionException

The keep-alive time controls how long those extra threads sit idle before they're killed and the pool shrinks back to core size. The thread factory is where I set names like payment-worker-3, which makes thread dumps readable.

The consequence of this order is important. With an unbounded queue like LinkedBlockingQueue(), the queue is never full, so the pool never grows past core size. The max parameter does nothing.

So in practice I always use a bounded queue, name the threads, and pick the rejection policy deliberately.

Interviewers look for: core, then queue, then max, then reject. And "an unbounded queue makes maximumPoolSize useless".

IntermediateWhat happens when the queue is full and all threads are busy?

So the executor calls its RejectedExecutionHandler. There are four built-in policies, and choosing one is really a decision about how the system behaves under overload.

PolicyWhat it doesWhen I use it
AbortPolicy (default)Throws RejectedExecutionExceptionCaller can return 503 or retry later
CallerRunsPolicyRuns the task on the submitting threadNatural back-pressure: the producer slows down
DiscardPolicySilently drops the new taskRarely; only for truly optional work like metrics
DiscardOldestPolicyDrops the oldest queued task, retries the new oneWhen only the latest value matters, like price ticks

CallerRunsPolicy is my usual choice for internal pipelines. If a Kafka consumer thread submits work and the pool is saturated, the consumer thread runs the task itself, so it stops polling for a moment. That throttles intake without losing anything.

I can also write my own handler, for example to log, increment a "rejected" metric, and then throw. Rejection also happens after shutdown(), so seeing it in logs during a deploy is normal.

So in practice, rejection is a feature. It's the pool telling me I'm overloaded, and I'd rather see that than a queue quietly growing until the heap is gone.

IntermediateWhy can Executors.newFixedThreadPool() and newCachedThreadPool() be dangerous in production?

So both factory methods hide an unbounded resource. The fixed pool has an unbounded queue, and the cached pool has an unbounded number of threads. Under a traffic spike or a slow downstream, one fills the heap and the other fills the OS with threads.

Let me show you what they really create:

// newFixedThreadPool(10)
new ThreadPoolExecutor(10, 10, 0L, MILLISECONDS,
    new LinkedBlockingQueue<Runnable>());          // capacity Integer.MAX_VALUE

// newCachedThreadPool()
new ThreadPoolExecutor(0, Integer.MAX_VALUE, 60L, SECONDS,
    new SynchronousQueue<Runnable>());             // no queue: every task needs a thread

In a previous project we used newFixedThreadPool(20) to push notifications. The SMS provider slowed to 5 seconds per call, the queue grew to a few million tasks, each holding a message payload, and the service died with OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space. Nothing was rejected, so no alarm fired until it crashed.

The cached pool fails differently: if tasks block, every new task creates a new thread, and you hit unable to create native thread or the box grinds to a halt from context switching.

So in practice I construct ThreadPoolExecutor directly with a bounded ArrayBlockingQueue, named threads and an explicit rejection policy, and I export queue size as a metric. Many teams' static analysis rules flag the Executors factories for exactly this reason.

Interviewers look for: the specific hidden bound (queue vs thread count), plus a real failure story. That's what separates people who've run this in production.

AdvancedHow do you shut down an ExecutorService correctly?

So there are two methods: shutdown() stops accepting new tasks but lets queued and running ones finish, and shutdownNow() interrupts running tasks and returns the ones still in the queue. The correct pattern combines both with a timeout.

void stop(ExecutorService pool) {
    pool.shutdown();                                   // no new tasks
    try {
        if (!pool.awaitTermination(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)) {
            List<Runnable> dropped = pool.shutdownNow();   // interrupt workers
            log.warn("dropped {} tasks", dropped.size());
            pool.awaitTermination(10, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
        }
    } catch (InterruptedException e) {
        pool.shutdownNow();
        Thread.currentThread().interrupt();            // keep the interrupt flag
    }
}

// Java 19+: ExecutorService is AutoCloseable
try (var pool = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    pool.submit(task);
}   // close() = shutdown + wait for all tasks

Two things people miss. First, shutdownNow() only interrupts. A task that ignores interruption, like a busy loop that never checks Thread.interrupted(), keeps running. Second, if I never shut down a pool of non-daemon threads, the JVM can't exit, which is why a main method sometimes "hangs" after finishing.

In Spring I don't do this by hand. I declare the executor as a bean and the container calls shutdown on context close.

So in practice: shutdown, await with a timeout, then shutdownNow, and write tasks that respond to interruption.

AdvancedHow do you decide the size of a thread pool?

So it depends on whether the tasks are CPU-bound or spend most of their time waiting. For pure CPU work, about the number of cores. For I/O-heavy work, more, according to how long threads wait compared to how long they compute.

int cores = Runtime.getRuntime().availableProcessors();   // container-aware since Java 10

// CPU-bound (hashing, image resizing):   cores  (or cores + 1)
// Blocking I/O:  threads = cores * (1 + waitTime / computeTime)
//   e.g. 8 cores, 90 ms waiting on DB per 10 ms of CPU
//   8 * (1 + 90/10) = 80 threads

The formula is a starting point, not an answer. The real limit is often downstream. If the database connection pool has 20 connections, 80 threads that all need a connection just means 60 of them wait on the pool. So I size against the scarcest resource.

Then I measure. I load-test, watch CPU, latency percentiles and queue depth, and adjust. If CPU is at 30% and the queue is growing, the pool is too small or something downstream is slow. If CPU is at 100% with poor latency, the pool is too big for CPU work.

I also keep separate pools for separate workloads, the bulkhead pattern, so a slow report job can't starve the checkout requests.

So in practice, on Java 21 the I/O case is mostly solved by virtual threads plus a semaphore on the downstream resource, and I only hand-size pools for CPU work.

Interviewers look for: CPU vs I/O, the wait/compute ratio, "bounded by the downstream resource", and "then measure".

32. CompletableFuture

BasicWhat's the difference between Future and CompletableFuture?

So Future, from Java 5, is a handle you can only poll or block on with get(). CompletableFuture, from Java 8, can be chained with callbacks, combined with other futures, completed manually, and it has proper error handling. It's the difference between waiting for a result and describing what to do when it arrives.

// Future: the only way forward is to block
Future<User> f = pool.submit(() -> userRepo.find(42));
User u = f.get();                       // this thread waits
String name = u.name().toUpperCase();

// CompletableFuture: a pipeline, nothing blocks
CompletableFuture<String> name =
    CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> userRepo.find(42), pool)
        .thenApply(user -> user.name().toUpperCase())
        .exceptionally(ex -> "UNKNOWN");

With a plain Future I can't say "when this finishes, call that service", I can't wait for "all of these" or "the first of these" without writing loops, and I can't attach an error handler. I also can't complete it myself, which CompletableFuture.complete(value) allows, handy for adapting callback-based APIs.

It implements both Future and CompletionStage, so it still works anywhere a Future is expected.

So in practice, whenever I have to fan out to several services and merge the results, CompletableFuture is the tool. With virtual threads on Java 21, I sometimes just write plain blocking code instead, because it's easier to read and debug.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between thenApply and thenCompose?

So it's map versus flatMap. thenApply takes a function that returns a plain value. thenCompose takes a function that returns another CompletableFuture, and flattens it so I don't end up with a future inside a future.

CompletableFuture<User> findUser(long id) { ... }
CompletableFuture<Integer> fetchOrders(User u) { ... }   // also async

// thenApply with an async function: nested, awkward
CompletableFuture<CompletableFuture<Integer>> nested =
    findUser(42).thenApply(u -> fetchOrders(u));

// thenCompose: flat, chains the second call after the first
CompletableFuture<Integer> orders =
    findUser(42).thenCompose(u -> fetchOrders(u));
System.out.println(orders.join());          // 3

// thenApply is right for a plain transformation
CompletableFuture<String> upper =
    findUser(42).thenApply(u -> u.name().toUpperCase());
System.out.println(upper.join());           // ASHA

The rule I use: if the lambda returns a CompletableFuture, use thenCompose. If it returns a normal value, use thenApply. It's the same idea as Optional.map vs Optional.flatMap, or Stream.map vs flatMap.

And there's a third sibling: thenCombine runs two independent futures in parallel and merges them, whereas thenCompose is sequential because the second call needs the first result.

So in practice, seeing CompletableFuture<CompletableFuture<…>> in a code review is a signal that someone meant thenCompose.

Interviewers look for: the map/flatMap analogy, and knowing that compose is sequential while combine is parallel.

IntermediateHow do you handle exceptions in a CompletableFuture chain?

So there are three methods. exceptionally runs only on failure and returns a fallback value. handle always runs and gets both the value and the exception, so it can transform either. whenComplete always runs too, but it's for side effects like logging and doesn't change the result.

Let me show you with a quick example:

String r = CompletableFuture
    .supplyAsync(() -> { throw new IllegalStateException("db down"); })
    .thenApply(s -> s + "!")                          // skipped: previous stage failed
    .exceptionally(ex -> "fallback: " + ex.getCause().getMessage())
    .join();
System.out.println(r);                                // fallback: db down

CompletableFuture<Integer> failing = CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> 10 / 0);
failing.handle((v, ex) -> ex == null ? v : 0).join();          // 0
failing.whenComplete((v, ex) -> log.error("failed", ex));      // still failed afterwards

try { failing.join(); }
catch (CompletionException e) { System.out.println(e.getCause()); }
// java.lang.ArithmeticException: / by zero

Two details matter. First, a failure skips every thenApply/thenAccept after it until it reaches a handler, like a catch block further down. Second, the exception I receive is usually wrapped in a CompletionException, so I call getCause() to reach the real one. That's why the example prints getCause().getMessage().

join() throws the unchecked CompletionException, while get() throws the checked ExecutionException. That's the only real difference between them.

So in practice I put one exceptionally or handle near the end of the chain, unwrap the cause, and log with whenComplete. The classic bug is a chain with no handler at all, where the error just vanishes.

Interviewers look for: the three methods and how they differ, "failures skip the stages after them", and unwrapping CompletionException.

IntermediateYou need data from three services for one page. How do you call them in parallel?

So I start all three calls immediately with supplyAsync on my own executor, then combine them. For two futures I use thenCombine. For N futures, allOf and then read each result with join(), which won't block because they're all done.

ExecutorService io = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor();

CompletableFuture<Profile> profile = supplyAsync(() -> profileApi.get(id), io);
CompletableFuture<List<Order>> orders = supplyAsync(() -> orderApi.recent(id), io);
CompletableFuture<Integer> points  = supplyAsync(() -> loyaltyApi.points(id), io)
                                        .exceptionally(ex -> 0);    // optional data

CompletableFuture<Page> page = CompletableFuture
    .allOf(profile, orders, points)
    .thenApply(v -> new Page(profile.join(), orders.join(), points.join()));

// Two futures: thenCombine
price.thenCombine(stock, (p, s) -> p + " / " + s).join();   // "99.0 / in stock"

The total latency becomes roughly the slowest call rather than the sum. If the three calls take 100, 150 and 80 ms, the page takes about 150 ms instead of 330.

A few decisions I make explicitly: which failures are fatal and which get a default (loyalty points fall back to 0 here), and a timeout on each call so one slow service can't hold the page. There's also anyOf for "first one wins", which I've used to hit two replicas and take whichever answers first.

So in practice this fan-out pattern is the most common reason I reach for CompletableFuture at all.

IntermediateWhich thread runs the callbacks? What's the problem with the common pool?

So supplyAsync without an executor runs on ForkJoinPool.commonPool(). The non-async callbacks like thenApply run on whichever thread completes the previous stage, or on the calling thread if it's already complete. The ...Async variants always hand the callback to an executor.

// Risky: blocking HTTP call on the shared common pool
supplyAsync(() -> httpClient.send(req, ofString()));

// Better: my own executor for blocking work
supplyAsync(() -> httpClient.send(req, ofString()), ioExecutor)
    .thenApplyAsync(this::parse, cpuExecutor);

The common pool has only cores - 1 threads, and it's shared by the whole JVM, including parallel streams. If I run blocking I/O on it, a handful of slow calls can starve every other part of the application that uses it. On a 2-core container, that pool has a single thread.

The other surprise is that thenApply's thread isn't predictable. If the future is already complete when I attach the callback, it runs right there on my thread, which might be a request thread I didn't want to block with heavy work.

So in practice I always pass an executor to supplyAsync for anything that blocks, and I use the Async variants with an executor when I care where a stage runs.

Interviewers look for: "the common pool is shared and small, so never block on it".

AdvancedHow do you add a timeout to a CompletableFuture?
