So since Java 9 there are two methods. orTimeout fails the future with a TimeoutException if it isn't done in time. completeOnTimeout completes it with a default value instead. Before Java 9, you needed a scheduled executor to do this by hand.

// Fall back to a cached value after 100 ms
String price = slowPriceCall()
    .completeOnTimeout("cached", 100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)
    .join();                                     // "cached"

// Treat slowness as a failure
try {
    slowCall().orTimeout(100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS).join();
} catch (CompletionException e) {
    System.out.println(e.getCause().getClass().getSimpleName());   // TimeoutException
}

// Blocking alternative: bound only the wait, not the work
future.get(100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS);

The important thing to say is that a timeout completes the future, but it does not stop the underlying task. The thread keeps running the slow HTTP call until it returns. cancel(true) doesn't help either, because CompletableFuture ignores the interrupt flag. So I also set a timeout on the actual client, for example the HTTP client's request timeout or the JDBC query timeout, so the resource is really freed.

So in practice: completeOnTimeout for optional data, orTimeout plus a fallback for required data, and a client-level timeout underneath both.

33. Concurrent Collections

BasicWhy use ConcurrentHashMap instead of Hashtable or Collections.synchronizedMap?

So Hashtable and synchronizedMap put one lock around the whole map, so only one thread can read or write at a time. ConcurrentHashMap lets reads run with no lock at all and lets writes to different buckets run in parallel. Under load, that's a big throughput difference.

Hashtable / synchronizedMapConcurrentHashMap
LockingOne lock for everythingLock-free reads, per-bucket locks for writes
Iteration while modifyingFail-fast: ConcurrentModificationExceptionWeakly consistent, never throws
Atomic compound opsMust lock externallycompute, merge, putIfAbsent
null keys/valuesHashtable: no. synchronizedMap: yesNo

The iteration point is a real trap with synchronizedMap. Each method is synchronized, but iterating isn't a single method call, so I have to wrap the loop in synchronized (map) { … } myself or risk an exception.

The flip side is that size() on a ConcurrentHashMap is an estimate while other threads are writing, and iteration may or may not show updates that happen during the loop. For caches and counters that's fine.

So in practice Hashtable is legacy and I never use it. ConcurrentHashMap is the default for any map shared across threads.

IntermediateHow does ConcurrentHashMap work internally? What changed between Java 7 and Java 8?

So in Java 7 it was split into 16 segments by default, each one a small hash table with its own ReentrantLock. Writes locked one segment, so at most 16 writers could proceed at once. Java 8 removed segments: it's now a single Node[] table like HashMap, using CAS for empty buckets and synchronized on just the head node of a bucket otherwise.

Let me walk through a Java 8 put:

  • Empty bucket: insert the new node with a single CAS, no lock at all.
  • Bucket in use: synchronized (firstNode), then append or replace. Only that one bucket is locked.
  • Long bucket: past 8 nodes, it turns into a red-black tree, like HashMap.
  • Resize: several threads help move buckets. A moved bucket is marked with a ForwardingNode, so readers know to look in the new table.

Reads take no lock. The node's val and next fields are volatile, so a reader always sees a fully published node.

The size counter is also clever. Instead of one contended counter, it uses a baseCount plus an array of CounterCells, the same idea as LongAdder, so heavy concurrent puts don't all fight over one number.

So the net effect of Java 8 is lock granularity at the bucket level instead of 16 segments, less memory, and better concurrency. The concurrencyLevel constructor argument still exists but is now only a sizing hint.

Interviewers look for: segments in 7; CAS plus synchronized on the bucket head in 8; lock-free reads via volatile; treeification. Mentioning the LongAdder-style counter is a bonus.

IntermediateWhy doesn't ConcurrentHashMap allow null keys or values?

So it's about ambiguity. In a plain HashMap, get returning null can mean "no key" or "key mapped to null", and I can tell them apart with containsKey. In a concurrent map, another thread can change the map between those two calls, so the answer would be meaningless. Banning null removes the ambiguity.

Map<String, String> hm = new HashMap<>();
hm.put("a", null);
System.out.println(hm.get("a") + " " + hm.containsKey("a") + " " + hm.get("b"));
// null true null      -- same return value, two different meanings

Map<String, String> chm = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();
chm.put("a", null);    // NullPointerException
chm.get(null);         // NullPointerException

With the null ban, get returning null has exactly one meaning: absent, at that moment. That's what makes atomic methods like putIfAbsent, computeIfAbsent and merge reliable, because they all use null to mean "no mapping". Doug Lea, who wrote it, explained it this way, and he also considered nulls in maps a design mistake generally.

When I need to represent "known to be missing", for example to cache a negative lookup, I store a sentinel or Optional.empty() instead of null.

So in practice this also means migrating a HashMap to a ConcurrentHashMap can throw NPEs if the old code stored nulls, so I check for that first.

Interviewers look for: "you can't safely do get-then-containsKey in a concurrent map". Just saying "because it throws NPE" isn't enough.

IntermediateWhat's wrong with this counter, even though the map is a ConcurrentHashMap? 
map.put("hits", map.get("hits") + 1);

So each call is thread-safe on its own, but the combination isn't atomic. Two threads can both read 5, both compute 6, and both write 6, so one increment is lost. A thread-safe collection doesn't make a sequence of calls thread-safe.

Let me show you by running both versions on 8 threads with 100,000 increments:

// racy
racy.put("hits", racy.get("hits") + 1);
// atomic
safe.merge("hits", 1, Integer::sum);

// racy=75935 safe=100000     (racy number changes every run)

The fix is to use the map's atomic compound methods, which lock the bucket for the whole read-modify-write:

  • merge(key, 1, Integer::sum) for counters.
  • computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new ArrayList<>()) for "get or create", instead of a get, null check and put.
  • compute(key, (k, v) -> …) for arbitrary updates.

For a very hot counter I go one step further and store a LongAdder as the value: map.computeIfAbsent(key, k -> new LongAdder()).increment(). That spreads contention across cells.

One caution: the function passed to compute runs while holding the bucket lock, so it must be short and must never modify the same map, or it can deadlock or throw IllegalStateException.

So in practice, whenever I see get followed by put on a shared map, I replace it with one atomic method.

IntermediateWhen would you use CopyOnWriteArrayList?

So I use it when reads vastly outnumber writes and I need to iterate safely without locking. Every write copies the whole backing array. Every iterator works on the snapshot it started with, so it never throws ConcurrentModificationException and never needs a lock.

List<String> plain = new ArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
for (String s : plain) if (s.equals("a")) plain.add("d");
// ConcurrentModificationException

List<String> cow = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>(List.of("a", "b", "c"));
for (String s : cow) { if (s.equals("a")) cow.add("d"); System.out.print(s + " "); }
// a b c          (the iterator sees the old snapshot)
System.out.println(cow);
// [a, b, c, d]

The textbook use case is a list of listeners or observers. Listeners are registered a few times at startup, but events fire thousands of times a second, and each event iterates the list. A listener can even unregister itself during the callback without breaking the loop. I've also used it for a small set of routing rules reloaded every few minutes and read on every request.

It's a bad choice when writes are frequent or the list is big: adding to a 100,000-element list copies 100,000 references each time, so a loop of adds is O(n²). And iterators don't support remove().

So in practice: small list, rare writes, constant iteration. Otherwise I use a ConcurrentLinkedQueue or a normal list with a lock.

IntermediateWrite a producer-consumer using a BlockingQueue.

So a BlockingQueue does all the hard parts for me: put blocks when the queue is full, take blocks when it's empty, and all the locking and signalling is inside. I don't write any wait/notify. To stop the consumer cleanly I send a "poison pill".

public class ProducerConsumer {
    private static final String POISON = "__DONE__";

    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        BlockingQueue<String> queue = new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(2);   // bounded

        Thread producer = Thread.ofPlatform().start(() -> {
            try {
                for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
                    queue.put("order-" + i);            // blocks when full
                    System.out.println("produced order-" + i);
                }
                queue.put(POISON);
            } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
        });

        Thread consumer = Thread.ofPlatform().start(() -> {
            try {
                while (true) {
                    String item = queue.take();         // blocks when empty
                    if (item.equals(POISON)) break;
                    Thread.sleep(50);                   // slow consumer
                    System.out.println("  consumed " + item);
                }
            } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
        });

        producer.join();
        consumer.join();
        System.out.println("done");
    }
}

When I run it, the producer races ahead by about two items, the capacity, and then has to wait for the slow consumer: "produced order-3", "consumed order-1", "produced order-4"… That's back-pressure. The bounded queue stops a fast producer from filling the heap.

With several consumers, I send one poison pill per consumer. I also restore the interrupt flag in the catch, rather than swallowing it.

So in practice this is the same pattern a ThreadPoolExecutor uses internally, and the same shape as a Kafka topic, just inside one JVM.

Interviewers look for: a bounded queue, put/take rather than offer/poll, a clean shutdown, and correct interrupt handling. If they then ask for a wait/notify version, use while, not if, around wait().

AdvancedCountDownLatch vs CyclicBarrier vs Semaphore: when do you use each?

So a CountDownLatch lets one or more threads wait until N events have happened, and it's single-use. A CyclicBarrier makes N threads wait for each other at a meeting point and resets for the next round. A Semaphore doesn't wait for events at all: it limits how many threads can be inside a section at once.

// Latch: main waits for 3 services to start
CountDownLatch ready = new CountDownLatch(3);
for (String s : new String[]{"db", "cache", "queue"})
    Thread.ofVirtual().start(() -> { System.out.println(s + " up"); ready.countDown(); });
ready.await();                      // "all services up, count=0"

// Barrier: 3 workers finish each round together; reusable
CyclicBarrier barrier = new CyclicBarrier(3, () -> System.out.println("-- round done --"));
// each worker: for (round 1..2) barrier.await();
// prints "-- round done --" twice

// Semaphore: at most 2 calls to a rate-limited API at a time
Semaphore permits = new Semaphore(2);
permits.acquire();
try { callApi(); } finally { permits.release(); }
// 10 tasks on virtual threads -> "max concurrent = 2"
CountDownLatchCyclicBarrierSemaphore
Who waitsWaiters, for other threadsThe parties, for each otherThreads, for a free permit
ReusableNoYes, resets each roundYes
Typical useStartup, test "go" signalParallel simulations in phasesConnection or rate limits

The latch's counting side and waiting side are different threads. With the barrier, the same threads count and wait. That's the clearest way to tell them apart.

So in practice I use Semaphore the most, especially with virtual threads, to cap how many of thousands of virtual threads hit a database at once. Latches show up mostly in tests. For anything more complex I'd look at Phaser.

34. Virtual Threads & Deadlocks

BasicWhat is a deadlock? What conditions cause one?

So a deadlock is when two or more threads each hold a lock the other needs, and each waits forever for the other to release it. Nothing crashes. The threads just stop, and the requests that depend on them hang.

Four conditions have to hold at the same time, the Coffman conditions:

  • Mutual exclusion: a lock can be held by only one thread.
  • Hold and wait: a thread holds one lock while waiting for another.
  • No preemption: a lock can't be taken away; the owner must release it.
  • Circular wait: T1 waits for T2, which waits for T1.

Break any one and deadlock is impossible. In Java the practical one to break is circular wait, by always acquiring locks in the same global order. The second practical one is hold-and-wait, broken with tryLock and a timeout, so a thread gives up its first lock if it can't get the second.

It's worth separating two neighbours. A livelock is threads that keep reacting to each other and retrying without progress, which can happen with naive tryLock retries. Starvation is a thread that never gets a turn because others always win.

So in practice, most deadlocks I've seen weren't two obvious synchronized blocks. They were a lock held while calling out to code that took another lock, like a listener callback or a synchronized DAO.

IntermediateWrite code that deadlocks, then fix it.

So the classic case is a bank transfer. Thread 1 transfers A to B and locks A then B. Thread 2 transfers B to A and locks B then A. Each gets its first lock and waits forever for the second.

// Deadlocks: lock order depends on the direction of the transfer
void transfer(Account from, Account to, long amount) {
    synchronized (from) {
        synchronized (to) {
            from.balance -= amount;
            to.balance += amount;
        }
    }
}
// thread 1: transfer(a, b, 1)   holds a, waits for b
// thread 2: transfer(b, a, 1)   holds b, waits for a

The fix is a global lock order. I always lock the account with the smaller id first, whatever the direction:

void transfer(Account from, Account to, long amount) {
    Account first  = from.id < to.id ? from : to;
    Account second = from.id < to.id ? to : from;
    synchronized (first) {
        synchronized (second) {
            from.balance -= amount;
            to.balance += amount;
        }
    }
}
// 10,000 transfers each way on 4 threads: a=1000 b=1000, no hang

The alternative fix, when there's no natural ordering, is ReentrantLock.tryLock with a timeout. If I can't get the second lock within 50 ms, I release the first and return false or retry after a random back-off. That breaks hold-and-wait. The random back-off matters, otherwise two threads can retry in lockstep and livelock.

So in practice: consistent ordering first, tryLock when ordering isn't possible, and above all, keep critical sections small and never call unknown code while holding a lock.

Interviewers look for: a deadlock you can explain line by line, and a fix that names which Coffman condition it breaks.

IntermediateHow do you detect a deadlock in a running application?

So the JVM detects Java-level deadlocks for me. I take a thread dump, and at the end it prints a "Found one Java-level deadlock" section naming the threads, the locks each one holds and the ones they're waiting for.

$ jcmd <pid> Thread.print        # or: jstack <pid>, or kill -3 <pid>

Found one Java-level deadlock:
=============================
"t1":
  waiting to lock monitor 0x... (object 0x..., a java.lang.Object),
  which is held by "t2"
"t2":
  waiting to lock monitor 0x... (object 0x..., a java.lang.Object),
  which is held by "t1"

I can also check from inside the application with ThreadMXBean, which is how I've built a health check that fails when threads are stuck:

ThreadMXBean mx = ManagementFactory.getThreadMXBean();
long[] ids = mx.findDeadlockedThreads();       // null if none
if (ids != null)
    for (ThreadInfo info : mx.getThreadInfo(ids))
        System.out.println(info.getThreadName() + " waits for " + info.getLockName()
            + " held by " + info.getLockOwnerName());
// t1 waits for java.lang.Object@119d7047 held by t2
// t2 waits for java.lang.Object@16b98e56 held by t1

The symptoms that make me look are requests hanging with low CPU and a thread pool whose active count is stuck at its max. I take two or three dumps a few seconds apart. If the same threads are BLOCKED on the same monitors every time, it's a deadlock or heavy contention.

So in practice I name threads through the thread factory, because "payment-worker-3 waits for a lock held by audit-writer-1" is solvable in minutes, and "pool-7-thread-3" is not.

IntermediateWhat are virtual threads, and how are they different from platform threads?

So virtual threads, final in Java 21, are threads managed by the JVM rather than the OS. A platform thread is a thin wrapper over an OS thread. A virtual thread is a small heap object that the JVM mounts onto a platform "carrier" thread only while it's running. When it blocks on I/O, it unmounts and the carrier runs another virtual thread.

try (var executor = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    IntStream.range(0, 10_000).forEach(i -> executor.submit(() -> {
        Thread.sleep(Duration.ofSeconds(1));    // simulated blocking I/O
        return i;
    }));
}
// 10,000 tasks took ~1s
Platform threadVirtual thread
Backed byOne OS thread, for its whole lifeBorrows a carrier only while running
Stack~1 MB reserved up frontGrows on the heap, starts tiny
How manyThousandsMillions
PoolingYes, they're expensiveNever; one per task
DaemonConfigurableAlways daemon

The key point is that virtual threads aren't faster. A CPU-bound task runs at the same speed. They give scale for blocking workloads. I can write simple blocking code, one thread per request, and still handle tens of thousands of concurrent requests without reactive programming. In Spring Boot 3.2+ it's one property: spring.threads.virtual.enabled=true.

So in practice I use them for I/O-bound services, and I still cap access to scarce resources with a semaphore or connection pool, because a million virtual threads can very easily overwhelm a database.

Interviewers look for: "scale, not speed", "don't pool them", and knowing they were finalised in Java 21 (preview in 19 and 20).

AdvancedWhat is pinning with virtual threads, and how do you avoid it?

So pinning is when a virtual thread blocks but can't unmount from its carrier, so the carrier OS thread is blocked too. In Java 21 that happens in two cases: blocking inside a synchronized block or method, and blocking inside a native method. There are only as many carriers as cores by default, so a few pinned threads can stall the whole application.

static final Object LOCK = new Object();
static final ReentrantLock RLOCK = new ReentrantLock();

static void pinned() throws InterruptedException {
    synchronized (LOCK) { Thread.sleep(10); }          // pins the carrier
}
static void notPinned() throws InterruptedException {
    RLOCK.lock();
    try { Thread.sleep(10); } finally { RLOCK.unlock(); }   // unmounts normally
}

// java -Djdk.tracePinnedThreads=short ...
// VirtualThread[#20]/runnable@ForkJoinPool-1-worker-1 reason:MONITOR
//     Pinning.pinned(Pinning.java:7) <== monitors:1
// (nothing is reported for the ReentrantLock version)

To find it, I run with -Djdk.tracePinnedThreads=short in testing, or watch the JFR event jdk.VirtualThreadPinned in production. To fix it, I replace synchronized around blocking calls with a ReentrantLock, and I upgrade libraries. Older JDBC drivers and connection pools were full of synchronized, and most have since switched.

A short synchronized block that doesn't block, like incrementing a field, is fine. Pinning only matters when it's held across I/O or a sleep.

Worth mentioning: JDK 24 (JEP 491) reworked monitors so synchronized no longer pins. On 21, though, it's a real concern.

Interviewers look for: the two causes, the carrier being blocked, ReentrantLock as the fix, and the tracing flag. Knowing it's fixed in 24 shows you follow the platform.

AdvancedWhen should you NOT use virtual threads?

So virtual threads help when threads spend most of their time waiting. They don't help CPU-bound work, and a few older patterns work against them.

  • CPU-bound tasks: image processing or number crunching gains nothing, since only as many can run as there are cores. A fixed pool sized to cores, or parallel streams, is the right tool.
  • Heavy ThreadLocal use: caching an expensive object per thread made sense with 200 pooled threads. With a million short-lived virtual threads, each one gets its own copy and the cache is useless. Java 21 has ScopedValue in preview for passing context instead.
  • Pooling them: putting virtual threads in a fixed pool of 50 to limit concurrency brings back the old limits. I limit the resource with a Semaphore instead.
  • Pinning-heavy code: on Java 21, libraries that block inside synchronized can serialize everything onto a few carriers.
// Wrong way to limit DB load
ExecutorService limited = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(50, Thread.ofVirtual().factory());

// Right way: unlimited virtual threads, limited resource
Semaphore db = new Semaphore(50);
try (var ex = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()) {
    ex.submit(() -> { db.acquire(); try { return query(); } finally { db.release(); } });
}

So in practice: typical REST services that call databases and other services are the sweet spot. For batch computation I stay with platform thread pools.

Part 6 — The JVM Inside

35. JVM Memory: Heap, Stack & Metaspace

BasicWhat's the difference between the heap and the stack?

So every thread has its own stack, which holds one frame per method call with that call's local variables, parameters and partial results. The heap is shared by all threads and holds every object and array. The stack is freed automatically when a method returns. The heap is cleaned by the garbage collector.

void placeOrder() {
    int qty = 3;                          // stack: primitive value lives in the frame
    Order order = new Order("A-17", qty); // stack: the reference 'order'
                                          // heap:  the Order object itself
    process(order);                       // new frame pushed; same heap object shared
}                                         // frame popped; Order becomes garbage
                                          // if nothing else references it
StackHeap
HoldsFrames: locals, parameters, referencesObjects, arrays, instance fields
SharedNo, one per threadYes, all threads
Freed byMethod returnGarbage collector
Size flag-Xss (per thread)-Xms / -Xmx
When fullStackOverflowErrorOutOfMemoryError

Because locals live on a thread's own stack, they're naturally thread-safe. Anything on the heap that more than one thread can reach needs synchronization.

One nuance: the JIT's escape analysis can avoid heap-allocating an object that never leaves a method, by scalar-replacing its fields. But that's an optimization. The language model is still "objects live on the heap".

So in practice, when someone says "the object is on the stack", I'd correct it gently: the reference is on the stack, the object is on the heap.

BasicWhat is Metaspace, and what's stored there?

So Metaspace holds class metadata: the internal structure of each loaded class, its methods and bytecode, field descriptions, the runtime constant pool and so on. It replaced PermGen in Java 8, and the big change is that it lives in native memory, outside the heap, and grows automatically by default.

DataWhere it lives (Java 8+)
Class metadata, method bytecodeMetaspace (native memory)
JIT-compiled machine codeCode cache (native)
String pool (interned strings)Heap (moved there in Java 7)
Static variablesHeap, inside the Class object
Objects and arraysHeap

The PermGen problem was its fixed size. Apps that loaded many classes hit OutOfMemoryError: PermGen space and people had to tune -XX:MaxPermSize. With Metaspace, the limit is the machine's memory unless I set -XX:MaxMetaspaceSize, which I do in containers so a leak fails fast instead of taking the whole pod down.

Metaspace is only freed when a class's class loader becomes unreachable. That's why the classic Metaspace leak is redeploying a web app in Tomcat many times: if one old object keeps a reference to the old class loader, every class it loaded stays in memory.

So in practice, if I see OutOfMemoryError: Metaspace, I suspect a class loader leak or runaway dynamic class generation, like proxies or lambdas created in a loop by a library.

Interviewers look for: "replaced PermGen in Java 8, native memory", and that the string pool and statics are not in Metaspace.

IntermediateStackOverflowError vs OutOfMemoryError: what causes each?

So a StackOverflowError means one thread's stack ran out of room for new frames, almost always because of recursion that's too deep or never stops. An OutOfMemoryError means the JVM couldn't allocate memory in some area, most commonly the heap, even after garbage collection. Both are Errors, not Exceptions.

static int factorial(int n) { return n * factorial(n - 1); }  // no base case

try { factorial(5); }
catch (StackOverflowError e) { System.out.println("StackOverflowError"); }
System.out.println("thread still alive, carrying on");
// Printed after tens of thousands of frames (the exact depth varies by run and -Xss)

// java -Xmx64m
List<byte[]> hold = new ArrayList<>();
while (true) hold.add(new byte[1024 * 1024]);
// java.lang.OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space

Other common stack overflow causes are accidental ones: a toString() that prints a parent which prints its children which print the parent, or bidirectional JPA entities serialized to JSON. The fix is a base case, iteration instead of recursion, or breaking the cycle. Raising -Xss only helps with legitimately deep, finite recursion.

The OutOfMemoryError message tells me which area failed: Java heap space, Metaspace, GC overhead limit exceeded (the JVM spends almost all its time in GC and recovers almost nothing), unable to create native thread, or Direct buffer memory.

So in practice I never catch OutOfMemoryError to "carry on", because the JVM's state is unreliable afterwards. I let it die, with -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError set, and the orchestrator restarts it.

IntermediateWhat are the main JVM memory flags, and how do they behave in containers?

So the essentials are -Xms for initial heap, -Xmx for maximum heap, -Xss for each thread's stack, and -XX:MaxMetaspaceSize. But in containers I usually prefer percentage flags so the heap follows the container's memory limit.

# Fixed sizes
java -Xms2g -Xmx2g -Xss512k -XX:MaxMetaspaceSize=256m -jar app.jar

# Container-friendly (Java 10+ reads cgroup limits)
java -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0 -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError \
     -XX:HeapDumpPath=/dumps -jar app.jar

# Check what the JVM actually picked
java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep -E "MaxHeapSize|UseG1GC"

Without any flags, the default max heap is only 25% of the container's memory, which wastes most of a 2 GB pod. So I set MaxRAMPercentage to about 70–75%.

I don't set it to 100%, because the heap is not the JVM's only memory. There's Metaspace, the code cache, thread stacks (200 threads × 1 MB is 200 MB), direct buffers used by Netty or NIO, and GC bookkeeping. I've seen a pod get OOM-killed by Kubernetes with exit code 137 while the heap looked perfectly healthy, because heap plus everything else exceeded the limit. That kill leaves no Java error at all.

Setting -Xms equal to -Xmx avoids heap resizing pauses in long-running servers.

So in practice: percentage-based heap, room for native memory, a heap dump on OOM, and -XX:NativeMemoryTracking=summary when I need to find where non-heap memory went.

AdvancedHow is the heap organised into generations, and why?

So most collectors split the heap into a young generation and an old generation, based on the observation that most objects die young. Request objects, temporary strings and iterators live for milliseconds. Collecting the young generation often, and the old generation rarely, is much cheaper than scanning everything.

Young generation                      | Old generation
[ Eden ][ Survivor S0 ][ Survivor S1 ] | [ long-lived objects: caches, pools, singletons ]

1. new objects are allocated in Eden (fast: bump a pointer in a thread-local buffer)
2. Eden fills -> minor GC: live objects copied to a survivor space, Eden wiped
3. each survival increments the object's age
4. age reaches the tenuring threshold (up to 15) -> promoted to old gen
5. very large objects may go straight to old gen (G1: "humongous" regions)

A minor GC only has to find the few survivors and copy them. The dead objects cost nothing, because Eden is simply reset. That's why allocating many short-lived objects in Java is cheap.

The trick that makes this work is the card table. When an old object points to a young one, a write barrier records it, so a minor GC can treat those old-to-young references as roots without scanning the whole old generation.

G1 keeps the generational idea but divides the heap into many equal regions, each tagged as Eden, survivor, old or humongous, instead of fixed contiguous areas.

So in practice, trouble starts with premature promotion: medium-lived objects, like a big batch held for a few seconds, survive enough minor GCs to be promoted, fill the old generation, and trigger expensive old-gen collections.

36. Garbage Collection

BasicHow does the garbage collector decide what to collect?

So it uses reachability, not reference counting. It starts from a set of GC roots and follows every reference. Anything it can reach is live. Everything else is garbage, even objects that still point to each other.

The GC roots are:

  • Local variables and parameters in every thread's active stack frames.
  • Static fields of loaded classes.
  • Active threads themselves.
  • JNI references, and objects used as monitors in synchronized.
class Node { Node other; }

void demo() {
    Node a = new Node();
    Node b = new Node();
    a.other = b;
    b.other = a;       // a cycle
    a = null;
    b = null;          // no root reaches either one
}                      // both are collectable, despite pointing at each other

That cycle is exactly what reference counting gets wrong: each object still has a count of 1, so it would never be freed. Tracing from roots handles cycles naturally.

The basic algorithm is mark (walk from the roots and mark live objects), sweep (reclaim the rest), and often compact or copy (move survivors together to avoid fragmentation). Modern collectors do much of the marking concurrently while the application runs.

So in practice this is why a "memory leak" in Java means objects that are still reachable but no longer needed, like entries in a static map. The GC is doing its job correctly. It just can't know I've finished with them.

Interviewers look for: "GC roots and reachability", a list of what counts as a root, and "cycles are collected".

BasicCan you force garbage collection in Java?

So no. System.gc() and Runtime.getRuntime().gc() are only a request. The JVM is allowed to ignore them, and there's no guarantee of when or whether a collection happens or which objects get freed.

System.gc();       // "please consider collecting now"

// It can be disabled entirely:
java -XX:+DisableExplicitGC -jar app.jar

// For diagnostics, from outside the process:
jcmd <pid> GC.run

In HotSpot, by default System.gc() actually does trigger a full, stop-the-world collection, which is exactly why calling it in production code is a bad idea. I once found a library calling it after every batch export, and each call caused a pause of about 800 ms. Adding -XX:+DisableExplicitGC fixed the latency spikes. The exception is when System.gc() is used deliberately to free direct buffer memory, so I check for that before disabling it.

Setting a reference to null doesn't force anything either. It only makes the object eligible. For a local variable that's about to go out of scope, it's pointless.

So in practice the only places I call System.gc() are demos and tests, like checking that a WeakReference gets cleared. If memory is a problem, I tune the heap or fix what holds the references.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between a minor GC, a major GC and a full GC?

So a minor GC collects only the young generation. It's frequent and usually takes milliseconds. A major GC collects the old generation. A full GC collects the whole heap, young and old, usually with compaction and a long stop-the-world pause. The terms "major" and "full" are often used loosely, so I describe what was collected rather than rely on the name.

Minor (young)Full
ScopeEden + survivorsEntire heap (+ Metaspace cleanup)
FrequencyOften, seconds apartShould be rare or never
PauseShort, proportional to live young objectsLong, proportional to live heap
Triggered byEden fullOld gen full, promotion failure, Metaspace, System.gc()

With G1 there's a middle ground: mixed collections that collect the young generation plus a selection of old regions with the most garbage. G1 is designed so the old generation is cleaned incrementally this way, and a full GC is a fallback when it can't keep up.

java -Xlog:gc -jar app.jar
[12.345s][info][gc] GC(41) Pause Young (Normal) (G1 Evacuation Pause) 512M->96M(1024M) 6.123ms
[98.765s][info][gc] GC(87) Pause Full (G1 Compaction Pause) 1010M->900M(1024M) 1840.2ms

That second line is a red flag: a full GC that took almost 2 seconds and only freed 110 MB. The live data nearly fills the heap. It's either too small or leaking.

So in practice I treat frequent full GCs as a symptom to investigate, not a tuning parameter.

IntermediateExplain strong, soft, weak and phantom references.

So they're four levels of how strongly a reference keeps an object alive. A strong reference, a normal variable, prevents collection. A soft reference is cleared only when memory is tight. A weak reference is cleared at the next GC once nothing strong points to the object. A phantom reference never returns the object at all: it only tells me, through a queue, that the object has been collected.

Object strong = new Object();
WeakReference<Object> weakToStrong = new WeakReference<>(strong);
WeakReference<Object> weak = new WeakReference<>(new Object());
SoftReference<byte[]> soft = new SoftReference<>(new byte[1024]);
ReferenceQueue<Object> q = new ReferenceQueue<>();
PhantomReference<Object> phantom = new PhantomReference<>(new Object(), q);

phantom.get();                 // null, always
System.gc();
weakToStrong.get() != null;    // true  (still strongly reachable)
weak.get();                    // null  (only weakly reachable: cleared)
soft.get() != null;            // true  (memory is fine, kept)
q.remove(1000) == phantom;     // true  (enqueued after collection)
TypeCleared whenUsed for
StrongNever, while reachableEverything normal
SoftBefore an OOM, under memory pressureMemory-sensitive caches (with care)
WeakNext GCWeakHashMap, metadata attached to objects
PhantomAfter collection, via the queueCleanup of native resources (Cleaner)

Soft-reference caches sound great but behave unpredictably: they tend to be cleared all at once under pressure, causing a burst of cache misses exactly when the system is struggling. I prefer a size-bounded cache like Caffeine.

So in practice I use weak references through WeakHashMap or ThreadLocal internals, and phantom references through java.lang.ref.Cleaner (Java 9), which is the replacement for finalize().

Interviewers look for: the order of strength, one real use per type, and "Cleaner replaced finalize".

IntermediateWhat is a WeakHashMap, and when would you use it?

So a WeakHashMap holds its keys through weak references. When a key object is no longer strongly reachable anywhere else, the GC clears it and the map drops the whole entry on its next operation. It's useful for attaching extra data to objects I don't own, without keeping those objects alive.

Map<Object, String> cache = new WeakHashMap<>();
Object key = new Object();
cache.put(key, "metadata");
cache.put("literal", "never cleared");
System.out.println("before: " + cache.size());          // before: 2

key = null;                  // drop the only strong reference
System.gc();
System.out.println("after:  " + cache.size() + " " + cache.values());
// after:  1 [never cleared]

Notice the "literal" entry survives: string literals are held strongly by the string pool, so they never become weakly reachable. The same is true for small cached Integer values. That's the first pitfall.

The second pitfall is a value that references its own key. The map holds values strongly, so the value keeps the key alive and the entry is never removed. The third is that it compares keys with equals, which is surprising for a map that's really about identity.

It's also not thread-safe, so I'd wrap it with Collections.synchronizedMap when sharing it.

So in practice, I use it rarely and deliberately, for example per-class-loader or per-session metadata. It's not a general cache: it evicts by reachability, not by size or age, so for caching I use Caffeine with maximumSize and expireAfterWrite.

AdvancedG1 vs ZGC: how do they differ and how do you choose a collector?

So G1 is the default since Java 9. It balances throughput and pause times, targets a pause goal (200 ms by default) and does its evacuation, the copying, in stop-the-world pauses. ZGC (production since Java 15, generational in 21) does almost all its work concurrently, including moving objects, so pauses stay under a millisecond regardless of heap size, at the cost of some throughput and extra CPU.

CollectorPausesBest forFlag
SerialStop-the-world, single threadTiny heaps, 1-CPU containers-XX:+UseSerialGC
ParallelStop-the-world, many threadsBatch jobs: maximum throughput-XX:+UseParallelGC
G1 (default)Tens of ms, goal-drivenMost services, heaps of 4–32 GB-XX:MaxGCPauseMillis=200
ZGCUnder 1 msLatency-critical, very large heaps-XX:+UseZGC -XX:+ZGenerational
ShenandoahVery short, concurrentSame niche as ZGC (not in all builds)-XX:+UseShenandoahGC

ZGC's trick is coloured pointers and load barriers. Metadata bits live in each reference, and every time the application loads a reference, a small barrier checks whether the object has moved and fixes the pointer. That's how it relocates objects while the application is running.

In Java 21, ZGC's generational mode needs -XX:+ZGenerational. Later releases made it the default and removed the non-generational mode. Without generations, ZGC has to trace the whole heap every cycle, which costs more CPU.

So in practice I start with G1 and only tune the heap size and pause goal. I'd switch to ZGC when p99 latency is dominated by GC pauses, like a trading API or a large in-memory cache, and I measure with GC logs before and after. Throughput-only batch jobs are where Parallel GC still wins.

Interviewers look for: G1 is the default since 9; ZGC is concurrent with sub-millisecond pauses; the trade-off is throughput and CPU; and "measure before switching".

AdvancedWhat's wrong with finalize(), and what replaced it?

So finalize() was meant to run cleanup before an object is collected, but it's unreliable and harmful. There's no guarantee when it runs, or if it runs at all. It was deprecated in Java 9 and marked for removal in Java 18 (JEP 421).

The problems I'd list:

  • No timing guarantee: file handles or sockets can stay open for minutes, until some future GC. Under low memory pressure, maybe never.
  • Slower GC: finalizable objects need at least two GC cycles to be freed, and a single finalizer thread processes them. If it falls behind, the queue grows until OutOfMemoryError.
  • Resurrection: finalize can store this in a static field and bring the object back to life.
  • Exceptions ignored: anything thrown in it is silently swallowed.
// Replacement 1: deterministic cleanup
try (var in = Files.newInputStream(path)) {
    ...
}   // closed right here, always

// Replacement 2: safety net for native resources (Java 9)
private static final Cleaner CLEANER = Cleaner.create();
NativeBuffer(long address) {
    this.cleanable = CLEANER.register(this, () -> free(address)); // must not capture 'this'
}

Cleaner is built on phantom references, so the object can't be resurrected. The cleanup action must not reference the object itself, otherwise the object stays reachable forever.

So in practice: AutoCloseable with try-with-resources is the real answer. Cleaner is only a backstop in case someone forgets to call close().

37. Class Loading

BasicWhat is a class loader? What are the built-in ones?

So a class loader finds the bytecode for a class by name, and turns it into a Class object inside the JVM. Classes are loaded lazily, the first time they're needed, not all at startup. Since Java 9 there are three built-in loaders arranged in a parent chain.

System.out.println(String.class.getClassLoader());
// null        -> bootstrap loader (native, has no Java object)

System.out.println(java.sql.Connection.class.getClassLoader().getName());
// platform

ClassLoader app = Loaders.class.getClassLoader();
System.out.println(app.getName() + " -> parent " + app.getParent().getName()
    + " -> parent " + app.getParent().getParent());
// app -> parent platform -> parent null
LoaderLoads
BootstrapCore modules: java.base (java.lang, java.util…). Written in native code, shown as null.
Platform (was Extension before Java 9)Other Java SE modules like java.sql
Application (system)My code and JARs on the classpath or module path

Loading has three phases: loading (find the bytes), linking (verify the bytecode, allocate static fields with default values, resolve symbolic references) and initialization (run static initializers and assign static fields).

A class's identity at runtime is its name plus the loader that loaded it. The same com.acme.User loaded by two different loaders is two different classes, and casting between them fails with a ClassCastException that looks absurd: "User cannot be cast to User".

So in practice I rarely touch class loaders directly, but that last point explains many strange errors in app servers and plugin systems.

IntermediateWhat is the parent delegation model, and why does it exist?

So when a class loader is asked for a class, it first asks its parent, which asks its own parent, all the way up to bootstrap. Only if the parents can't find it does the loader try to load it itself. The request goes up first, and loading comes back down.

// Simplified ClassLoader.loadClass
protected Class<?> loadClass(String name, boolean resolve) {
    Class<?> c = findLoadedClass(name);            // 1. already loaded?
    if (c == null) {
        try {
            c = parent.loadClass(name, false);     // 2. ask the parent first
        } catch (ClassNotFoundException e) {
            c = findClass(name);                   // 3. only then look myself
        }
    }
    return c;
}

It exists for two reasons. Security: if I put my own java.lang.String on the classpath, the application loader asks bootstrap first, bootstrap finds the real one, and mine is never used. Core classes can't be replaced. Consistency: each class is loaded once, by the highest loader that can see it, so everyone shares the same String class and there are no duplicate types.

When I write a custom class loader, I override findClass, not loadClass, so I keep delegation for free.

Some systems deliberately invert it. Tomcat's web-app loader checks the WAR's own WEB-INF/lib before its parent, so each app can use its own library versions. It still delegates java.* to the parent.

So in practice, delegation is why "which JAR did this class actually come from?" is a real debugging question. -verbose:class or MyClass.class.getProtectionDomain().getCodeSource() answers it.

Interviewers look for: "parent first", the security reason (you can't override java.lang), and knowing that containers like Tomcat invert it for isolation.

IntermediateClassNotFoundException vs NoClassDefFoundError: what's the difference?

So ClassNotFoundException is a checked exception thrown when code asks for a class by name at runtime, via Class.forName or loadClass, and it isn't there. NoClassDefFoundError is an error thrown when a class that was present at compile time can't be loaded or initialized at runtime.

// 1. ClassNotFoundException: dynamic lookup, driver JAR missing
try { Class.forName("com.mysql.cj.jdbc.Driver"); }
catch (ClassNotFoundException e) { System.out.println("ClassNotFoundException: " + e.getMessage()); }
// ClassNotFoundException: com.mysql.cj.jdbc.Driver

// 2. NoClassDefFoundError: the class exists, but its static init failed
static class Config {
    static final int PORT = Integer.parseInt(System.getenv().getOrDefault("APP_PORT", "abc"));
}
for (int i = 1; i <= 2; i++) {
    try { System.out.println(Config.PORT); }
    catch (Throwable t) { System.out.println("attempt " + i + ": " + t.getClass().getName()); }
}
// attempt 1: java.lang.ExceptionInInitializerError
// attempt 2: java.lang.NoClassDefFoundError

The second case is the one that fools people. The first time, the static initializer throws, and I get ExceptionInInitializerError with the real cause. The JVM marks the class as failed, so every later use gets NoClassDefFoundError: Could not initialize class Config. If I only look at the latest logs, I see the error, check that the JAR is there, and get confused. The root cause is the first error.

The other common cause of NoClassDefFoundError is a dependency present at compile time but missing at runtime, like a Maven scope set to provided by mistake, or two versions of a library conflicting.

So in practice: CNFE means check the classpath and the name string. NCDFE means scroll back for an ExceptionInInitializerError, then check mvn dependency:tree.

Interviewers look for: checked exception vs error, dynamic lookup vs compile-time reference, and the static-initializer failure case.

IntermediatePredict the output: when does a class get initialized? 
static class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("Parent static"); }
    { System.out.println("Parent instance"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("Parent constructor"); }
}
static class Child extends Parent {
    static final int CONST = 42;
    static int counter = 7;
    static { System.out.println("Child static"); }
    { System.out.println("Child instance"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("Child constructor"); }
}

System.out.println(Child.CONST);
System.out.println("---");
System.out.println(Child.counter);
System.out.println("---");
new Child();
System.out.println("---");
new Child();

So the output is:

42
---
Parent static
Child static
7
---
Parent instance
Parent constructor
Child instance
Child constructor
---
Parent instance
Parent constructor
Child instance
Child constructor

Let me walk through it. Child.CONST is a static final compile-time constant, so the compiler inlines 42 into the caller and Child isn't initialized at all. Reading Child.counter is a real static field access, which triggers initialization, and a superclass is always initialized first: "Parent static", then "Child static".

Static blocks run once per class loader, so the second new Child() doesn't print them again. For each instance, the order is parent first: parent's instance initializers, parent constructor, then the child's.

A class is initialized on: new, a static method call, a non-constant static field access, Class.forName, a subclass being initialized, or being the main class. Declaring a variable of the type, or Child.class, doesn't do it.

So in practice, the constant-inlining rule has a real consequence: if I change a public static final constant in a library and don't recompile the callers, they keep the old value.

AdvancedWhen would you write a custom class loader, and how do class loader leaks happen?

So custom loaders exist for isolation and dynamic code: app servers giving each web app its own classes, plugin systems loading JARs at runtime, hot reload in dev tools like Spring DevTools, and loading classes generated at runtime or from encrypted or remote sources.

// Load a plugin JAR, isolated from other plugins
URL jar = Path.of("plugins/report.jar").toUri().toURL();
try (URLClassLoader loader = new URLClassLoader(new URL[]{jar},
                                                getClass().getClassLoader())) {
    Class<?> cls = loader.loadClass("com.acme.ReportPlugin");
    Plugin p = (Plugin) cls.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance();  // shared interface
    p.run();
}

The shared Plugin interface has to come from the parent loader. If the plugin JAR also bundled its own copy of Plugin, the cast would fail, because they'd be two different classes.

The leak comes from this chain: every object knows its class, every class knows its loader, and a loader references every class it loaded. So one surviving object from a plugin or old web app keeps the whole loader, and all its classes and Metaspace, alive. The classic culprits I've seen are:

  • A JDBC driver registered in DriverManager by the web app and never deregistered.
  • A ThreadLocal value on a container thread that outlives the app.
  • A thread started by the app, like a timer, that's never stopped.

After a few redeploys, you see OutOfMemoryError: Metaspace. In a heap dump, I look for multiple instances of the web-app class loader and follow the path to GC roots of the old ones.

So in practice, that's one reason the industry moved to one app per JVM in a container: redeploying means a new process, so this whole class of leak disappears.

38. Performance, Profiling & Memory Leaks

BasicHow can Java have memory leaks if it has a garbage collector?

So the GC frees unreachable objects. A Java memory leak is objects that are still reachable, because some reference chain from a GC root still leads to them, but that the program will never use again. The GC can't read my intentions, so it has to keep them.

record Request(String id, byte[] payload) {}
static final Map<String, Request> INFLIGHT = new HashMap<>();   // GC root: static

static void handle(Request r) {
    INFLIGHT.put(r.id(), r);
    // ... process ...
    // missing: INFLIGHT.remove(r.id());
}
// after 1000 finished requests: INFLIGHT.size() == 1000, and it keeps growing

The usual suspects I check are:

  • Static collections and caches without eviction.
  • Listeners and callbacks registered and never removed.
  • ThreadLocals on pooled threads that are never cleared.
  • Unclosed resources: streams, connections, result sets, which also leak native memory.
  • Non-static inner classes and lambdas that capture the outer object and outlive it.
  • Mutable keys in a HashMap whose hash changes, so the entry can never be found and removed.

The symptom is a sawtooth heap graph where the low points after each GC keep rising, ending in more frequent GCs, latency spikes and finally OutOfMemoryError.

So in practice, any cache I write has a maximum size or TTL, any "register" has a matching "unregister", and resources go in try-with-resources.

IntermediateHow can ThreadLocal cause a memory leak or a data bug in a thread pool?

So a ThreadLocal value lives as long as the thread. In a pool, threads live for the whole application, so values set during one request stay on that thread after the request ends. That's a leak if the values are large, and a data bug if the next task on the same thread reads the previous user's data.

static final ThreadLocal<StringBuilder> BUF = ThreadLocal.withInitial(StringBuilder::new);
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(1);

pool.submit(() -> BUF.get().append("user=alice")).get();
pool.submit(() -> BUF.get().toString()).get();          // "user=alice"  (leaked into next task)

pool.submit(() -> {
    try { BUF.get().append("x"); }
    finally { BUF.remove(); }                            // always clean up
}).get();
pool.submit(() -> BUF.get().toString()).get();          // ""

Internally, each thread has a ThreadLocalMap whose keys are weak references to the ThreadLocal object, but whose values are strong. So even if the ThreadLocal itself is gone, the value stays until that slot happens to be cleaned up. And in an app server, if that value's class came from a web app's class loader, the whole loader leaks too.

In a real incident I saw, a security context stored in a ThreadLocal wasn't cleared on an error path, and a later request on the same Tomcat thread briefly ran with the previous user's tenant id. That's a data leak, not only a memory leak.

So in practice: every set has a remove() in a finally, ideally in one filter or interceptor. On Java 21 with virtual threads, ScopedValue (preview) avoids the problem because the value is bound only for a scope.

Interviewers look for: "the thread outlives the request", remove() in finally, and weak key with strong value.

IntermediateHow would you find a memory leak in production?

So I work in three steps: confirm it's really a leak, capture a heap dump, and find what's holding the memory. A rising heap on its own isn't a leak. A rising heap after full GCs is.

1. Confirm. I look at GC logs or a metrics dashboard (Micrometer, Grafana) for old-gen usage after each collection. If the floor keeps climbing over hours, and GCs get more frequent while freeing less, it's a leak.

# Always on in production, so a crash leaves evidence
-XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError -XX:HeapDumpPath=/dumps -Xlog:gc*:file=gc.log

# Quick look: which classes have the most instances (live objects only)
jcmd <pid> GC.class_histogram | head -20

# Full dump (pauses the app; take one instance out of the load balancer first)
jcmd <pid> GC.heap_dump /dumps/app.hprof

# Low overhead continuous option: JFR with old object sampling
jcmd <pid> JFR.start duration=30m settings=profile filename=/dumps/leak.jfr

2. Compare. Two class histograms taken 30 minutes apart are often enough. If byte[] and Request counts both grew by 200,000, I have a strong suspect.

3. Analyse. I open the dump in Eclipse MAT and look at the dominator tree and the "Leak Suspects" report, which show the objects that retain the most memory. Then I use "path to GC roots, excluding weak references" on a suspect object to see the chain that keeps it alive, like static RequestTracker.INFLIGHT → HashMap → Request. That chain points straight at the line of code.

In my last project this found a metrics tag built from a URL containing user ids, which created a new meter per user. The dominator tree showed the meter registry holding 1.4 GB.

So in practice: dump on OOM always enabled, histograms to find the suspect, and MAT's path to GC roots to find the cause. Then I write a test that reproduces it before fixing it.

Interviewers look for: a concrete sequence of tools (GC logs, jcmd, heap dump, MAT dominator tree, path to GC roots) and awareness that a heap dump pauses the JVM.

IntermediateA Java service is at 100% CPU. How do you troubleshoot it?

So first I figure out whether the CPU is burned by my code or by the garbage collector, then I find the exact threads and stack traces responsible. The core technique is matching the OS's per-thread CPU usage with a thread dump.

# 1. Which threads are hot? (Linux; -H shows threads, TIDs are decimal)
top -H -p <pid>
#   PID  %CPU  COMMAND
# 25347  99.0  java

# 2. Thread dump, then find that thread
jcmd <pid> Thread.print > dump.txt
# Java 21 prints nid in decimal:
"order-matcher" #20 [25347] daemon prio=5 ... cpu=1688.35ms ... nid=25347 runnable
   java.lang.Thread.State: RUNNABLE
	at Spin.lambda$main$0(Spin.java:3)
# Older JDKs print nid in hex: convert with  printf '%x\n' 25347  -> 6303

# 3. Is it GC? Check GC activity
jstat -gcutil <pid> 1000       # old gen near 100% and FGC climbing = GC thrashing

I take three dumps a few seconds apart. If the same thread is RUNNABLE in the same method every time, that's my hot spot. Typical causes I've seen: an infinite loop from a bad exit condition, a regex with catastrophic backtracking on user input, a HashMap used unsafely from multiple threads, busy-wait polling loops, and huge JSON serialization.

If the hot threads are named like GC Thread#0 or G1 Conc#0, the CPU problem is really a memory problem: the heap is nearly full and the JVM keeps collecting. Then I switch to the memory leak process.

For anything subtler than one stuck thread, I use a profiler: JFR (jcmd <pid> JFR.start) with JDK Mission Control, or async-profiler to get a flame graph, both safe for production.

So in practice: top -H, thread dump, match the thread ID, repeat the dump to confirm, rule out GC, then profile if needed.

Interviewers look for: the top -H plus thread dump technique, several dumps rather than one, and checking GC before blaming application code.

AdvancedHow does the JIT compiler work, and why does Java get faster after warm-up?

So the JVM starts by interpreting bytecode, counts how often each method and loop runs, and compiles hot code to native machine code. Since Java 8 it uses tiered compilation: first C1, a fast compiler with light optimisation, and then, for really hot code, C2, which produces highly optimised code based on the profile collected at runtime.

Tier 0: interpreter                         (collects profile)
Tier 3: C1 compiled + full profiling        (after ~200 calls / loop iterations)
Tier 4: C2 fully optimised                  (after ~thousands of calls)

java -XX:+PrintCompilation -jar app.jar     # watch methods being compiled

C2 can optimise better than a static compiler in some cases, because it sees real behaviour. It inlines small methods, removes checks it can prove unnecessary, uses escape analysis to avoid allocating objects that don't escape, and makes speculative assumptions, like "this interface call always hits the same class". If an assumption breaks, say a second implementation gets loaded, it deoptimises back to the interpreter and recompiles later.

That's why a freshly started service has slow first requests and a latency graph that settles after a minute or two. In Kubernetes I've handled this by sending warm-up traffic before the readiness probe passes. For startup-critical cases there's CDS/AppCDS for faster class loading, and GraalVM native image, or CRaC, for near-instant startup.

So in practice, warm-up is also why naive benchmarks lie: timing a method in a simple loop measures the interpreter, then the compiler, then possibly code that C2 deleted entirely.

AdvancedHow do you benchmark Java code correctly?

So I use JMH, the Java Microbenchmark Harness from the OpenJDK team. Hand-written timing loops are almost always wrong because of JIT warm-up, dead code elimination and constant folding. JMH handles all of that for me.

// Naive: misleading
long t = System.nanoTime();
for (int i = 0; i < 1_000_000; i++) compute(i);   // result unused: C2 may delete it
System.out.println(System.nanoTime() - t);

// JMH
@BenchmarkMode(Mode.AverageTime)
@OutputTimeUnit(TimeUnit.NANOSECONDS)
@Warmup(iterations = 5) @Measurement(iterations = 5) @Fork(2)
@State(Scope.Thread)
public class ConcatBench {
    String a = "order-", b = "42";

    @Benchmark
    public String plus() { return a + b; }            // returned: can't be eliminated

    @Benchmark
    public void builder(Blackhole bh) {
        bh.consume(new StringBuilder(a).append(b).toString());
    }
}

What JMH does: warm-up iterations so I measure compiled code, several forks (separate JVMs) so one lucky JIT decision doesn't skew results, Blackhole and return values to stop dead code elimination, and @State fields instead of constants so the compiler can't fold the whole calculation at compile time.

Then I read the error margin, not just the average. If two results overlap within the error, there's no real difference.

And microbenchmarks answer narrow questions. For "is my service fast enough", I load-test the whole system with Gatling or k6 and profile with JFR or async-profiler under that load. The bottleneck is usually I/O, a missing index or lock contention, not the microsecond detail.

So in practice: profile first to find what's actually slow, JMH to compare alternatives for that hot spot, and a load test to confirm the improvement end to end.

Interviewers look for: JMH by name, and at least two of the pitfalls: warm-up, dead code elimination, constant folding, forking.

Part 7 — Modern & Practical Java

39. Modern Java: 8 to 21

BasicWhat are the big features in Java 8, 11, 17 and 21, and which ones have you actually used?

So I think of it as four LTS releases, each with one headline theme. Java 8 brought functional programming, 11 was mostly a clean-up plus the HTTP client, 17 made data modelling nicer with records and sealed types, and 21 brought virtual threads and pattern matching together.

ReleaseWhat I'd name
Java 8 (2014)Lambdas, streams, Optional, default methods, java.time, CompletableFuture
Java 11 (2018)var in lambdas (var itself is 10), HttpClient, String.isBlank/strip/lines/repeat, Files.readString, run a single file with java App.java
Java 17 (2021)Records (16), sealed classes, text blocks (15), switch expressions (14), pattern matching for instanceof (16), helpful NullPointerExceptions, strong encapsulation of JDK internals
Java 21 (2023)Virtual threads, pattern matching for switch, record patterns, sequenced collections (getFirst, reversed)

In day-to-day work, streams and java.time are everywhere in my code. Records replaced most of our Lombok @Value DTOs when we moved to 17. On the 21 upgrade we switched our blocking HTTP fan-out service to Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor(), and that let us drop a hand-tuned thread pool of 200 threads without rewriting anything to reactive.

So in practice I always tie a feature to a problem it solved for me, not just list release notes.

Interviewers look for: real usage, not a memorised list. Knowing which version a feature landed in (records in 16, final in the 17 LTS) is a nice bonus.

BasicWhat is a record, and how is it different from a normal class?

So a record is a transparent carrier for immutable data. You declare the components once and the compiler gives you a canonical constructor, private final fields, accessors, and equals, hashCode and toString based on all components. Records were final in Java 16.

Let me show you with a quick example.

record Money(long cents, String currency) {
    Money {                                   // compact constructor: validation
        if (cents < 0) throw new IllegalArgumentException("negative");
        Objects.requireNonNull(currency);
    }
    Money plus(Money o) { return new Money(cents + o.cents, currency); }
}

Money a = new Money(500, "USD");
a.plus(new Money(250, "USD"));        // Money[cents=750, currency=USD]
a.equals(new Money(500, "USD"));      // true, value-based equality

The differences from a regular class: a record is implicitly final, it can't extend another class (it already extends java.lang.Record), and it can't declare extra instance fields. It can implement interfaces, have static fields, and add methods. Accessors are cents(), not getCents(), which matters for some older frameworks.

One catch: records are only shallowly immutable. If a component is a List, I copy it in the compact constructor with List.copyOf.

So in practice I use records for DTOs, API responses, map keys and value objects. I use a class when I need mutable state, inheritance, or JPA entities, because JPA needs a no-arg constructor and mutable fields.

Interviewers look for: "shallow immutability" and why records don't work as JPA entities.

BasicWhat does var do? What are the pros and cons?

So var, added in Java 10, is local variable type inference. The compiler works out the static type from the initializer. It's still fully statically typed. Nothing is dynamic at runtime, and var isn't a keyword, it's a reserved type name.

var names = new ArrayList<String>();          // ArrayList<String>
var byDept = new HashMap<String, List<Employee>>();   // saves a lot of noise
for (var entry : byDept.entrySet()) { ... }

var x;                 // compile error: no initializer
var n = null;          // compile error: can't infer
var list = List.of();  // List<Object>, probably not what you wanted
var total = service.compute();   // what type is this? reader has to go look

The pros: less repetition with long generic types, cleaner loops and try-with-resources, and names become more important, which pushes people to write good ones. The cons: it can hide the type when the right-hand side is a method call, it programs to the concrete type (ArrayList instead of List), and it's only allowed for locals, not fields, parameters or return types.

So in practice my rule is: use var when the type is obvious from the right-hand side, like new or a literal, and write the type out when it's a method call whose return type isn't obvious.

IntermediateWhat are text blocks and switch expressions, and why are they better than what we had?

So text blocks (Java 15) are multi-line string literals in triple quotes, and switch expressions (Java 14) let a switch return a value with arrow labels and no fall-through. Both remove a whole class of small bugs.

Let me show you with a quick example.

var json = """
        {
          "name": "Asha",
          "role": "dev"
        }
        """;
// the indentation of the closing """ decides how much leading space is stripped

int hours = switch (day) {
    case "SAT", "SUN" -> 0;
    default -> {
        int base = 8;
        yield base;          // yield returns a value from a block
    }
};

With text blocks I no longer write "{\n" + " \"name\"..." with escaped quotes. They're great for JSON in tests, SQL, and HTML templates. The compiler strips the common indentation, so the code stays nicely indented.

With the old switch statement, forgetting a break silently fell through to the next case. The arrow form never falls through, one arm can match several labels, and because it's an expression the compiler forces me to cover every case, or add a default, before I can assign the result.

So in practice I use the arrow switch everywhere now, even as a statement.

IntermediateWhat are sealed classes and when would you use one?

So a sealed class or interface (Java 17) lists exactly which types are allowed to extend it. It sits between final, which allows no subclasses, and a normal open type, which allows any. The payoff is that the compiler knows the full set of subtypes, so a switch over them can be checked for exhaustiveness.

Let me show you with a quick example.

sealed interface Shape permits Circle, Square, Rect { }
record Circle(double r) implements Shape { }
record Square(double side) implements Shape { }
record Rect(double w, double h) implements Shape { }

static double area(Shape s) {
    return switch (s) {                 // no default needed: compiler knows all cases
        case Circle c -> Math.PI * c.r() * c.r();
        case Square sq -> sq.side() * sq.side();
        case Rect(double w, double h) -> w * h;
    };
}
// area(new Rect(2, 3)) = 6.0

Each permitted subtype has to say what it is: final, sealed again, or non-sealed to reopen that branch. Records are implicitly final, so they fit perfectly.

The real use case is modelling a closed set of outcomes. In my last project a payment call returned sealed interface PaymentResult permits Approved, Declined, NeedsThreeDS. When product added a fourth case, every switch that didn't handle it stopped compiling, so we found every place to update in one build instead of in production.

So in practice: sealed plus records gives Java proper algebraic data types. I use it for results, commands and events, not for things plugins should extend.

Interviewers look for: the link between sealed types and exhaustive switches. That's the whole point, not just "restricting inheritance".

IntermediateExplain pattern matching for switch and record patterns in Java 21.

So pattern matching lets a case test the type of a value and bind it to a variable in one step. Java 16 did this for instanceof, and Java 21 brought it to switch, with guards using when, explicit case null, and record patterns that take a record apart.

static String describe(Object o) {
    return switch (o) {
        case null -> "null";
        case Integer i when i > 100 -> "big int " + i;
        case Integer i -> "int " + i;
        case String s -> "string of length " + s.length();
        default -> "something else";
    };
}
// describe(150) = "big int 150", describe(7) = "int 7", describe(null) = "null"

if (obj instanceof String s && s.length() > 3) {   // s is in scope here
    System.out.println(s.toUpperCase());
}

A few rules I keep in mind. Order matters: a more specific case must come before a more general one, or the compiler reports it as dominated. So Integer i when i > 100 goes above plain Integer i. Without case null, a switch on a null still throws NullPointerException, like before. And over a sealed type, no default is needed.

Record patterns go one level deeper: case Rect(double w, double h) matches and pulls the components out, and they can be nested.

So in practice this replaces long if/else instanceof chains and most uses of the Visitor pattern.

AdvancedYour team is on Java 11. Make the case for moving to 21. What do you gain, and what might break?

So the gains are real on both the language and the runtime side, and the risks are mostly about libraries that relied on JDK internals. I'd pitch it as mostly a dependency upgrade project, not a code rewrite.

What we gain:

  • Virtual threads: blocking I/O code scales to thousands of concurrent requests with no reactive rewrite.
  • Language: records, sealed types, pattern matching and text blocks mean less boilerplate and more compile-time safety.
  • Runtime: better G1 and ZGC (generational ZGC in 21), faster startup, lower memory, helpful NPE messages.
  • Support: Spring Boot 3 needs Java 17 minimum, so staying on 11 blocks framework upgrades too.

What can break:

  • Since JDK 16/17, internals like sun.misc and private fields of JDK classes are strongly encapsulated. Old versions of Lombok, Mockito, ByteBuddy and some serialization libraries fail with InaccessibleObjectException until you upgrade them.
  • Removed things: the Nashorn JS engine, the Security Manager is deprecated, and --illegal-access is gone.
  • Build plugins (compiler, surefire, jacoco) need newer versions.

In my last team we did it in steps: bump the build plugins and libraries on 11, then compile and test on 21 in CI while still targeting 11, fix the warnings, then switch the runtime image, and only then start using new language features.

So in practice: upgrade dependencies first, run the full test suite on the new JDK, and roll out one service at a time.

Interviewers look for: strong encapsulation as the main migration pain, and a staged plan instead of a big-bang switch.

40. I/O, NIO & Serialization

BasicWhat's the difference between byte streams and character streams?

So byte streams, InputStream and OutputStream, move raw bytes. Character streams, Reader and Writer, move chars and handle the encoding for you. The bridge between them is InputStreamReader and OutputStreamWriter, and that's where the charset gets decided.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// binary data: images, zip files, anything that isn't text
try (InputStream in = new BufferedInputStream(new FileInputStream("logo.png"))) { ... }

// text: always say which charset you mean
try (BufferedReader r = new BufferedReader(
        new InputStreamReader(new FileInputStream("users.csv"), StandardCharsets.UTF_8))) {
    String line;
    while ((line = r.readLine()) != null) { ... }
}

// modern shortcut (NIO.2), UTF-8 by default
List<String> lines = Files.readAllLines(Path.of("users.csv"));

The bug I've actually hit with this: someone used new FileReader(file), which before Java 18 used the platform default charset. It worked on every Mac, then a Windows server read the same UTF-8 file as Windows-1252 and names with accents came out garbled. Java 18 made UTF-8 the default everywhere, but I still pass the charset explicitly.

So in practice: bytes for binary, readers and writers for text, always with an explicit charset, always wrapped in a buffer, and always in try-with-resources.

BasicWhat is serialization? What are Serializable and serialVersionUID for?

So serialization turns an object graph into a byte stream so it can be stored or sent over the network, and deserialization rebuilds it. Serializable is a marker interface with no methods. It just tells ObjectOutputStream that the class agrees to be serialized, otherwise you get NotSerializableException.

class User implements Serializable {
    private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;
    String name;
    transient String password;
}

try (var out = new ObjectOutputStream(new FileOutputStream("user.bin"))) {
    out.writeObject(new User("asha", "s3cret"));
}

serialVersionUID is the version stamp of the class. It gets written into the stream, and on read the JVM compares it with the class it has loaded. If they differ, it throws InvalidClassException.

If I don't declare it, the JVM computes one from the class structure: fields, methods, interfaces. So adding a harmless helper method changes the ID, and every object stored with the old version can't be read anymore. That's why I always declare it explicitly. Then I decide when a change really is incompatible, and compatible changes like adding a field just get default values on read.

A few more rules: every field must itself be serializable or transient, static fields aren't serialized, and the first non-serializable superclass must have a no-arg constructor, because it gets called on read.

Interviewers look for: what happens when you don't declare the UID: a generated value that changes with harmless edits.

BasicWhat does the transient keyword do?

So transient marks a field that Java serialization should skip. When the object is read back, that field gets its type's default value: null, 0 or false. The field initializer and constructor don't run.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class User implements Serializable {
    private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;
    String name;
    transient String password;
    transient int loginCount = 5;
    User(String name, String password) { this.name = name; this.password = password; }
}

User u = roundTrip(new User("asha", "s3cret"));   // write then read back
System.out.println(u.name + " " + u.password + " " + u.loginCount);
// asha null 0

The part people get wrong is loginCount. It's initialised to 5, but it comes back as 0, not 5. Deserialization doesn't call the constructor of a serializable class, so field initializers never run.

I use transient for three things: secrets like passwords and tokens, derived or cached values I can recompute (like a cached hash), and fields that can't be serialized at all, like a Thread, a socket or a logger. If I need a transient field restored, I rebuild it in a private readObject method.

So in practice: transient means "not part of the saved state", and I plan how it gets rebuilt.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between java.io and java.nio?

So java.io is stream-oriented and blocking: one thread reads from a stream, and it blocks until data arrives. java.nio (Java 1.4) is buffer- and channel-oriented, and it supports non-blocking I/O with a Selector, so one thread can watch many connections. Then NIO.2 in Java 7 added Path and Files, the modern file API.

java.iojava.nio
ModelStreams, one byte or char at a timeChannels read into and write from ByteBuffers
BlockingAlways blockingCan be non-blocking, with a Selector
Threads for 10k socketsAbout one per connectionA few event-loop threads
ExtrasSimple, easy to wrap with decoratorsMemory-mapped files, zero-copy transferTo, file locks, WatchService

In application code I rarely touch channels and selectors directly. Netty, which sits under Spring WebFlux, gRPC and many other servers, is built on NIO selectors, and that's where it matters. For files, I use Files.readString, Files.lines, Files.walk and Files.copy every day, because they have better error messages than the old File class, which returns false instead of telling you why a delete failed.

With virtual threads in Java 21 the picture shifted again. Simple blocking code on virtual threads now scales like NIO, so for a normal service I'd write blocking code rather than hand-roll a selector loop.

Interviewers look for: blocking vs non-blocking and the selector idea, plus the fact that NIO.2 Files replaced File.

IntermediateHow would you process a 10 GB log file in Java without running out of memory?

So the key is to stream it line by line and never hold the whole file in memory. Files.readAllLines or readString would load all 10 GB onto the heap and die with OutOfMemoryError.

Map<String, Long> errorsByService;
try (Stream<String> lines = Files.lines(Path.of("app.log"), StandardCharsets.UTF_8)) {
    errorsByService = lines
            .filter(l -> l.contains(" ERROR "))
            .map(l -> l.split(" ")[2])                 // service name column
            .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(s -> s, Collectors.counting()));
}

Files.lines is lazy. It reads through a buffered reader as the stream pulls, so memory stays flat no matter how big the file is. It holds an open file handle, though, so it has to be in try-with-resources. That's one of the few streams you must close.

If I need more speed: a BufferedReader with a bigger buffer, a parallel stream (splitting works well for files on Java 9 and later), or splitting the file into byte ranges and handing each chunk to a worker. For pure byte scanning, a memory-mapped FileChannel is the fastest option.

So in practice: stream it, close it, and only aggregate the small result.

AdvancedWhy do people say you should avoid Java serialization? What would you use instead?

So there are three reasons: security, fragility, and the fact that it quietly breaks your class's invariants. Even the Java architects have called it a mistake, and I'd never use it for data coming from outside the process.

  • Security: readObject can build any serializable class on the classpath, and it runs their readObject methods along the way. Attackers chain those calls, called "gadget chains", into remote code execution. The 2015 Apache Commons Collections exploit hit WebLogic, Jenkins and JBoss exactly like this.
  • Hidden constructor: deserialization builds objects without calling your constructor, so validation is skipped, and it's how singletons and immutable objects get broken.
  • Fragility: the format is tied to class structure and serialVersionUID, it's Java-only, verbose and slow.
// if you are stuck with it, allow-list what may be deserialized (Java 9+, JEP 290)
ObjectInputFilter filter = ObjectInputFilter.Config.createFilter(
        "com.myapp.dto.*;java.base/*;!*");
