in.setObjectInputFilter(filter);

Instead I use JSON with Jackson for APIs and config, Protobuf or Avro for high-volume messages between services, because they have explicit schemas and versioning rules, and records as the DTOs being mapped. For caches like Redis I use JSON or a Kryo/Protobuf serializer, not the JDK default.

So in practice: an explicit data format with a schema, and the domain classes never implement Serializable just because some library wanted it.

Interviewers look for: deserialization of untrusted data as a remote code execution risk, and input filters as the mitigation.

41. Design Patterns in Java

BasicHow do you implement a Singleton in Java? Walk me through the variants.

So there are five common versions, and they differ in laziness and thread safety. My default is either the holder idiom or an enum.

VariantLazy?Thread-safe?Comment
Eager: static final INSTANCE = new X()NoYesSimplest. Fine if creation is cheap.
Lazy if (instance == null)YesNoTwo threads can both create one.
synchronized getInstance()YesYesTakes a lock on every call.
Double-checked locking + volatileYesYesCorrect only with volatile.
Holder class idiomYesYesJVM class init does the locking.
enum { INSTANCE }NoYesAlso safe against reflection and serialization.
 
final class Config {
    private Config() { }
    private static final class Holder {
        static final Config INSTANCE = new Config();   // runs on first Holder access
    }
    static Config getInstance() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
}

The holder idiom works because the JVM initialises a class lazily, the first time it's used, and class initialisation is guaranteed thread-safe. So Holder isn't loaded until getInstance() is first called, and there's no locking after that.

Double-checked locking needs volatile because instance = new X() isn't atomic. Without it, another thread can see the reference before the constructor has finished and get a half-built object.

So in practice, in a Spring app I rarely write one at all. Beans are singletons by default and they're easier to test than a static getInstance().

Interviewers look for: why volatile is needed in double-checked locking, and the testability downside of singletons.

BasicWhy use the Builder pattern instead of telescoping constructors?

So telescoping constructors means a chain of overloads, each adding one more parameter. Once you have more than three or four optional parameters it becomes unreadable and easy to get wrong. A Builder gives you named, chainable setters and one place to validate before the object is created.

// telescoping: which boolean is which?
new Pizza("large", "stuffed", true, false, true);

// builder: reads like a sentence, the object itself stays immutable
Pizza p = Pizza.builder("large")      // required params go in the builder factory
        .crust("stuffed")
        .extraCheese()
        .topping("olive")
        .build();                     // validation happens here

The telescoping problems are: callers pass arguments in the wrong order and the compiler can't catch it when two params share a type, you need an overload for every combination, and reading true, false, true tells you nothing. The JavaBeans alternative, a no-arg constructor plus setters, fixes readability but leaves the object mutable and possibly half-configured.

The builder keeps the target immutable with a private constructor and final fields, puts required values in the builder's constructor, and checks cross-field rules in build(). You can see the full version in the coding tasks below.

So in practice I reach for a builder at around four or more parameters, often with Lombok's @Builder. For a small record, a static factory like Money.of(5, USD) is enough.

IntermediateHow can a Singleton be broken, and why does an enum fix it?

So a normal singleton can be broken three ways: reflection can call the private constructor, deserialization creates a new object without the constructor, and clone() copies it if the class is cloneable. An enum is protected against all three by the JVM itself.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Constructor<Lazy> c = Lazy.class.getDeclaredConstructor();
c.setAccessible(true);
c.newInstance() != Lazy.get();          // true   reflection made a second one
roundTrip(Lazy.get()) != Lazy.get();    // true   serialize + deserialize made another
Lazy.get().clone() != Lazy.get();       // true   clone made another

// the manual defences
private Guarded() {
    if (INSTANCE != null) throw new IllegalStateException("already created");
}
private Object readResolve() { return INSTANCE; }   // swap the copy for the real one
// and don't implement Cloneable (or make clone() throw)

enum IdGenerator { INSTANCE; ... }
roundTrip(IdGenerator.INSTANCE) == IdGenerator.INSTANCE;   // true
// reflection: IllegalArgumentException: Cannot reflectively create enum objects

Walking through it: the constructor guard stops reflection, readResolve makes deserialization return the existing instance, and not supporting clone closes the last hole. That's three separate fixes you have to remember.

With an enum, the JVM refuses to create enum instances through reflection, enum serialization writes only the name and looks it up with valueOf on read, and enums can't be cloned. That's why Joshua Bloch calls a single-element enum the best way to implement a singleton.

So in practice I use the enum when the singleton doesn't need to extend a class or be lazy.

Interviewers look for: all three attacks, readResolve by name, and the enum as the answer.

IntermediateFactory Method vs Abstract Factory: what's the difference?

So a Factory Method creates one product and hides which concrete class you get. An Abstract Factory creates a family of related products that have to match each other, and you swap the whole family by swapping the factory.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// Factory method: one product, chosen by input
static Notifier notifierFor(String channel) {
    return switch (channel) {
        case "email" -> new EmailNotifier();
        case "sms" -> new SmsNotifier();
        default -> throw new IllegalArgumentException("unknown channel " + channel);
    };
}
notifierFor("sms").send("hi");     // sms: hi

// Abstract factory: a family that must stay consistent
interface CloudFactory {
    BlobStore blobStore();
    MessageQueue queue();
    SecretStore secrets();
}
class AwsFactory implements CloudFactory { ... }    // S3, SQS, Secrets Manager
class GcpFactory implements CloudFactory { ... }    // GCS, Pub/Sub, Secret Manager

With the factory method, the caller asks for "a notifier" and gets the right one. With the abstract factory, the point is consistency. If the app is wired with AwsFactory, every component it hands out is an AWS one, so I can't accidentally pair S3 with Pub/Sub.

In the JDK, List.of, Calendar.getInstance() and NumberFormat.getInstance() are factory methods. DocumentBuilderFactory in the XML API is closer to an abstract factory, because the implementation it picks decides a whole set of parser classes.

So in practice, a static factory method covers most cases. In Spring, the DI container plus @Profile or @ConditionalOnProperty often plays the abstract factory role for me.

IntermediateHow do lambdas change the Strategy pattern?

So Strategy means passing in the algorithm instead of hard-coding it. Before Java 8 that meant an interface plus one class per strategy. Now any functional interface is a strategy type, and a lambda or method reference is the implementation, so most of those small classes disappear.

enum Pricing implements DoubleUnaryOperator {
    REGULAR(p -> p), SALE(p -> p * 0.8), CLEARANCE(p -> Math.max(0, p - 50));
    private final DoubleUnaryOperator f;
    Pricing(DoubleUnaryOperator f) { this.f = f; }
    public double applyAsDouble(double p) { return f.applyAsDouble(p); }
}

final class Checkout {
    private final DoubleUnaryOperator pricing;
    Checkout(DoubleUnaryOperator pricing) { this.pricing = pricing; }
    double total(List<Double> prices) {
        return prices.stream().mapToDouble(pricing::applyAsDouble).sum();
    }
}

new Checkout(Pricing.SALE).total(List.of(100.0, 40.0));   // 112.0
new Checkout(p -> p * 0.5).total(List.of(100.0, 40.0));   // 70.0, ad-hoc strategy

What I like here: the named strategies live in an enum, so they're discoverable and can be picked from config with Pricing.valueOf("SALE"). Checkout still accepts any lambda, which makes tests trivial. And the JDK does this everywhere: Comparator passed to sort is the textbook Strategy.

When a strategy needs state, several methods, or its own dependencies, I go back to a proper interface with classes, often as Spring beans collected into a Map<String, PricingStrategy>.

So in practice: lambdas for one-method strategies, classes when it's more than a function.

Interviewers look for: Comparator as a JDK example, and knowing when a lambda isn't enough.

IntermediateImplement the Observer pattern in Java. What pitfalls do you watch for?

So Observer means a subject keeps a list of listeners and notifies them when something happens, without knowing what they do. In modern Java I just store Consumers and return an unsubscribe handle.

final class OrderEvents {
    private final List<Consumer<String>> listeners = new CopyOnWriteArrayList<>();

    Runnable subscribe(Consumer<String> l) {
        listeners.add(l);
        return () -> listeners.remove(l);        // caller keeps this to unsubscribe
    }
    void publish(String orderId) { listeners.forEach(l -> l.accept(orderId)); }
}

Runnable unsubscribe = events.subscribe(id -> System.out.println("email for " + id));
events.subscribe(id -> System.out.println("analytics for " + id));
events.publish("A1");     // email for A1, analytics for A1
unsubscribe.run();
events.publish("A2");     // analytics for A2

The pitfalls I watch for:

  • Memory leaks: the subject holds strong references, so a listener that never unsubscribes lives forever, along with everything it captured. Returning an unsubscribe handle makes cleanup easy.
  • Concurrent modification: a listener that unsubscribes during publish would break an ArrayList iteration. CopyOnWriteArrayList handles that, and it's cheap because reads far outnumber writes.
  • One bad listener: an exception in one stops the rest, so in production I catch and log per listener.
  • Slow listeners: notification is synchronous, so a slow one blocks the publisher. Then I'd use an executor or a message queue.

So in practice, inside a Spring app I use ApplicationEventPublisher and @EventListener, and between services I use Kafka. java.util.Observable has been deprecated since Java 9.

AdvancedWhere is the Decorator pattern used in the JDK?

So java.io is the classic example. Each stream wraps another stream of the same type and adds one behaviour: buffering, compression, character decoding. You stack them at runtime instead of having a subclass for every combination.

Let me show you with a quick example.

try (Writer w = new BufferedWriter(              // adds buffering
        new OutputStreamWriter(                  // adds chars -> bytes (UTF-8)
            new GZIPOutputStream(                // adds compression
                new FileOutputStream(tmp)),      // the real sink
            StandardCharsets.UTF_8))) {
    w.write("hello decorator");
}

try (BufferedReader r = new BufferedReader(
        new InputStreamReader(new GZIPInputStream(new FileInputStream(tmp)), StandardCharsets.UTF_8))) {
    System.out.println(r.readLine());            // hello decorator
}

Every layer is an OutputStream or Writer and has one inside it. That's the decorator shape: same interface, wraps an instance, forwards calls and adds something. FilterInputStream is literally the base decorator class. Without it you'd need BufferedGzipFileOutputStream and every other combination as its own class.

Two things to know. Closing the outermost stream closes the whole chain, so one try-with-resources is enough. And order matters: buffering outside the GZIP stream buffers chars before compression, which is what you want.

Other JDK decorators are Collections.unmodifiableList and synchronizedMap. In Spring, @Transactional and @Cacheable are decorators built at runtime as proxies.

So in practice: decorator vs inheritance is composition over inheritance. You add behaviour per instance, at runtime, in any combination.

Interviewers look for: the "is-a and has-a the same type" structure, and why it beats a subclass for every combination.

42. SOLID in Java

BasicWhat does SOLID stand for? Give me a one-line example for each.

So SOLID is five design principles from Robert Martin. Together they're about making code easy to change without breaking unrelated parts.

PrincipleMeaningJava example
Single ResponsibilityOne reason to changeInvoiceCalculator doesn't also send the email or write the PDF
Open/ClosedExtend without editing existing codeNew discount = new DiscountPolicy class, not another if
Liskov SubstitutionSubtypes must honour the parent's contractA Square extends Rectangle that breaks setWidth violates it
Interface SegregationSmall, focused interfacesReadable and Writable instead of one fat Repository with 30 methods
Dependency InversionDepend on abstractions, not concretionsOrderService takes an OrderRepository interface in its constructor

They support each other. Small interfaces (I) are easy to depend on (D), and depending on interfaces is what lets you add new implementations without editing callers (O).

So in practice I don't apply them mechanically. For a 50-line script they're overkill. They pay off in code that many people change over years.

IntermediateExplain Single Responsibility with a real example. What does "one reason to change" mean?

So SRP says a class should have one reason to change, and a "reason" really means one stakeholder or one area of the business. It doesn't mean "one method". If finance and the email team can both force edits to the same class, it has two responsibilities.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// before: three reasons to change in one class
class ReportService {
    Report build(Month m) { ... }             // finance rules change
    byte[] toPdf(Report r) { ... }            // layout changes
    void email(byte[] pdf, String to) { ... } // SMTP provider changes
}

// after: each class changes for one reason
class ReportBuilder   { Report build(Month m) { ... } }
class PdfRenderer     { byte[] render(Report r) { ... } }
class ReportMailer    { void send(byte[] pdf, String to) { ... } }
class MonthlyReportJob {                       // just coordinates the three
    void run(Month m) { mailer.send(renderer.render(builder.build(m)), "cfo@corp"); }
}

In my last project we had a 2,000-line OrderService. Changing the tax rules meant touching the same file as the refund flow, so merge conflicts and regressions were constant. Splitting it by responsibility (pricing, payment, fulfilment) meant each team owned a class and tests got much smaller, because I could test pricing without mocking an SMTP server.

The trade-off is going too far: fifty tiny classes that each do one line make the flow hard to follow.

So in practice I split when I see a class that needs mocks for unrelated things in its tests, or when its name needs "And" or "Manager".

Interviewers look for: "reason to change = actor or stakeholder", not "a class does one thing".

IntermediateHow do you apply Open/Closed and Dependency Inversion in Java code?

So Open/Closed means I can add behaviour by adding code, not by editing code that already works. Dependency Inversion is what makes that possible: high-level logic depends on an interface, and the concrete details plug in from outside.

interface DiscountPolicy { double apply(double amount); }
record NoDiscount() implements DiscountPolicy { public double apply(double a) { return a; } }
record PercentOff(double pct) implements DiscountPolicy {
    public double apply(double a) { return a * (1 - pct / 100); }
}

interface OrderRepository { void save(String order); }

final class OrderService {
    private final OrderRepository repo;          // abstraction
    private final DiscountPolicy discount;       // abstraction
    OrderService(OrderRepository repo, DiscountPolicy discount) {
        this.repo = repo; this.discount = discount;
    }
    double place(String id, double amount) {
        double total = discount.apply(amount);
        repo.save(id);
        return total;
    }
}

new OrderService(new InMemoryRepo(), new PercentOff(10)).place("A1", 200);   // 180.0

Adding a "buy one get one" discount is a new class. OrderService doesn't change and doesn't need re-testing. It also doesn't know whether the repository is JPA, Mongo or an in-memory fake, which is why I can unit test it with no database.

One nuance with Java 17+: a sealed interface with an exhaustive switch deliberately goes the other way. Adding a case forces edits everywhere. I choose that when the set of variants is part of the domain and I want the compiler to find every place to update.

So in practice: interfaces at the boundaries (repositories, gateways, policies), constructor injection, and Spring wires the concrete classes.

IntermediateWhat is the Liskov Substitution Principle? Show me a violation.

So LSP says anywhere the code expects a parent type, I should be able to pass a subtype and the code should still behave correctly. Compiling isn't enough. The subtype must keep the parent's promises: no stronger preconditions, no weaker postconditions, no surprise exceptions.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class Rectangle {
    protected int w, h;
    void setWidth(int w) { this.w = w; }
    void setHeight(int h) { this.h = h; }
    int area() { return w * h; }
}
class Square extends Rectangle {
    @Override void setWidth(int w) { this.w = w; this.h = w; }
    @Override void setHeight(int h) { this.w = h; this.h = h; }
}

static int stretch(Rectangle r) { r.setWidth(5); r.setHeight(4); return r.area(); }

stretch(new Rectangle());   // 20
stretch(new Square());      // 16  the caller's assumption is broken

Mathematically a square is a rectangle, but a mutable square isn't a mutable rectangle. The Rectangle contract says setting the height doesn't change the width, and Square breaks it. The fix is to not make them parent and child. Use immutable value types, or a common Shape interface with just area().

A JDK example people hit: List.of(...) returns a List, but add throws UnsupportedOperationException. That's a documented "optional operation", but it's still an LSP smell that bites people in production.

So in practice, whenever an override throws UnsupportedOperationException or needs an instanceof check in the caller, that's my sign the inheritance is wrong.

Interviewers look for: "behavioural contract, not just type compatibility". The square/rectangle outputs make it concrete.

IntermediateWhat is Interface Segregation? When is an interface too big?

So ISP says clients shouldn't be forced to depend on methods they don't use. If implementers keep writing empty methods or throw new UnsupportedOperationException(), the interface is too big and should be split by who uses it.

// fat interface: a simple printer has to fake scan and fax
interface Machine {
    void print(Doc d);
    void scan(Doc d);
    void fax(Doc d);
}

// segregated: each client depends only on what it needs
interface Printer { void print(Doc d); }
interface Scanner { void scan(Doc d); }
class BasicPrinter implements Printer { ... }
class OfficeMachine implements Printer, Scanner { ... }

The practical benefit I see most is in testing and in change impact. A service that only reads users should depend on a UserReader with two methods, not a UserRepository with thirty. Its fake is tiny, and adding a method to the write side doesn't recompile or re-test the readers.

Java makes this easy because a class can implement many interfaces, and a lambda can implement any one-method interface. The JDK's java.util.function package is ISP taken to the limit: Supplier, Consumer, Predicate, each with one job.

The trade-off is interface explosion, so I split along real client needs, usually read vs write, not method by method.

AdvancedWhat's the difference between Dependency Inversion, Dependency Injection and Inversion of Control?

So they're related but they sit at different levels. Dependency Inversion is a design principle: depend on abstractions, and let the abstraction be owned by the high-level module. Dependency Injection is a technique: the object receives its dependencies from outside instead of creating them. Inversion of Control is the broader idea that a framework calls your code and manages its lifecycle, instead of your code calling everything.

// no DI, no DIP: creates a concrete dependency itself
class OrderService { private final JpaOrderRepo repo = new JpaOrderRepo(); }

// DI without DIP: injected, but still a concrete class
class OrderService { OrderService(JpaOrderRepo repo) { ... } }

// DI + DIP: injected abstraction, owned by the domain package
class OrderService { OrderService(OrderRepository repo) { ... } }

The subtle point about DIP is ownership. The OrderRepository interface lives in the domain module, and the JPA implementation in the infrastructure module depends on it. The dependency arrow points from the details to the domain. That's the "inversion", and it's the basis of hexagonal or clean architecture.

Spring is IoC: the container creates beans and calls your @PostConstruct and your controllers. It does DI through constructor injection. But Spring can't give you DIP. If you inject concrete classes everywhere you get DI without the design benefit.

So in practice: constructor injection, which makes dependencies explicit and final and is easy to test with new, and interfaces at the boundaries that the domain owns.

Interviewers look for: keeping the three terms apart, and that DI alone doesn't give you DIP.

43. Reflection & Annotations

BasicWhat is reflection, and where is it actually used?

So reflection is the API for inspecting and using classes at runtime when you don't know them at compile time. You can list fields, methods and constructors, read annotations, create instances, call methods and read or write fields, including private ones if access is allowed.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Method m = String.class.getMethod("toUpperCase");
m.invoke("reflect");                                   // "REFLECT"

Class<?> cls = Class.forName("java.util.ArrayList");   // class name as a string
Object list = cls.getDeclaredConstructor().newInstance();
list.getClass().getSimpleName();                       // "ArrayList"

I almost never write reflection in business code, but nearly every framework I use depends on it:

  • Spring: scans for @Component, calls constructors, injects @Autowired fields, and builds proxies for @Transactional.
  • Jackson: finds getters, fields and record components to map JSON.
  • Hibernate/JPA: reads @Entity and @Column, and sets private fields when loading rows.
  • JUnit: finds @Test methods and invokes them.
  • Plugins and JDBC drivers: load classes by name from config.

So in practice reflection is framework plumbing. In application code I'd reach for an interface or a lambda first, because those are type-checked by the compiler.

BasicWhat are annotations, and what do the retention policies mean?

So annotations are metadata attached to code: classes, methods, fields, parameters. They don't do anything by themselves. Some tool has to read them: the compiler, an annotation processor, or a framework at runtime. Retention says how long the annotation survives, and that decides who can see it.

RetentionKept inWho reads itExample
SOURCESource only, dropped by javacCompiler, annotation processors@Override, @SuppressWarnings, Lombok's @Getter
CLASS (default).class file, not loaded at runtimeBytecode toolsSome static-analysis annotations
RUNTIME.class file and visible by reflectionFrameworks at runtime@Test, @Entity, @Autowired, @Deprecated

The classic bug: someone writes a custom annotation, forgets @Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME), and then getAnnotation always returns null, because the default is CLASS. The code compiles fine, and the validation just silently never runs.

The other meta-annotations are @Target, which says where it can be placed, @Inherited, which makes a class-level annotation pass to subclasses, @Documented, and @Repeatable.

So in practice: if a framework needs to see it at runtime, it must be RUNTIME.

IntermediateWhat are the costs and risks of reflection?

So there are four: it's slower, it throws away compile-time safety, it breaks encapsulation, and since Java 16/17 it can simply be refused by the module system.

  • Performance: a reflective call does access and argument checks and boxing, and it's harder for the JIT to inline. Since Java 18, core reflection is built on method handles, which helps, but a hot loop of Method.invoke is still slower than a direct call. Looking up the Method each time is the expensive part, so frameworks cache it.
  • No type safety: a renamed method becomes a NoSuchMethodException at runtime instead of a compile error, and refactoring tools can't see string-based lookups.
  • Breaks encapsulation: setAccessible(true) can change private and even final fields, which breaks invariants and thread-safety guarantees.
  • Modules: JDK internals are strongly encapsulated, so setAccessible on, say, a private field of String throws InaccessibleObjectException unless you add --add-opens.

There's one more cost people forget: GraalVM native images need reflection declared ahead of time, because the compiler can't see what a string-based lookup will load. That's why Spring Boot 3 has AOT processing that generates that configuration.

So in practice I cache Method and Field objects, prefer MethodHandle or a lambda built once when performance matters, and keep reflection at the edges of the system.

Interviewers look for: the module-system restriction, not just "it's slow".

IntermediateWrite a custom annotation and the code that processes it.

So I'd define the annotation with runtime retention and a field target, then write a small validator that walks the fields with reflection and checks each annotated one. It's a mini version of Bean Validation's @NotBlank.

Let me show you with a quick example.

@Retention(RetentionPolicy.RUNTIME)
@Target(ElementType.FIELD)
@interface NotBlank {
    String message() default "must not be blank";
}

class SignupForm {
    @NotBlank(message = "email is required") String email = "";
    @NotBlank String name = "Asha";
    String nickname = "";                        // not annotated, not checked
}

static void validate(Object o) throws IllegalAccessException {
    for (Field f : o.getClass().getDeclaredFields()) {
        NotBlank rule = f.getAnnotation(NotBlank.class);
        if (rule == null) continue;
        f.setAccessible(true);
        Object v = f.get(o);
        if (v == null || v.toString().isBlank()) {
            System.out.println(f.getName() + ": " + rule.message());
        }
    }
}

validate(new SignupForm());     // email: email is required

Walking through it: annotation elements look like methods and can have defaults. When there's a single element named value, callers can skip the name. getDeclaredFields returns private fields too but not inherited ones, so a real version would walk up getSuperclass(). name passes because it's not blank, and nickname is ignored.

Element types are limited to primitives, String, Class, enums, other annotations, and arrays of those, and the values must be compile-time constants.

So in practice, for real validation I use Jakarta Bean Validation, where I'd write a custom ConstraintValidator. But this is exactly how it works underneath.

Interviewers look for: RUNTIME retention, @Target, and getDeclaredFields vs getFields.

AdvancedHow does Spring's @Transactional work, and why doesn't it work when a method calls another method in the same class?

So Spring doesn't change your class. It wraps the bean in a proxy, and other beans get the proxy injected. The proxy starts a transaction, calls your real method, then commits or rolls back. The JDK's dynamic proxy shows the mechanism.

static <T> T withTransaction(T target, Class<T> type) {
    InvocationHandler h = (proxy, method, args) -> {
        System.out.println("BEGIN");
        try {
            Object result = method.invoke(target, args);
            System.out.println("COMMIT");
            return result;
        } catch (InvocationTargetException e) {
            System.out.println("ROLLBACK");
            throw e.getCause();
        }
    };
    return (T) Proxy.newProxyInstance(type.getClassLoader(), new Class<?>[] { type }, h);
}

AccountService svc = withTransaction(new RealAccountService(), AccountService.class);
svc.transfer("A", "B", 500);
// BEGIN
// moving 500 from A to B
// COMMIT

JDK proxies only work for interfaces. For plain classes Spring uses CGLIB to generate a subclass at runtime, which is why @Transactional on a final or private method is silently ignored.

Self-invocation: if placeOrder() calls this.saveAudit(), that call goes to the real object through this, not through the proxy. So saveAudit's @Transactional(propagation = REQUIRES_NEW) never runs. I've debugged exactly this: audit rows vanished whenever the order rolled back. The fix was to move saveAudit to a separate bean so the call goes through its proxy.

By default, rollback also happens only for unchecked exceptions. A checked exception commits unless you set rollbackFor.

So in practice: annotation plus proxy, public methods only, and calls must come from outside the bean.

Interviewers look for: proxy-based AOP, the self-invocation trap, and the checked-exception rollback default.

AdvancedRuntime annotation processing vs compile-time annotation processing: how do Lombok and MapStruct differ from Spring?

So Spring reads RUNTIME annotations with reflection while the app is starting. Lombok, MapStruct, Dagger and Immutables run as annotation processors inside javac, and they do their work before the code ever runs.

Runtime (Spring, Hibernate, Jackson)Compile time (MapStruct, Dagger, Lombok)
WhenAt startup and on each callDuring javac
OutputProxies, reflective callsGenerated .java source (Lombok edits the AST)
Errors foundAt startup or in productionAs compile errors
Startup costClasspath scanning, reflectionNone, it's plain code
DebuggingStack traces through proxiesYou can read the generated code

MapStruct is a nice example. You write an interface with @Mapper, and the processor generates a plain class full of getter and setter calls. If a target field has no source, you get a compile warning instead of a silent null at runtime. Lombok is the odd one out: it uses internal javac APIs to change the class being compiled rather than generating new files, which is why it tends to break on new JDK releases.

This is also the direction the ecosystem is moving. Micronaut and Quarkus do DI at compile time, and Spring Boot 3 AOT pre-computes bean definitions, for faster startup and GraalVM native images.

So in practice I like compile-time generation for mapping and boilerplate, and I accept runtime reflection for the DI container.

44. Build Tools & Testing: Maven, Gradle, JUnit

BasicMaven vs Gradle: what's the difference, and which would you pick?

So both resolve dependencies from Maven Central, compile, test and package. The difference is the model. Maven is declarative XML with a fixed lifecycle and convention over configuration. Gradle is a programmable build in Kotlin or Groovy with a task graph, incremental builds and a build cache.

MavenGradle
Configpom.xml, declarativebuild.gradle.kts, code
Build modelFixed phases, plugins bound to phasesA graph of tasks with inputs and outputs
SpeedRebuilds a lotIncremental, build cache, daemon. Much faster on big multi-module builds.
FlexibilityCustom logic means writing a pluginCustom logic inline, which is also easy to abuse
Where it's commonMost enterprise Spring backendsAndroid, large monorepos, Kotlin projects

My experience: Maven builds are boring in a good way. Any Java developer can read a pom, and two projects look the same. On a 60-module repo we moved to Gradle and CI went from about 25 minutes to about 8, mostly because of the build cache and only rebuilding modules that changed. The cost was that some build scripts turned into real code that needed its own review.

So in practice: Maven for typical services and teams that value predictability, Gradle for big multi-module builds or when build speed is the bottleneck. And always commit the wrapper (mvnw or gradlew) so everyone uses the same version.

BasicExplain Maven dependency scopes and their Gradle equivalents.

So a scope decides which classpaths a dependency lands on (compile, test, runtime) and whether it's passed on to projects that depend on yours.

Maven scopeCompileTestRuntime / packagedGradleExample
compile (default)YesYesYes, transitiveapi / implementationGuava, Jackson
providedYesYesNo, the container supplies itcompileOnlyServlet API on Tomcat, Lombok
runtimeNoYesYesruntimeOnlyJDBC driver
testTest code onlyYesNotestImplementationJUnit, Mockito, Testcontainers
importOnly in dependencyManagement, pulls in a BOM's versionsspring-boot-dependencies

The JDBC driver is the example I like. My code only talks to java.sql interfaces, so the Postgres driver should be runtime. Then nobody can accidentally import a Postgres-specific class.

Gradle splits Maven's compile in two. implementation dependencies aren't exposed to consumers' compile classpath, and api ones are. That gives cleaner boundaries and faster incremental builds. There's also system, which points at a local jar path, but it's deprecated and I'd never use it.

So in practice: the narrowest scope that works, and test libraries never leaking into the production jar.

Interviewers look for: provided vs runtime with a real example of each.

IntermediateWhat happens when you run mvn clean install?

So that runs two things: the clean lifecycle, which deletes target/, and then the default lifecycle up to and including install. Maven runs every phase before the one you name, in order.

validate -> compile -> test -> package -> verify -> install -> deploy
              |          |         |          |          |
         compiler   surefire   jar/war   failsafe   copies to ~/.m2/repository
                  (unit tests)       (integration tests)

Walking through the key ones: compile compiles src/main/java, test runs unit tests with Surefire (the build fails if any test fails), package builds the jar, verify runs integration tests with Failsafe plus checks like JaCoCo coverage, and install copies the jar into my local ~/.m2 so other local projects can depend on it. deploy would push it to Nexus or Artifactory.

Things I use a lot: -DskipTests skips running tests but still compiles them, while -Dmaven.test.skip=true doesn't even compile them. -pl module -am builds one module plus what it needs. -o works offline.

And a small opinion: in CI I run mvn verify, not install. Installing into the local repository is only useful when another local build needs the artifact, and clean is often unnecessary and just slows things down.

IntermediateWhat changed from JUnit 4 to JUnit 5?

So JUnit 5 is a rewrite. It's split into the Platform (the launcher that IDEs and build tools talk to), Jupiter (the new API), and Vintage (runs old JUnit 4 tests). For daily work, the new annotations, the extension model and parameterized tests are the main differences.

JUnit 4JUnit 5 (Jupiter)
@Before / @After@BeforeEach / @AfterEach
@BeforeClass / @AfterClass@BeforeAll / @AfterAll
@Ignore@Disabled
@RunWith, @Rule@ExtendWith, one extension model
@Test(expected = X.class)assertThrows(X.class, () -> ...) returns the exception
Public class and methods requiredPackage-private is fine
Parameterized runner, clunky@ParameterizedTest with @CsvSource, @MethodSource, and so on
@DisplayName, @Nested, @Tag, assertAll, assertTimeout

The biggest practical win is extensions. In JUnit 4 you could have only one @RunWith, so combining the Spring runner with the Mockito runner was painful. In JUnit 5, @ExtendWith({SpringExtension.class, MockitoExtension.class}) just works.

assertThrows is also better than expected. It pins the exception to one line and lets me assert on the message, instead of passing if any line throws.

So in practice: JUnit 5 for all new code, with Vintage only as a bridge while migrating.

IntermediateHow do you use Mockito? Show me a test with a mock.

So Mockito creates fake implementations of a class's dependencies so I can test the class alone. I stub what the fakes return with when(...).thenReturn(...), and verify the interactions that matter with verify(...).

Let me show you with a quick example.

class OrderServiceTest {
    PaymentGateway gateway;
    OrderRepository repo;
    OrderService service;

    @BeforeEach
    void setUp() {
        gateway = mock(PaymentGateway.class);
        repo = mock(OrderRepository.class);
        service = new OrderService(gateway, repo);
    }

    @Test
    @DisplayName("successful charge saves PAID")
    void paidWhenChargeSucceeds() {
        when(gateway.charge(eq("c1"), anyLong())).thenReturn(true);

        String status = service.checkout("o1", "c1", 500);

        assertEquals("PAID", status);
        verify(repo).save("o1", "PAID");
    }

    @Test
    void rejectsNonPositiveAmount() {
        var ex = assertThrows(IllegalArgumentException.class,
                () -> service.checkout("o1", "c1", 0));
        assertEquals("amount must be positive", ex.getMessage());
        verifyNoInteractions(gateway, repo);
    }

    @ParameterizedTest
    @CsvSource({"true, PAID", "false, FAILED"})
    void statusFollowsGateway(boolean charged, String expected) {
        when(gateway.charge(anyString(), anyLong())).thenReturn(charged);
        assertEquals(expected, service.checkout("o2", "c2", 100));
    }
}

Walking through it: an unstubbed mock returns defaults (false, 0, null, empty collections). If one argument uses a matcher, all of them must, which is why I wrote eq("c1"). And I only verify side effects, like the save. Verifying every call makes tests break on harmless refactors.

A mock fakes everything, while a spy wraps a real object and only fakes what you stub. With @ExtendWith(MockitoExtension.class) I can use @Mock and @InjectMocks, and strict stubs flag stubbing I never used. Mockito 5 can also mock final classes and static methods.

So in practice: mock the things I don't own or that are slow (HTTP clients, gateways, clocks), and use real objects for simple values and collaborators.

Interviewers look for: stub vs verify, the matcher rule, and not over-mocking.

IntermediateUnit tests vs integration tests: what's the difference, and how do you balance them?

So a unit test checks one class or small unit in isolation, in memory, in milliseconds, with its dependencies faked. An integration test checks that real pieces work together (database, HTTP, Kafka, Spring wiring), so it's slower but catches problems mocks can never catch.

UnitIntegration
ScopeOne class or methodSeveral components, real infrastructure
SpeedMillisecondsSeconds
CatchesLogic and edge casesSQL mistakes, mappings, config, serialization, transactions
ToolsJUnit, Mockito, AssertJ@SpringBootTest, @DataJpaTest, Testcontainers, WireMock
MavenSurefire, *TestFailsafe, *IT, in the verify phase

A real case: our repository unit tests were all green with a mocked repository, but the native query used a Postgres-only function and failed on H2 in one environment and on real Postgres in another. We switched those tests to Testcontainers running the same Postgres version as production, and that class of bug disappeared.

I follow the test pyramid: lots of unit tests for business rules, a solid layer of integration tests at the boundaries (repositories, HTTP clients, message consumers), and a few end-to-end tests for the critical flows. Slice tests like @WebMvcTest and @DataJpaTest are a nice middle ground because they load only part of the Spring context.

So in practice: unit tests for logic, integration tests for anything that talks to something I don't control, and real databases in containers rather than H2.

AdvancedTwo libraries need different versions of the same dependency. How do you find and fix the conflict?

So first I find it with the dependency tree, then I pin one version centrally and make sure it's compatible. The usual symptom is a runtime NoSuchMethodError or ClassNotFoundException even though everything compiled, because the jar that actually got picked is a different version from the one the code was compiled against.

mvn dependency:tree -Dverbose -Dincludes=com.fasterxml.jackson.core
./gradlew dependencyInsight --dependency jackson-databind

<dependencyManagement>
  <dependencies>
    <dependency>                      <!-- a BOM keeps a whole family aligned -->
      <groupId>com.fasterxml.jackson</groupId>
      <artifactId>jackson-bom</artifactId>
      <version>2.17.2</version>
      <type>pom</type>
      <scope>import</scope>
    </dependency>
  </dependencies>
</dependencyManagement>

The resolution rules differ, and it's worth knowing them. Maven uses "nearest wins": the version closest to your project in the tree is chosen, and on a tie, the first one declared. So it can quietly pick an older version. Gradle picks the highest version by default.

The fixes, from best to worst: align with a BOM (Spring Boot's does most of this for you), pin the version in dependencyManagement or Gradle constraints, use <exclusions> to drop a bad transitive dependency, and as a last resort shade or relocate the library when two truly incompatible major versions must coexist.

To stop it coming back, I add the Maven Enforcer plugin with dependencyConvergence, which fails the build when two paths bring different versions.

Interviewers look for: NoSuchMethodError at runtime as the symptom, "nearest wins", and BOMs.

Practice

Predict the Output

BasicWhat does this print? (String == vs equals) 
String a = "java";
String b = "java";
String c = new String("java");
String d = "ja" + "va";
String part = "ja";
String e = part + "va";
System.out.println(a == b);
System.out.println(a == c);
System.out.println(a == d);
System.out.println(a == e);
System.out.println(a == e.intern());
System.out.println(a.equals(c));

Output: true, false, true, false, true, true, one per line.

So a and b are both literals, and literals are interned into the string pool, so they point to the same object: true. new String always creates a new heap object, so a == c is false.

d is the interesting one. "ja" + "va" is two constants, so the compiler folds it into the literal "java" at compile time, and it's the pooled object: true. e uses a variable, part, which isn't a compile-time constant, so the concatenation happens at runtime and creates a new object: false. If part were declared final, it would be a constant and this would be true.

intern() returns the pooled copy, so true. And equals compares characters, so true. So in practice I use == only to ask "same object?", never for text.

BasicWhat does this print? (Integer cache) 
Integer a = 127, b = 127;
Integer c = 128, d = 128;
System.out.println(a == b);
System.out.println(c == d);
System.out.println(c.equals(d));
System.out.println(c == 128);

Output: true, false, true, true.

So autoboxing calls Integer.valueOf, and valueOf returns cached objects for -128 to 127. Both 127s come from the cache and are the same object, so == is true. 128 is outside the cache, so each boxing creates a new Integer and == compares two different references: false.

equals compares values, so true. And the last line is a trap in the other direction. Comparing an Integer with an int literal unboxes c, so it's a numeric comparison: true.

I've seen this as a real bug: two Long IDs compared with == passed every test, because test IDs were small, and then failed in production once IDs grew past 127. The upper bound can be raised with -XX:AutoBoxCacheMax, but the fix is simply equals, or Objects.equals when either side can be null.

BasicWhat does this print? (char + int) 
System.out.println('a' + 1);
System.out.println("" + 'a' + 1);
System.out.println('a' + 'b' + "c");
System.out.println("c" + 'a' + 'b');
System.out.println((char) ('a' + 1));

Output:

98
a1
195c
cab
b

So a char is a numeric type. In 'a' + 1 it's promoted to int: 97 plus 1 is 98, and println(int) prints the number.

+ is evaluated left to right, and once one side is a String it becomes concatenation. In "" + 'a' + 1, the string comes first, so we get "a", then "a1".

'a' + 'b' + "c" is the one people miss. The first + is between two chars, so it's arithmetic: 97 + 98 = 195. Only then does it meet "c", which gives 195c. In "c" + 'a' + 'b', the string is first, so everything is concatenation: cab.

The last line casts 98 back to a char, which is b. That's the trick I use for things like (char) ('a' + index).

BasicWhat does this print? (i = i++) 
int i = 5;
i = i++;
System.out.println(i);

int j = 5;
j = j++ + ++j;
System.out.println(j);

Output: 5 then 12.

So i++ is post-increment. It evaluates to the old value, 5, and then increments i to 6 as a side effect. Then the assignment runs last and stores the value it already evaluated, 5, back into i. The increment is overwritten, so i stays 5 forever, no matter how many times the line runs.

For j, Java evaluates strictly left to right. j++ gives 5 and makes j 6. Then ++j is pre-increment, so j becomes 7 and the expression gives 7. So 5 + 7 = 12, which is assigned to j.

Unlike C, this is fully defined in Java because of the left-to-right rule. But I'd still flag either line in a code review. Just write i++ on its own line.

BasicWhat does this print? (0.1 + 0.2 and float comparison) 
System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2);
System.out.println(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3);
System.out.println(0.1f + 0.2f);
System.out.println(0.1f + 0.2f == 0.3f);
System.out.println(0.1f == 0.1);
System.out.println(Math.abs((0.1 + 0.2) - 0.3) < 1e-9);
System.out.println(new BigDecimal("0.1").add(new BigDecimal("0.2")));

Output:

0.30000000000000004
false
0.3
true
false
true
0.3

So doubles are binary fractions, and 0.1, 0.2 and 0.3 can't be represented exactly. The rounding errors of 0.1 and 0.2 add up to a double that's one step above the nearest double to 0.3, so == is false.

In float the errors happen to round to exactly the same float as 0.3f, so that comparison is true. That's luck, not correctness. 0.1f == 0.1 is false because the float is widened to double, and the float's approximation of 0.1 is a different number from the double's.

So in practice I compare floating-point values with a tolerance, and for money I use BigDecimal built from a String, or long cents. new BigDecimal(0.1) from a double would carry the binary error along.

BasicWhat does this print? (Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1) 
int max = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
System.out.println(max + 1);
System.out.println(max + 1L);
System.out.println(Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE));
try {
    Math.addExact(max, 1);
} catch (ArithmeticException e) {
    System.out.println(e.getMessage());
}

Output:

-2147483648
2147483648
-2147483648
integer overflow

So int arithmetic wraps around silently in two's complement. 2147483647 + 1 overflows to Integer.MIN_VALUE, with no exception. With 1L, the expression is promoted to long before adding, so it's correct.

Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE) is still negative, because +2147483648 doesn't fit in an int. That one has caused real bugs with Math.abs(hashCode()) % n producing a negative bucket index. Math.floorMod is the safe version.

The *Exact methods throw ArithmeticException instead of wrapping. The classic place this matters is binary search, where (lo + hi) / 2 can overflow on huge arrays, so I write lo + (hi - lo) / 2 or (lo + hi) >>> 1.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (finally overriding return) 
static int f() {
    try {
        return 1;
    } finally {
        return 2;
    }
}
static int g() {
    int x = 1;
    try {
        return x;
    } finally {
        x = 5;
    }
}
System.out.println(f());
System.out.println(g());

Output: 2 then 1.

So in f, return 1 evaluates the value and gets ready to return, but finally always runs before the method actually exits. The finally block does its own return 2, and that replaces the pending return. The compiler even warns that "finally block does not complete normally".

g is the opposite. return x evaluates x right then, which is 1, and saves that value. The finally then changes the local variable to 5, but the saved return value is already 1, so the method returns 1. For an object it would be different: if finally mutated the object, the caller would see the change, because only the reference was saved.

A return in finally also swallows any exception thrown in the try. So in practice I never return or throw from finally. It's only for cleanup.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (static and instance initializer order with inheritance) 
class Parent {
    static { System.out.println("1. Parent static"); }
    { System.out.println("Parent instance block"); }
    Parent() { System.out.println("Parent constructor"); }
}
class Child extends Parent {
    static { System.out.println("2. Child static"); }
    { System.out.println("Child instance block"); }
    Child() { System.out.println("Child constructor"); }
}

new Child();
System.out.println("---");
new Child();

Output:

1. Parent static
2. Child static
Parent instance block
Parent constructor
Child instance block
Child constructor
---
Parent instance block
Parent constructor
Child instance block
Child constructor

So there are two stages. First, class initialisation, which happens once per class, parent before child. That's why both static blocks run first and never run again on the second new Child().

Then, for each object, the constructor chain. Child() implicitly starts with super(), so the parent part is fully built first: the parent's instance initializers and field initializers run in textual order, then the rest of the parent constructor body. Only after super() returns do the child's instance blocks run, followed by the rest of the child constructor.

A trap that follows from this: if the parent constructor calls an overridable method, the child's override runs before the child's fields are initialised, so it sees nulls and zeros. That's why I never call overridable methods from a constructor.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (overloading with null) 
static void print(Object o) { System.out.println("Object"); }
static void print(String s) { System.out.println("String"); }

print(null);
print((Object) null);
Object o = "hello";
print(o);

Output: String, Object, Object.

So null can be passed to both methods, and when several overloads match, the compiler picks the most specific one. String is a subtype of Object, so print(String) is more specific and wins.

With the cast, the static type of the argument is Object, so only print(Object) applies. And the third call is the important lesson: o holds a String at runtime, but overloading is resolved at compile time from the declared type, which is Object. Only overriding is dynamic.

If I add a third overload print(Integer), then print(null) doesn't compile: "reference to print is ambiguous", because String and Integer are unrelated and neither is more specific. The fix is to cast the null to the type I mean.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (ternary unboxing NPE) 
Map<String, Integer> stock = new HashMap<>();
boolean known = true;
Integer fromMap = stock.get("apple");
System.out.println(fromMap);
Integer count = known ? stock.get("apple") : 0;
System.out.println(count);

Output: null, then NullPointerException: Cannot invoke "java.lang.Integer.intValue()" because the return value of "java.util.Map.get(Object)" is null.

So the first line is fine: get returns null for a missing key, and we store it in an Integer.

The ternary is the trap. One branch is Integer and the other is the int literal 0. The type rules for the conditional operator say that when one operand is a primitive and the other is its wrapper, the result type is the primitive int. So Java unboxes stock.get("apple") by calling intValue() on null, which throws, even though we're assigning back into an Integer that could hold null.

The fix is to make both branches the same reference type, like Integer.valueOf(0), or better, to avoid the ternary: stock.getOrDefault("apple", 0). The helpful NPE message (Java 14+) pointing at intValue() is the clue that it was hidden unboxing.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (HashSet with a mutated key) 
static class Point {
    int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof Point p && p.x == x && p.y == y;
    }
    @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }
}

Set<Point> set = new HashSet<>();
Point p = new Point(1, 2);
set.add(p);
p.x = 99;
System.out.println(set.contains(p));
System.out.println(set.contains(new Point(1, 2)));
System.out.println(set.remove(p));
System.out.println(set.size());

Output: false, false, false, 1.

So when p was added, it went into the bucket for the hash of (1, 2). Changing x changes its hashCode, but the set doesn't know, and the object stays in the old bucket.

contains(p) now computes the hash for (99, 2), looks in a different bucket, and finds nothing. contains(new Point(1, 2)) looks in the right bucket and finds our object, but equals compares (1, 2) with (99, 2), so it's false. The element can't be found by any key and can't be removed, yet size() is still 1. It's effectively leaked.

So in practice: fields used in hashCode and equals must not change while the object is in a hash-based collection. That's the best argument for immutable keys like String, records, or final fields.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (static method hiding vs overriding) 
class Animal {
    static String kind() { return "Animal.kind"; }
    String sound() { return "Animal.sound"; }
}
class Dog extends Animal {
    static String kind() { return "Dog.kind"; }
    @Override String sound() { return "Dog.sound"; }
}

Animal a = new Dog();
System.out.println(a.kind());
System.out.println(a.sound());
Dog d = new Dog();
System.out.println(d.kind());

Output: Animal.kind, Dog.sound, Dog.kind.

So static methods belong to the class, not the object, and they can't be overridden, only hidden. Calling a static method through a variable is legal but misleading. The compiler replaces a.kind() with Animal.kind() based on the declared type of a. The actual object is never looked at, and that's why it prints Animal.kind. It would even work if a were null.

sound() is an instance method, so it uses dynamic dispatch. The JVM looks at the runtime object, a Dog, and calls the override. d.kind() uses the declared type Dog, so it gives Dog.kind.

Putting @Override on the static kind() would fail to compile, which is a quick way to prove it's hiding. So in practice I always call static methods through the class name, and IDEs warn when you don't.

IntermediateWhat does this print? (List.remove(int) vs remove(Object)) 
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>(List.of(10, 20, 30, 1));
list.remove(1);
System.out.println(list);
list.remove(Integer.valueOf(1));
System.out.println(list);
Collection<Integer> c = list;
c.remove(10);
System.out.println(list);

Output:

[10, 30, 1]
[10, 30]
[30]

So List has two overloads: remove(int index) and remove(Object o). With the literal 1, which is an int, the compiler picks remove(int) without boxing, because overload resolution tries exact primitive matches before autoboxing. So it removes the element at index 1, which is 20, not the value 1.

To remove the value, I pass an object: Integer.valueOf(1), and the 1 at the end goes away.

The last call is a nice twist. Collection only has remove(Object), so through a Collection reference the 10 gets autoboxed and removes the value 10. Same object, same argument, different result, because overloads are picked from the static type. I've seen this bug in "remove all IDs in this list" code, so with List<Integer> I always box explicitly or use removeIf.

AdvancedWhat does this print? (exceptions thrown from catch and finally) 
static void run() {
    try {
        System.out.println("try");
        throw new RuntimeException("from try");
    } catch (RuntimeException e) {
        System.out.println("catch: " + e.getMessage());
        throw new IllegalStateException("from catch");
    } finally {
        System.out.println("finally");
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("from finally");
    }
}

try {
    run();
} catch (Exception e) {
    System.out.println("caller got: " + e.getClass().getSimpleName() + " " + e.getMessage());
    System.out.println("suppressed: " + e.getSuppressed().length);
}

Output:

try
catch: from try
finally
caller got: IllegalArgumentException from finally
suppressed: 0

So the try throws, and the catch handles it and prints. The catch then throws its own IllegalStateException, but before that can leave the method, finally runs. The finally throws IllegalArgumentException, which replaces the pending exception completely.

The caller only ever sees the finally exception. The one from catch is simply lost: it isn't attached as a cause or as a suppressed exception, which is why the suppressed count is 0. In production that means the log shows a cleanup error and hides the real failure.

That's exactly the problem try-with-resources fixes. If close() throws while an exception is already in flight, the close exception is added with addSuppressed and the original one propagates. So in practice: never throw from finally, and use try-with-resources for anything closeable.

Interviewers look for: "the finally exception masks the original", and try-with-resources' suppressed exceptions as the fix.

Coding Tasks

BasicWrite an immutable class that has a List field.

So the rules are: the class is final, fields are private and final, no setters, and the part people forget: defensive copies of mutable fields, both coming in and going out. List.copyOf handles both in one go, because it returns an unmodifiable copy.

import java.util.*;

public final class Team {
    private final String name;
    private final List<String> members;

    public Team(String name, List<String> members) {
        this.name = Objects.requireNonNull(name);
        this.members = List.copyOf(members);   // defensive copy, unmodifiable
    }

    public String name() { return name; }
    public List<String> members() { return members; }   // already unmodifiable, safe to share

    public Team withMember(String m) {                  // "modify" by returning a new object
        List<String> next = new ArrayList<>(members);
        next.add(m);
        return new Team(name, next);
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<String> source = new ArrayList<>(List.of("Asha", "Ben"));
        Team t = new Team("core", source);
        source.add("Eve");                             // does not leak into t
        System.out.println(t.members());               // [Asha, Ben]
        try {
            t.members().add("Mallory");
        } catch (UnsupportedOperationException e) {
            System.out.println("cannot modify");
        }
        Team t2 = t.withMember("Chen");
        System.out.println(t.members() + " " + t2.members());   // [Asha, Ben] [Asha, Ben, Chen]
    }
}

Walking through it: if I'd stored the caller's list directly, source.add("Eve") would have changed my "immutable" team. If I'd returned a mutable list, callers could change it through the getter. List.copyOf fixes both, and it also rejects null elements. Collections.unmodifiableList(members) alone would not be enough, because it's a view, and the original list could still change underneath it.

The class is final so a subclass can't add mutable state or override getters. The elements here are Strings, which are immutable. If they were mutable objects, I'd need a deep copy too.

With Java 16+, I'd usually write this as record Team(String name, List<String> members) with members = List.copyOf(members) in the compact constructor.

BasicWrite a custom checked exception and show exception chaining.

So a checked exception extends Exception (not RuntimeException), and the important part is chaining: when I translate a low-level exception into my own, I pass the original as the cause so the stack trace keeps the root problem.

import java.io.*;
import java.nio.file.*;

public class ConfigLoader {
    static class ConfigLoadException extends Exception {
        private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;
        private final String path;

        ConfigLoadException(String path, Throwable cause) {
            super("Could not load config from " + path, cause);   // chain the cause
            this.path = path;
        }

        String path() { return path; }
    }

    static String loadConfig(String path) throws ConfigLoadException {
        try {
            return Files.readString(Path.of(path));
        } catch (IOException e) {
            throw new ConfigLoadException(path, e);   // translate, don't swallow
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        try {
            loadConfig("/no/such/app.yml");
        } catch (ConfigLoadException e) {
            System.out.println(e.getMessage());
            System.out.println("cause: " + e.getCause().getClass().getSimpleName());
            System.out.println("path: " + e.path());
        }
    }
}
// Could not load config from /no/such/app.yml
// cause: NoSuchFileException
// path: /no/such/app.yml

Walking through it: callers now deal with one meaningful exception, "config couldn't load", instead of an I/O detail. But getCause() still has the NoSuchFileException, and a printed stack trace shows both with a "Caused by:" section. I also carry the path as a field so the handler doesn't have to parse the message.

The mistakes I look for in code review: throw new ConfigLoadException(e.getMessage()), which loses the original stack trace; catching and only logging, which swallows it; and logging and rethrowing, which logs the same error twice.

So in practice: I use checked exceptions when the caller can reasonably recover (use defaults, retry, ask the user), and an unchecked exception extending RuntimeException for programming errors. In Spring services most custom exceptions end up unchecked.

BasicImplement the Builder pattern.

So the target class gets a private constructor that takes the builder, and final fields. A static nested Builder holds the required values in its constructor and the optional ones with defaults. Each setter returns this for chaining, and build() validates and creates the object.

import java.util.*;

public final class Pizza {
    private final String size;           // required
    private final String crust;          // optional
    private final boolean extraCheese;   // optional
    private final List<String> toppings; // optional

    private Pizza(Builder b) {
        this.size = b.size;
        this.crust = b.crust;
        this.extraCheese = b.extraCheese;
        this.toppings = List.copyOf(b.toppings);   // builder can't change it later
    }

    public static Builder builder(String size) { return new Builder(size); }

    @Override public String toString() {
        return "Pizza[" + size + ", " + crust + ", cheese=" + extraCheese + ", " + toppings + "]";
    }

    public static final class Builder {
        private final String size;
        private String crust = "thin";               // default
        private boolean extraCheese;
        private final List<String> toppings = new ArrayList<>();

        private Builder(String size) { this.size = Objects.requireNonNull(size); }

        public Builder crust(String crust) { this.crust = crust; return this; }
        public Builder extraCheese() { this.extraCheese = true; return this; }
        public Builder topping(String t) { this.toppings.add(t); return this; }

        public Pizza build() {
            if (toppings.size() > 5) throw new IllegalStateException("max 5 toppings");
            return new Pizza(this);
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        Pizza p = Pizza.builder("large")
                .crust("stuffed")
                .extraCheese()
                .topping("olive")
                .topping("basil")
                .build();
        System.out.println(p);                          // Pizza[large, stuffed, cheese=true, [olive, basil]]
        System.out.println(Pizza.builder("small").build());   // Pizza[small, thin, cheese=false, []]
    }
}

Walking through the design choices: the required size goes in builder(size), so you can't forget it, and the compiler enforces that rather than a runtime check. Validation of cross-field rules happens once in build(), so an invalid Pizza can never exist. List.copyOf in the constructor matters: if I reused the builder after build(), adding a topping must not change the pizza already built.

So in practice I'd often generate this with Lombok's @Builder, but writing it by hand shows I understand why each piece is there.

IntermediateWrite a thread-safe, lazily initialised Singleton.

So I'd give two versions: the holder idiom, which is lazy with no locking, and the enum, which is the simplest and also safe against reflection and serialization. I'll show double-checked locking too, because it's often the follow-up.

// 1. Holder idiom: lazy, thread-safe via class initialisation, no locks
final class Config {
    private Config() { System.out.println("Config created"); }
    private static final class Holder {
        static final Config INSTANCE = new Config();
    }
    static Config getInstance() { return Holder.INSTANCE; }
}

// 2. Double-checked locking: correct only because of volatile
final class Registry {
    private static volatile Registry instance;
    private Registry() { }
    static Registry getInstance() {
        Registry r = instance;               // one volatile read on the fast path
        if (r == null) {
            synchronized (Registry.class) {
                r = instance;
                if (r == null) instance = r = new Registry();
            }
        }
        return r;
    }
}

// 3. Enum: my default when laziness is not needed
enum IdGenerator {
    INSTANCE;
    private final AtomicLong next = new AtomicLong();
    long nextId() { return next.incrementAndGet(); }
}

// test: 100 tasks on 8 threads
System.out.println("before first call");
ExecutorService pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(8);
Set<Object> seen = ConcurrentHashMap.newKeySet();
for (int i = 0; i < 100; i++)
    pool.submit(() -> { seen.add(Config.getInstance()); seen.add(Registry.getInstance()); });
pool.shutdown();
pool.awaitTermination(5, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
System.out.println("distinct instances: " + seen.size());
System.out.println(IdGenerator.INSTANCE.nextId() + " " + IdGenerator.INSTANCE.nextId());
// before first call
// Config created
// distinct instances: 2
// 1 2

"Config created" prints only after "before first call" and only once, which shows it's both lazy and created exactly once. "distinct instances: 2" means one Config and one Registry across all 100 tasks.

The holder works because the JVM loads Holder only on first access, and class initialisation is guarded by a lock the JVM manages. In double-checked locking, volatile prevents another thread seeing a non-null reference to an object whose constructor hasn't finished, and the local r avoids a second volatile read.

So in practice: enum if I can, holder if I need laziness or a class hierarchy, and double-checked locking mostly for lazy instance fields, where the holder trick doesn't apply.

IntermediateWrite a producer-consumer using a BlockingQueue.

So BlockingQueue already does all the hard parts. put blocks when the queue is full, take blocks when it's empty, and it's thread-safe. I just need a clean way to stop the consumer, and I'll use a "poison pill" value.

import java.util.concurrent.*;

public class ProducerConsumer {
    private static final int POISON = -1;

    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        BlockingQueue<Integer> queue = new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(2);   // bounded = back-pressure

        Thread producer = new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
                    queue.put(i);                    // blocks when full
                    System.out.println("produced " + i);
                }
                queue.put(POISON);                   // tell the consumer to stop
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        });

        Thread consumer = new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                int sum = 0;
                while (true) {
                    int item = queue.take();         // blocks when empty
                    if (item == POISON) break;
                    sum += item;
                }
                System.out.println("consumer done, sum = " + sum);
            } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
            }
        });

        producer.start();
        consumer.start();
        producer.join();
        consumer.join();
    }
}
// produced 1 ... produced 5 (timing varies), then: consumer done, sum = 15

Walking through it: the capacity of 2 is deliberate. A bounded queue gives back-pressure, so a fast producer gets slowed down instead of filling the heap. With an unbounded LinkedBlockingQueue, a slow consumer eventually causes an OutOfMemoryError. The poison pill goes through the same queue, so it arrives after all real items, and the consumer shuts down cleanly. With N consumers I'd put N pills.

On interruption I restore the interrupt flag rather than swallowing it, so the code that owns the thread can see it.

So in practice I'd usually use an ExecutorService, which is itself a producer-consumer queue, but this is the pattern underneath.

IntermediatePrint numbers 1 to 10 with two threads, one odd and one even, in order.

So the two threads share one counter and one lock. Each thread waits until it's its turn (odd or even), prints, increments, and notifies the other. The shared state decides whose turn it is, so start order doesn't matter.

public class OddEven {
    private static final int MAX = 10;
    private static int current = 1;
    private static final Object lock = new Object();

    static void printWhile(boolean wantOdd) {
        synchronized (lock) {
            while (current <= MAX) {
                boolean isOdd = current % 2 == 1;
                if (isOdd == wantOdd) {
                    System.out.println(Thread.currentThread().getName() + ": " + current);
                    current++;
                    lock.notifyAll();
                } else {
                    try {
                        lock.wait();
                    } catch (InterruptedException e) {
                        Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
                        return;
                    }
                }
            }
            lock.notifyAll();   // wake the other thread so it can see we are done
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        Thread odd = new Thread(() -> printWhile(true), "odd");
        Thread even = new Thread(() -> printWhile(false), "even");
        even.start();           // started first on purpose: order still comes out right
        odd.start();
        odd.join();
        even.join();
    }
}
// odd: 1
// even: 2
// odd: 3
// ...
// even: 10

Walking through it: I deliberately start the even thread first. It takes the lock, sees current is 1 (not its turn), and calls wait(), which releases the lock. The odd thread gets in, prints 1, increments, and notifies. From then on they alternate.

The loop re-checks current <= MAX after every wake-up, and the final notifyAll matters. Without it, whichever thread finishes last could leave the other one waiting forever and the program would hang on join.

So in practice there are other ways too: two Semaphores passing a permit back and forth, or a ReentrantLock with a Condition. The semaphore version is the shortest, but I like showing wait and notify because that's what this question is testing.

IntermediateImplement an LRU cache using LinkedHashMap.

So LinkedHashMap has everything built in. The third constructor argument, accessOrder = true, makes get and put move an entry to the end, and overriding removeEldestEntry lets me evict the head, the least recently used entry, whenever we go over capacity.

import java.util.*;

public class LruCache<K, V> extends LinkedHashMap<K, V> {
    private static final long serialVersionUID = 1L;
    private final int capacity;

    public LruCache(int capacity) {
        super(16, 0.75f, true);   // accessOrder = true: get() moves entry to the end
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }

    @Override
    protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<K, V> eldest) {
        return size() > capacity;  // called after every put
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        LruCache<String, Integer> cache = new LruCache<>(3);
        cache.put("a", 1);
        cache.put("b", 2);
        cache.put("c", 3);
        cache.get("a");              // a is now most recently used
        cache.put("d", 4);           // evicts b, the least recently used
        System.out.println(cache.keySet());   // [c, a, d]

        Map<String, Integer> safe = Collections.synchronizedMap(new LruCache<>(3));
    }
}

Walking through it: after inserting a, b and c the order is a, b, c. get("a") moves a to the end, giving b, c, a. Putting d makes the size 4, so removeEldestEntry returns true and the head, b, is removed. The result is c, a, d, and every operation is O(1), because it's a hash map plus a doubly linked list.

Two caveats I'd mention. It's not thread-safe, and with access order even get modifies the structure, so a read-write lock won't help. Readers need exclusive access too. Collections.synchronizedMap works but everything goes through one lock. And extending exposes all of LinkedHashMap's API, so for a library I'd use composition and expose just get and put.

So in practice, for a real service cache I'd use Caffeine, which is concurrent, has near-optimal eviction and supports expiry. In a DSA round I'd write the hash map plus doubly linked list by hand.

IntermediateUsing streams, find the top 3 highest-paid employees in each department.

So it's a groupingBy on department, and the downstream collector turns each group into a sorted, limited list of names. collectingAndThen lets me post-process each group's list, and a TreeMap gives stable department order in the output.

import java.util.*;
import java.util.stream.*;

public class TopEarners {
    record Employee(String name, String dept, double salary) { }

    public static void main(String[] args) {
        List<Employee> employees = List.of(
                new Employee("Asha", "Eng", 180),
                new Employee("Ben", "Eng", 150),
                new Employee("Chen", "Eng", 210),
                new Employee("Dev", "Eng", 120),
                new Employee("Eve", "Sales", 90),
                new Employee("Farah", "Sales", 110),
                new Employee("Gus", "HR", 70));

        Map<String, List<String>> top3 = employees.stream()
                .collect(Collectors.groupingBy(
                        Employee::dept,
                        TreeMap::new,
                        Collectors.collectingAndThen(Collectors.toList(), list -> list.stream()
                                .sorted(Comparator.comparingDouble(Employee::salary).reversed())
                                .limit(3)
                                .map(Employee::name)
                                .toList())));

        top3.forEach((dept, names) -> System.out.println(dept + " -> " + names));
    }
}
// Eng -> [Chen, Asha, Ben]
// HR -> [Gus]
// Sales -> [Farah, Eve]

Walking through it: groupingBy with three arguments takes the key function, a map factory, and a downstream collector. Each department's employees are collected to a list, then collectingAndThen sorts that list by salary descending, keeps 3, and maps to names. Dev in Eng is the fourth-highest, so he's dropped, and departments with fewer than 3 people just return what they have.

A few follow-ups I'd expect. For ties at third place, I'd add .thenComparing(Employee::name) so the result is deterministic. For huge groups, sorting each whole group is O(n log n). A size-3 min-heap per department would be O(n log 3), but for normal HR data the stream version is far more readable. And if they want only the single top earner, Collectors.maxBy is simpler, though it returns an Optional.

So in practice: I'd write this readable version first, and optimise only if profiling shows it matters.

AdvancedNow write producer-consumer without BlockingQueue, using wait and notify.

So I'd build a small bounded buffer where both methods synchronize on the buffer. put waits while it's full, take waits while it's empty, and each one wakes the other side after changing the state.

import java.util.*;

public class WaitNotifyDemo {
    static final class BoundedBuffer<T> {
        private final Deque<T> items = new ArrayDeque<>();
        private final int capacity;

        BoundedBuffer(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

        public synchronized void put(T item) throws InterruptedException {
            while (items.size() == capacity) wait();   // while, not if
            items.addLast(item);
            notifyAll();
        }

        public synchronized T take() throws InterruptedException {
            while (items.isEmpty()) wait();
            T item = items.removeFirst();
            notifyAll();
            return item;
        }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws InterruptedException {
        BoundedBuffer<Integer> buf = new BoundedBuffer<>(2);
        Thread producer = new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) buf.put(i);
                buf.put(-1);                                 // poison pill
            } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
        });
        Thread consumer = new Thread(() -> {
            try {
                List<Integer> got = new ArrayList<>();
                for (int x; (x = buf.take()) != -1; ) got.add(x);
                System.out.println("consumed " + got);       // consumed [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
            } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
        });
        producer.start(); consumer.start();
        producer.join(); consumer.join();
    }
}

Three details the interviewer is checking. First, wait and notify must be called while holding the monitor of the same object, which is why the methods are synchronized. Otherwise it's an IllegalMonitorStateException. Second, the condition is checked in a while loop, because a thread can wake up spuriously, or another thread can grab the slot first, so it has to re-check after waking.
