Third, notifyAll rather than notify: with one shared monitor, notify could wake another producer instead of a consumer, and everyone ends up waiting.

wait() releases the lock while waiting and takes it back before returning, and that's what lets the other side get in.

So in practice I'd use BlockingQueue, or a ReentrantLock with two Conditions (notFull, notEmpty) so each side wakes only the right threads.

Interviewers look for: while not if, notifyAll, and calling wait inside synchronized on the same object.

AdvancedWrite code that deadlocks, then fix it.

So the classic deadlock is two threads taking the same two locks in opposite order. A bank transfer shows it naturally: thread 1 moves money A to B, thread 2 moves B to A. Each takes its "from" lock and waits forever for the other's. The fix is a global lock ordering, or tryLock with a timeout.

import java.util.concurrent.*;
import java.util.concurrent.locks.*;

public class DeadlockDemo {
    static final class Account {
        final int id;
        int balance;
        final Lock lock = new ReentrantLock();
        Account(int id, int balance) { this.id = id; this.balance = balance; }
    }

    // BROKEN: locks in argument order, so a->b and b->a can deadlock
    static void transferBroken(Account from, Account to, int amount) {
        synchronized (from) {
            sleep(50);                       // widen the window to make it reliable
            synchronized (to) {
                from.balance -= amount;
                to.balance += amount;
            }
        }
    }

    // FIX 1: always lock the lower id first, so every thread agrees on the order
    static void transfer(Account from, Account to, int amount) {
        Account first = from.id < to.id ? from : to;
        Account second = first == from ? to : from;
        synchronized (first) {
            sleep(50);
            synchronized (second) {
                from.balance -= amount;
                to.balance += amount;
            }
        }
    }

    // FIX 2: tryLock with timeout, back off and retry
    static boolean transferTry(Account from, Account to, int amount) throws InterruptedException {
        while (true) {
            if (from.lock.tryLock(100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)) {
                try {
                    if (to.lock.tryLock(100, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)) {
                        try {
                            from.balance -= amount;
                            to.balance += amount;
                            return true;
                        } finally { to.lock.unlock(); }
                    }
                } finally { from.lock.unlock(); }
            }
            Thread.sleep(ThreadLocalRandom.current().nextInt(10));   // random back-off
        }
    }

    static void sleep(long ms) {
        try { Thread.sleep(ms); } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
    }

    public static void main(String[] args) throws Exception {
        Account a = new Account(1, 100), b = new Account(2, 100);
        Thread t1 = new Thread(() -> transferBroken(a, b, 10));
        Thread t2 = new Thread(() -> transferBroken(b, a, 10));
        t1.setDaemon(true); t2.setDaemon(true);       // so the JVM can still exit
        t1.start(); t2.start();
        t1.join(500); t2.join(500);
        System.out.println("broken version stuck: " + (t1.isAlive() && t2.isAlive()));

        Account c = new Account(3, 100), d = new Account(4, 100);
        Thread t3 = new Thread(() -> transfer(c, d, 10));
        Thread t4 = new Thread(() -> transfer(d, c, 30));
        t3.start(); t4.start(); t3.join(); t4.join();
        System.out.println("ordered locks: " + c.balance + " " + d.balance);
    }
}
// broken version stuck: true
// ordered locks: 120 80

Walking through it: a deadlock needs four conditions at once: mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no preemption, and a circular wait. Lock ordering breaks the circular wait. Both threads lock account 3 first, so one simply waits its turn. tryLock breaks hold-and-wait: if I can't get the second lock, I release the first and retry after a random back-off, so the threads don't keep colliding (livelock). In my run the tryLock version also ends with 120 and 80.

To diagnose one in production I take a thread dump with jstack <pid> or jcmd <pid> Thread.print. The JVM prints "Found one Java-level deadlock" along with the lock cycle.

So in practice: a consistent lock order, as few nested locks as possible, and timeouts on anything that can block.
